# Sierra Leone — Language Arts Primary 5 Lesson Plans, Term 3 — FULLY READ + CITATION CHECK PASSED 2026-11-03

## The book itself

- Title as printed on the cover: "Lesson plans for PRIMARY Language ARTS", with cover badges "5 CLASS" and "3 TERM", and "NOT FOR SALE" at the foot of the cover (PDF p. 1, cover image).
- Issuing authority as printed on the cover: "Ministry of Education, Science and Technology", with the coat of arms and "THE PRESIDENT'S RECOVERY PRIORITIES — Education" — same cover, foreword and Introduction as Terms 1–2.
- Year: no publication year is printed on the cover or in the foreword as read.
- PDF pages: 68. PDF p. 2 is blank. Term 3 is half the length of Terms 1–2: Lessons 121–150 (L-05-121 to L-05-150), every lesson 35 minutes and two printed pages, closing Primary 5 at 150 lessons for the year. Lesson 1 of the term (L121) starts on PDF p. 7.

## Lesson map — Term 3, Lessons 121–150, in order

- Lessons 121–125, Theme: Descriptive Writing. L121 Descriptive Composition (the word "community" is unjumbled to open the lesson; pupils read a text about Shirley, an English teacher living in Chelsea, London — what is opposite/next to/under/near her house — answer questions on it, and write a five-sentence paragraph describing their own area). L122 Literary Devices in Poetry (simile and metaphor defined against each other — a simile compares using "like" or "as", a metaphor without them — built on a single line by the Scottish poet Robert Burns, whose short poem at the end of the plan contains the two similes pupils must find; animal similes — as busy as a bee, as strong as an ox, as slow as a tortoise, as wise as an owl, as proud as a peacock, as stubborn as a mule — complete the lesson). L123 Literary Devices: Similes; L124 Literary Devices: Metaphors ("Your hair is a bird's nest" is the worked example — untidy hair); L125 Literary Devices: Metaphors and Similes (pupils first share metaphors/similes asked for at home, in local languages; "You are the sunshine of my life" is the teacher's example).
- Lessons 126–130, Theme: Structure in Writing. L126 Questioning (asking questions about a story — the story of six blind men and an elephant, introduced through describing an elephant: huge, grey, trunk, tusks); L127 Storytelling: Planning and L128 Storytelling: Telling a Story (planning with different characters, then writing and telling the pupils' own stories, growing out of the blind-men story); L129 Analogies (comparisons of the "as quiet as a mouse / as loud as thunder" kind, sorted back into simile/metaphor); L130 Indefinite Pronouns (writing about people/things without naming exactly who — "everyone" in "Is everyone here today?" is the entry word).
- Lessons 131–135, Theme: Speaking and Listening. L131 Good Listener (a Head-Shoulders-Knees listening game opens it); L132 Good Speaker (a 30-second "hot seat" talk on hobbies opens it); L133 Speech: Puns, Jokes, Analogies (a knock-knock joke is read in parts and pulled apart); L134 Identification (pupils identify their own speaking strengths — good speakers found at home are the homework link); L135 Improving Skills (improving a weakness — the teacher's model is nervousness about speaking at assembly).
- Lessons 136–140, Theme: Descriptive Writing. L136 Scene Description (describing a favourite scene from a film, poem, play or book); L137 Role Play (acting that favourite scene); L138 Narrative Composition (writing about a special event in the past — birthday, wedding, family reunion, a trip); L139–L140 Comparative Adjectives (two more lessons of comparing two or three things — animal riddles first, then a ball-throwing subject game; a direct repeat of the Term 1/Term 2 comparative work, in a descriptive-writing frame).
- Lessons 141–145, Theme: Number Names; Poetry. L141 Matching Words to Numbers (counting aloud 1–20, then the tens to 100, and matching number words to figures); L142 Morals in Passages (nonfiction, fiction, poetry) (the moral of the Six Blind Men story — different people can have different ideas — generalised across text types); L143 Writing with Lessons and L144 Writing with Lessons (Continued) (rhyming-word work first — place/face, day/may — then writing, and finishing, a poem carrying a lesson); L145 Sharing Poetry (reciting the pupils' poems well, using the good-speaker list — confident, clear, eye contact, expression; a poem about honesty is recalled).
- Lessons 146–150, Theme: Descriptive Language. A closing "messages" sequence. L146 Reading Visual Texts (reading a sign or advert for its main idea, around a community word-web: village, school, church, market, medical centre); L147 Writing Visual Texts (designing a classroom-rules poster — things we must and must not do); L148 Metaphors in Messages (the metaphor "A book is a key to the house of knowledge" is analysed, then used as the model for a metaphor in a written message); L149 Communicating Messages to Others (the week's message types are named inside the next lesson: a community sign, a classroom-rules poster, a thank-you note, a welcome speech); L150 Respond to Messages — the last lesson of Primary 5 (writing a thank-you note and a reply to a partner's note, and, if there is time, a goodbye song; the teacher's model message thanks the class for the term and wishes them luck "for the next year").

## What a pupil is actually taught, and how

Term 3 is the lightest term: its new ground is literary language (simile, metaphor, analogy, puns), the speaking/listening pair taught as skills in themselves, indefinite pronouns, number names, morals across text types, and visual/message literacy (signs, adverts, posters, notes, a welcome speech). Consolidation does much of the work — storytelling, narrative composition and comparative adjectives are all returns. The year ends not with a test but with an exchange of thank-you notes.

## Repeats and continuities

- Similes and metaphors open this term (L122–L125, L129) and are picked up again, at analysis level, in Primary 6 Term 3 (similes, metaphors, alliteration). Comparative adjectives appear in all three Primary 5 terms (T1 L21, T2 L89, T3 L139–L140). The good-speaker/good-listener pair (L131–L132) returns in Primary 6 Term 2. The Six Blind Men and the Elephant is used twice in this term (L126–L128 and again for morals at L142).

## Honest negatives — this book

- Half-length term: 30 lessons against 60 in Terms 1–2, with no explanation printed in the book.
- No new tense, no new letter form, and no reading book is introduced; the only extended text is the Shirley/London description and the retold blind-men story, both board texts.
- Number names (L141) is the only number work in the whole Primary 5 year, and it stops at 100.
- No assessment, test or revision lesson is printed, even as the year closes.

## Citation check

14 concrete page anchors were checked against the actual PDF text and 14/14 passed, including: PDF p. 4 contents ("Descriptive Composition"); PDF p. 7 "Shirley" (L121); PDF p. 9 "Robert Burns" (L122); PDF p. 17 "six blind men and an elephant" (L126); PDF p. 25 "Indefinite Pronouns" (L130); PDF p. 27 "Good Listener" (L131); PDF p. 31 "Puns, Jokes, Analogies" (L133); PDF p. 41 "Narrative Composition" (L138); PDF p. 47 "Matching Words to Numbers" (L141); PDF p. 49 "Morals in Passages" (L142); PDF p. 57 "Reading Visual Texts" (L146); PDF p. 61 "A book is a key to the house of knowledge" (L148); PDF p. 65 "Respond to Messages" (L150); PDF p. 66 "Good Bye Song" (closing activity of L150).

- Title as printed on the cover: "Lesson plans for PRIMARY Language ARTS", with cover badges "6 CLASS" and "1 TERM", and "NOT FOR SALE" at the foot of the cover (PDF p. 1, cover image).
- Issuing authority as printed on the cover: "Ministry of Education, Science and Technology", with the coat of arms and "THE PRESIDENT'S RECOVERY PRIORITIES — Education" — same cover, foreword (signed Dr. Minkailu Bah; it again promises "2,700 lesson plans") and Introduction as the Primary 5 books.
- Year: no publication year is printed on the cover or in the foreword as read.
- PDF pages: 132. PDF p. 2 is blank; PDF p. 132 is a closing "IN PARTNERSHIP WITH" logos page (partner names appear only as logos, not as extractable text). Numbering restarts for the new class: Lessons 1–60 (L-06-001 to L-06-060), every lesson 35 minutes. Most lessons are two printed pages; a few run to three (e.g. L5 Role of Characters in a Story begins on PDF p. 16, and L56 Connecting Themes in Texts on PDF p. 120).

## Lesson map — Term 1, Lessons 1–60, in order

- Lessons 1–5, Theme: Conversing with Others. One story carries the block: "Why the lion roars". L1 Oral Retelling (read the story, explain it in your own words); L2 Reading with Fluency (the same story — how the other animals felt); L3 Active Listening (listening for main ideas; the week's animals are lion, rabbit, bees, zebra, elephant); L4 Responding to a Partner (listening for nouns, verbs, adverbs and adjectives, then responding); L5 Role of Characters in a Story (writing about the lion story's characters).
- Lessons 6–10, Theme: Building Up Vocabulary (L10: "Building up Vocabulary" as printed). L6 Specific Vocabulary (describing how cassava is grown and picked — the process vocabulary); L7 Story Telling (a story of a lion and a mouse — can a mouse help a lion?); L8 Story Telling: Lesson and Characters (the story's lesson as the book states it — kindness is repaid with kindness — and writing the pupils' own story on it); L9 Performing a Story (performing those stories); L10 Causes of Child Labour (reading about Abu, a boy who works in the diamond mines).
- Lessons 11–15, Theme: Writing skills. L11 Developing Ideas into a Paragraph (causes and effects of early marriage, in sentences — poverty in the family is the book's example cause); L12 Organising Sentences into a Paragraph (the same early-marriage material, ordered); L13 Effects of Child Labour (back to Abu and the diamond mines); L14 Tenses: Participles (worked on the everyday doings of Abu and of Hawanatu, the second story character of the week); L15 Conjunctions (joining sentences on the week's two topics, child labour and early marriage).
- Lessons 16–20, Theme: Building Vocabulary. L16 Nouns: Countable and Uncountable (introduced through reading about Sierra Leone); L17 Nouns: Countable (amount words for countables — mangoes, countries, people as examples); L18 Nouns: Uncountable (water, rice, pollution, transport as examples); L19 Meanings of Words: "un" (making opposites with the prefix un-); L20 Suffixes: "able" and "ment" (after un- has been named a prefix, suffixes are introduced).
- Lessons 21–25, Theme: Creative Writing. L21 Types of Compositions (reading two different kinds of text — one describing a person, one a special moment); L22 Writing: My Personal Moment (planning a composition about a special moment — the plan is built around receiving a special gift); L23 Writing: Expressing Emotions (writing that moment, with how the pupil felt); L24 Writing: Proofreading and Editing (punctuation marks named in the air — full stop, comma, exclamation mark — then editing the pupils' own stories); L25 Reading: Others' Personal Moments (reading classmates' stories aloud and sharing positive thoughts about them).
- Lessons 26–29, Theme: Writing skills / Writing Skills. L26 Poems (a poem worked through the five senses — sight, sound, touch, smell, taste); L27–L28 Responding to Text (two lessons on a text about Kofi Annan — introduced with what the United Nations is; Annan is presented as a famous African who worked for the UN and helped fight AIDS; L28 teaches supporting a personal opinion about the text); L29 Main Characters: Role Play (an African story about a bat — bats fly, live in caves, sleep by day — acted out).
- Lessons 30–35, Theme: Writing Skills, then Structure. L30 Comparative Adjectives (comparing people and objects in the classroom); L31 Comparative Adjectives: In Our Lives (comparing family and community members — a repeat-in-new-context of the Primary 5 comparative lessons); L32 Planning a Composition: Maths in Our Everyday Lives and the following Writing a Composition: Maths in Our Everyday Lives (why we study maths — plan, then write; a numbering slip is printed in the book: both lesson headers, and the contents, give the number L-06-032, so the writing lesson — logically L33 — carries no number of its own); L34 Past tense: "Used to" (talking about life before school — crawling, drinking from a bottle); L35 Adverbs of Frequency (things done every day).
- Lessons 36–40, Theme: Comprehension and Response to Text. L36 Responding to Text: Character (adjectives for a character in a reading passage — the passage's girl is Matilda: popular, clever, kind); L37 Responding to Text: Predictions (more of the Matilda passage — checking predictions); L38 Writing in Paragraphs (Matilda again — what is special about her, in the book's telling, is that she can play pranks; connecting ideas in a paragraph); L39 Sentence Structure: Simple Sentence (unjumbling "Today we will write some sentences"); L40 Sentence Structure: Compound Sentence (building harder sentences from simple ones — the compound sentence returning from Primary 5).
- Lessons 41–45, Theme: Responding to Text. L41 How to Write About an Incident Witnessed (accident vocabulary — break, crash, hit, and their past forms broke, crashed, hit); L42 Writing About An Incident Witnessed (a paragraph about an accident the pupil saw); L43 Asking Questions and Making Statements (Listening and Speaking) (hearing whether a speaker is sure or unsure); L44 Speaking to Respond to Ideas of Others (reading and responding to an article about a famous African); L45 Story Writing (the previous lesson's article was about Nelson Mandela — pupils now write a story about a main character).
- Lessons 46–50, Theme: Reading Comprehension. L46 Reading and Comprehension (finding the most important points in a passage — the hook is the seven continents, Africa among them); L47 Using a Dictionary (how dictionaries work — the first dictionary lesson in the whole primary set read here); L48 Question Tags (with continent facts as the practice material — "There are 8 continents, aren't there?" / "No, there are 7" — a repeat of Primary 5 Term 1 L60); L49 Complete Statements; L50 Answering Questions in Complete Sentences (the practice questions are Sierra Leone facts — "What is the official language of Sierra Leone?" — "English is the official language"; "Sierra Leone is in West Africa").
- Lessons 51–55, Theme: Vocabulary Development. L51 Building Vocabulary (jobs — father's job, mother's job, and a reading text about jobs); L52 Adjectives: Colours; L53 Simple Prepositions (where things are — on the desk, in the bag, under the desk); L54 Using a Dictionary (a second dictionary lesson — alphabetical order work continues); L55 Using Adjectives and Adverbs (describing a market scene).
- Lessons 56–60, Theme: Connections to Text. A fairy-tale closing run. L56 Connecting Themes in Texts (what a fairy tale is; two tales are read — Thumbelina and Pinocchio); L57 Connecting Characters in Stories (comparing the two tales' characters); L58 Connecting Plots of Stories (how the two plots connect); L59 Connecting Stories to Real life Situation (the tales are not real — connecting them to the pupils' real lives); L60 Importance of Reading — the term's last lesson (pupils order eight given sentences on why reading matters — among them, in the book's own short phrases, reading "gives you new ideas", "improves your concentration" and "is important for education and jobs" — and are told to read everything they can find, "stories, information, road signs").

## What a pupil is actually taught, and how

Primary 6 opens at a higher level of independence: pupils retell, perform, proofread and edit their own writing, support opinions about a text, and make text-to-text and text-to-life connections. The social-issues strand is heavier than anything in Primary 5 — child labour (a named child in the diamond mines) and early marriage are read about, analysed for cause and effect, and written up in organised paragraphs. Grammar is consolidation plus participles, countable/uncountable nouns, un-/able/ment word-building, "used to", and the dictionary — taught here, in the final primary year, for the first time in this set.

## Repeats and continuities

- Question tags (P5 T1 L60 → here L48), compound sentences (P5 T1 L58–L59 → here L40), conjunctions (→ L15), comparative adjectives (P5 all terms → L30–L31) and character/setting work (→ L5, L36) are all deliberate returns. The lion-and-mouse kindness lesson (L7–L9) teaches the same moral frame as Primary 5's story work. Cassava returns as vocabulary material (P5 T1 garrie processing → here L6 growing/picking cassava).

## Honest negatives — this book

- The dictionary arrives only now (L47, L54): no Primary 5 book in this set teaches it, so a pupil following only these plans meets alphabetical reference skills in the last primary year.
- No new continuous tense, no present perfect, no future form is taught this term; the tense work is participles and "used to".
- The two famous-African texts (Kofi Annan, Nelson Mandela) are short board/article texts written for the plan; no source, author or date is printed for them in the parts read.
- The Matilda passage is presented without any author attribution in the lesson as read; this filing names only what the book names.

## Citation check

15 concrete page anchors were checked against the actual PDF text and 15/15 passed, including: PDF p. 3 foreword ("2,700 lesson plans"); PDF p. 7 "Oral Retelling" (L1); PDF p. 9 "Why the lion roars" (L2); PDF p. 27 "diamond mines" (L10); PDF p. 35 "Participles" (L14); PDF p. 39 "Sierra Leone" (L16); PDF p. 61 "Kofi Annan" (L27); PDF p. 79 "Matilda" (L36); PDF p. 95 "Nelson Mandela" (L44/L45); PDF p. 101 "Using a Dictionary" (L47); PDF p. 108 "official language of Sierra Leone" (L50); PDF p. 120 "Thumbelina and Pinocchio" (L56); PDF p. 123 "Connecting Characters in Stories" (L57); PDF p. 129 "Importance of Reading" (L60); PDF p. 130 "Reading gives you new ideas" (L60 sentences).

# Sierra Leone — Language Arts Primary 6 Lesson Plans, Term 2 — FULLY READ + CITATION CHECK PASSED 2026-11-03

## The book itself

- Title as printed on the cover: "Lesson plans for PRIMARY Language ARTS", with cover badges "6 CLASS" and "2 TERM", and "NOT FOR SALE" at the foot of the cover (PDF p. 1, cover image — verified visually for this book).
- Issuing authority as printed on the cover: "Ministry of Education, Science and Technology", with the coat of arms and "THE PRESIDENT'S RECOVERY PRIORITIES — Education" — same cover, foreword and Introduction as the other five books.
- Year: no publication year is printed on the cover or in the foreword as read.
- PDF pages: 132. PDF p. 2 is blank. Numbering continues from Term 1: Lessons 61–120 (L-06-061 to L-06-120), every lesson 35 minutes, two printed pages per lesson in the main (L75 begins on PDF p. 36, a three-page spread in the printed book).

## Lesson map — Term 2, Lessons 61–120, in order

- Lessons 61–65, Theme: Critical Thinking. L61 Transitional Words Using Modal Verbs (connecting words identified in a text about food); L62 Modals: Must, Can (things that may happen vs things that will happen for sure); L63 Structure (talking about advertisements — where they are read/heard); L64 Speculation (guessing an animal from a half-drawn picture — goat, buffalo, hippo, elephant); L65 Speculation (Continued) (speculating around good behaviour at school).
- Lessons 66–70, Theme: Speaking and Listening Experiences. L66 Qualities of a Good Speaker (the President as the model of why a speaker must be good — a repeat of Primary 5 Term 3 L132 in a new frame); L67 Qualities of a Good Listener (listening to a speech — on TV/radio, by a famous person); L68 Identifying Messages in Advertisements (who adverts are written for — pupils, mothers, farmers, tourists; adverts seen in school and community — hand washing, milk powder, mobile services); L69 Messages in Advertisements (filling the missing words in advert messages — soap, save — and planning a school notice); L70 Writing an Advertisement (writing the planned notice — for a school dance competition).
- Lessons 71–75, Theme: Vocabulary. L71 Suffixes (a word-square first — care, joy, fear, power, hope — then new words by adding endings; the -ful/-less pair, "with" and "without", is the next lesson's recall); L72 Building Vocabulary (more suffixes as a way to guess new words — fearful/fearless, hopeful/hopeless); L73 Comparative Adjectives (one adjective each for a person, a place, an object, then comparing — a further return of the Primary 5/6 comparative strand); L74 Uncountable Nouns (counting the class, then "Can you count the love you feel for your family?" — countable vs uncountable named again); L75 Using Countable and Uncountable Nouns in the Subject Position.
- Lessons 76–80, Theme: Reading Comprehension. L76 Complex Sentences (introduced through a text about a road accident — the term's first complex-sentence lesson; simple and compound sentences from earlier terms are the assumed base); L77 Writing Complex Sentences (about the pupil's daily routine); L78 Commas in Complex Sentences (punctuation inside them); L79 Complex Sentences (writing about free-time activities — two things done well, two not); L80 Titles (reading a passage and choosing the best title for it).
- Lessons 81–85, Theme: Connections and Inferences. L81 Nouns that Make no Spelling Changes in their Plural (the plural lesson — some nouns keep the same form; humans/animals on other planets is the hook); L82 Connections in Reading: Real Life (part 1 of a story, connected to the pupil's own life); L83 Connections in Reading Other Texts (a second story — its girl, Alice, is bored, and pupils connect her boredom to their own); L84 Narrative Writing (writing about a time the pupil felt as Alice did — boredom as the composition subject); L85 Idiomatic Expressions (expressions that make language "stronger, more interesting and more beautiful" — the idiom strand returning from Primary 5 Term 2).
- Lessons 86–90, Theme: Writing Letters. L86 Semi-formal Letter (we change our words for older people — register is the point); L87 Semi-formal Letter (a thank-you letter for a gift); L88 Verbs and their Noun Forms (the contents give the example "marry-marriage"; verb = doing word, noun = naming word, then converting between them); L89 Oral Composition (talking about a recent interesting event — marriage, festival, concert, sports competition — and drawing a picture of it); L90 Meaning and Types of Noun Gender (Masculine, Feminine, and Neuter) (girls stand, boys stand, then the things in the room — a repeat of Primary 5 Term 2 L73–L75 at review level).
- Lessons 91–95, Theme: Structure (L91 filed under its own discussion heading in the lesson). L91 Class Discussion on Hopes, Fears and Worries about Secondary School (the transition lesson — hoping teachers are nice, worrying about strict teachers and homework, recalled again in L92); L92 Pronoun – Verbs Agreement; L93 Subject Verb Agreement (built on the masculine/feminine/neuter nouns of L90 — man/uncle, aunt/mother, chair/table); L94 Compare and Contrast Characters (the story of the elephant and the King of the Rivers — the animals who wanted the title were a cat, a monkey, a flamingo and a crab; pupils compare them); L95 Language of Informal Letter (writing a friendly letter — the informal counterpart to L86–L87).
- Lessons 96–100, Theme: Structure. L96 Figurative Expression (a short poem about the sea — how a poet makes language beautiful); L97 Modal Auxiliary Verbs: Can and Must (school rules — what you can and cannot do); L98 Modal Auxiliary Verbs: Can and Must (Continued) (rules and laws — the modals of L61–L62 returned to and deepened); L99 Punctuation and Capital Letters (matching symbols to names: capital T, comma, exclamation mark, question mark, apostrophe, full stop); L100 Reading Comprehension: Newspapers/Magazines (explaining a news story even when you do not know all the words; newspaper contents — news, adverts, public information, government notices, letters).
- Lessons 101–105, Theme: Structures in Oral Language. L101 Topic for Discussion (a dialogue read, then opinions given — the teacher's model topic is enjoying nature); L102 Prepositions (at, in, on, under — rubbish goes "in the bin"); L103 Prepositional Phrases in Sentences (a word-square yields across, over, under, down; giving directions with verb + preposition + noun); L104 Writing Poem about the Environment (pupils' own poems on a planet problem — air pollution is the teacher's example); L105 Descriptive Writing (describing something seen every day on the way to school but never noticed).
- Lessons 106–110, Theme: Responding to School Issues. L106 Intonation (using an interesting voice about a favourite place in school); L107 Present Perfect Tense (describing activities that happened in the past but we do not know when — the only present-perfect lesson in the six Primary 5–6 books); L108 Exploring a School Issue (we all have special talents — then a problem some pupils have at school is talked through); L109 Letter of Complaint (to the school director — more play time, more books in the classroom are the example wants); L110 Collective Nouns (words for groups of people and things — counting the class opens it).
- Lessons 111–115, Theme: Structure. L111 Functions of Adverbs (words that tell more about action words — who is always late, who writes carefully); L112 Types of Adverbs (Adverbs of Frequency, etc.) (always, quickly, there as recalled examples); L113 Order of Sentences (ordering sentences, around a class survey of favourite snacks); L114 Reporting Events (describing a traditional ceremony — a wedding is the example); L115 Reading for Information (a newspaper article; what Sierra Leoneans like to read — newspapers, magazines, novels).
- Lessons 116–120, Theme: Reading Nonfiction. L116 Nonfiction Vocabulary (a text about the Earth — its problems: pollution, earthquakes, droughts); L117 Paraphrasing: Nonfiction (the same text, now named as global warming; the new words carried forward are predict, atmosphere, rise, destruction, occurs, frequent, severe; pupils find the main ideas); L118 Paraphrasing: Nonfiction (causes — burning oil, gas and coal, destruction of forests; effects — food shortages, frequent severe flooding; pupils rewrite the ideas in their own words); L119 Reflective Pronouns (title as printed — "Reflective" — for the myself/yourself family: "Today I made my own breakfast… What did you do today without the help of anyone?"); L120 Possessive Pronouns — the term's last lesson (whose things are these — yours, his, theirs, ours — practised in pairs over objects on the desk).

## What a pupil is actually taught, and how

Term 2 of Primary 6 is the most varied term in the set: critical thinking with modals and speculation, media literacy (advert messages and audiences), complex sentences with their commas, paraphrasing a factual text about global warming, collective and unchanged-plural nouns, the present perfect (once), a complaint letter, and a frank class discussion of hopes and fears about secondary school. The pedagogy is unchanged — board text, pair work, exercise-book writing — but several lessons (L91, L108, L109) put the pupil's own school life, rather than a story, at the centre.

## Repeats and continuities

- Modals can/must are taught at L61–L62 and again, as "modal auxiliary verbs", at L97–L98. Countable/uncountable nouns repeat Primary 6 Term 1 L16–L18 (L74–L75). Noun gender repeats Primary 5 Term 2 L73–L75. Good speaker/listener repeats Primary 5 Term 3 L131–L132. Idioms repeat Primary 5 Term 2 L96–L97 (as "idiomatic expressions"). Semi-formal and informal letters continue the letter strand running through every term since Primary 5 Term 1.

## Honest negatives — this book

- The present perfect gets exactly one lesson (L107) and is never returned to in Term 3.
- No reading text in this term is longer than a lesson's board passage; the global-warming text (L116–L118) is the most substantial factual text and is still a single short passage reused for three lessons.
- Despite the "Newspapers/Magazines" lesson (L100), no real newspaper or magazine is reproduced in the book as read; pupils work from board texts about newspapers.
- No direct/indirect-speech terminology, no passive, no conditional and no past perfect appear — searched for in the full text and absent (see the Primary 6 Term 3 section for the whole-run negatives).

## Citation check

15 concrete page anchors were checked against the actual PDF text and 15/15 passed, including: PDF p. 7 "Transitional Words Using Modal Verbs" (L61); PDF p. 21 "Advertisements" (L68); PDF p. 33 "Uncountable Nouns" (L74); PDF p. 38 "Complex Sentences" (L76); PDF p. 52 "Alice" (L83); PDF p. 58 "Semi-formal Letter" (L86); PDF p. 69 "secondary school" (L91); PDF p. 75 "King of the Rivers" (L94); PDF p. 88 "Newspapers/Magazines" (L100); PDF p. 103 "Present Perfect Tense" (L107); PDF p. 107 "Letter of Complaint" (L109); PDF p. 121 "global warming" (L116); PDF p. 123 "predict, atmosphere" (L117); PDF p. 127 "Reflective Pronouns" (L119); PDF p. 129 "Possessive Pronouns" (L120).

# Sierra Leone — Language Arts Primary 6 Lesson Plans, Term 3 — FULLY READ + CITATION CHECK PASSED 2026-11-03

## The book itself

- Title as printed on the cover: "Lesson plans for PRIMARY Language ARTS", with cover badges "6 CLASS" and "3 TERM", and "NOT FOR SALE" at the foot of the cover (PDF p. 1, cover image).
- Issuing authority as printed on the cover: "Ministry of Education, Science and Technology", with the coat of arms and "THE PRESIDENT'S RECOVERY PRIORITIES — Education" — same cover, foreword and Introduction as the other five books.
- Year: no publication year is printed on the cover or in the foreword as read.
- PDF pages: 84 — longer than the other Term 3 book (Primary 5 Term 3 is 68 PDF pages) because several Primary 6 Term 3 lessons run to three printed pages (e.g. L123 begins on PDF p. 11, L124 on PDF p. 14, L126 on PDF p. 18, L141 on PDF p. 52). Lessons 121–150 (L-06-121 to L-06-150), every lesson 35 minutes, closing the primary run at 150 lessons for Primary 6 and 300 lessons across Primary 5–6. Lesson 121 starts on PDF p. 7. Two numbering slips are printed in the book and are recorded, not corrected, in the map below: the conjunctions lesson after L132 is headed ": L-06-133" (with a stray colon) in its plan, and the planning-a-formal-letter lesson is headed "L-106-135" for L-06-135.

## Lesson map — Term 3, Lessons 121–150, in order

- Lessons 121–125, Theme: Responding to Broadcasts and Speeches. L121 Vocabulary (the five senses recalled — sight, smell, taste, sound, touch, and which matters most — then listening to a radio broadcast); L122 Important Messages (more of the broadcast; picking out its message and details); L123 Express Agreement or Disagreement (the broadcast was about a new clinic for eye problems — is it a good idea? hands up for yes, hands on head for no; working with different opinions about the clinic); L124 Business Letter (a radio news text about Samuel, who sells cassava and sweet potatoes and must travel far to reach a market — read in an angry voice, discussed — and a jumbled class letter about it to a trade official, which pupils must put in order); L125 Business letter (continued) (continuing and completing that letter).
- Lessons 126–128, Theme: Writing. L126 Newspaper Articles (a newspaper story about a very special dog, summarised under headings); L127 Current Events (reading and writing about new hospitals — where do you go if you are sick; do we need more hospitals?); L128 Expressing Opinions (smoking — why people smoke, why it is bad: it harms the lungs — reasons given for and against).
- Lessons 129–135, Theme: Writing, then Formal Letters. The formal-letter block: L129 Formal Letter Format (the last letter the class wrote was to a trade official — now the formal/official letter's format is taught as such); L130 Why Formal Letters? (when and why formal letters are written); L131 Formal Language (the formal word for "to ask for something" is "request"; formal vocabulary belongs in formal letters and academic essays); L132 Vocabulary: Word Families (satisfactory/unsatisfactory as the formal pair for good/bad — are they nouns, verbs, adjectives or adverbs? — then word families built on such words); L133 Conjunctions and Compound Sentences and L134 Conjunctions and Compound Sentences (continued) ("William walked to the shop slowly because he was very tired" — two ideas, one joining word; then creating sentences with so, because, before, after — a direct repeat of Primary 5 Term 1 L58–L59, now inside the formal-letters theme); L135 Planning a Formal Letter (the imagined case: the school is to close / there is no school — plan a letter asking the government to build one here).
- Lessons 136–140, Theme: Reading nonfiction. L136 Headings, Titles and Illustrations (nobody reads a newspaper cover to cover — headlines and pictures as finding tools; writing headlines); L137 How to Write Headings, Titles and Illustrations (continued) (the connection between pictures and news stories); L138 Using Questions to Clarify (a lesson about questions themselves — pupils open by questioning the teacher about today's class); L139 Charts, Diagrams and Graphs and L140 Charts, Diagrams and Graphs (continued) (why nonfiction books carry pictures, photos, diagrams, charts and graphs; reading a chart about favourite school subjects).
- Lessons 141–145, Theme: Writing with Information. A five-lesson information-writing project, the longest single writing sequence in Primary 6: L141 Writing with Information (choosing a topic for an information text — your favourite subject); L142 Writing with Information (continued) (what texts give information — nonfiction); L143 Writing with Information (continued) (ordering and organising the information so readers understand); L144 Writing with Information (continued) (writing the text — the class topic has become sweet potatoes; a good illustration must be thought of too); L145 Writing with Information (continued) (making the compositions "even better" — improving/editing for full marks).
- Lessons 146–150, Theme: Poetry — the primary run's closing block. L146 Analysis of Poetry: Poetic Language (listening to and reading two poems; describing the images poetic language creates); L147 Analysis of Poetry: Similes, Metaphors, Alliteration (identifying the devices in a poem — the Primary 5 Term 3 device work, returned to as analysis); L148 Analysis of Poetry: Rhythm (following and expressing a poem's rhythmic pattern, through rhymes); L149 Writing Poetry (using all of it — imagery, similes, metaphors, alliteration, rhyme, rhythm — in the pupils' own poems); L150 Writing and Reciting Poetry — the last lesson of primary school in this set (revising the poems written in L149 and reciting them to the class; what makes a good poem — rhyme, rhythm, imagery, metaphors, alliteration, similes, fun — and whether a poem can make you feel something).

## What a pupil is actually taught, and how

The final term is a working-writing term: listening to broadcasts and answering back in a business/formal letter, reading newspapers through their headings and pictures, reading charts and graphs, and producing a complete, organised, illustrated information text over five lessons. It ends with the fullest poetry work in the primary set — analysis (language, devices, rhythm) before composition and recitation. Formal register is the term's through-line: formal letters, formal language, word families, and a planned appeal to government for a school.

## Repeats and continuities

- Conjunctions and compound sentences repeat Primary 5 Term 1 L58–L59 almost lesson-for-lesson. Similes/metaphors repeat Primary 5 Term 3 L122–L125, with alliteration added. The trade-official letter of L124–L125 is the direct ancestor of the formal-letter block (L129–L135) that follows it. Samuel's cassava and sweet potatoes (L124) and the sweet-potato information text (L144) continue the local-food material that runs from garrie (P5 T1) and cassava (P6 T1) through the whole set.

## Honest negatives — this book, and the primary run as a whole

This is the last primary year, so the negatives below are stated for the whole run as far as these six books (Primary 5 Terms 1–3 and Primary 6 Terms 1–3, 300 lessons) show it — they are claims about these books, not about any Primary 1–4 material, which is outside this filing:

- No phonics or letter-sound teaching, no handwriting instruction and no dictation appear in any of the six books (searched in the full text of all six: zero occurrences of "phonics", "handwriting" or "dictation"). A pupil is assumed to decode already; reading work is fluency, expression and comprehension of short board texts.
- No systematic spelling teaching: no spelling rules, no word lists to learn by heart. Word-building (compound nouns, prefixes un-, suffixes -able/-ment/-ful/-less, word families) is taught as vocabulary, and the only spelling-form lesson is the unchanged-plural lesson (P6 T2 L81).
- Grammar ceilings: no past perfect, no passive voice, no conditional sentences, and no direct/indirect-speech terminology anywhere in the six books (all searched, zero occurrences). Reported speech is taught (P5 T1) without those labels. The future is never taught as a tense — "will" forms appear only inside verb-form matching (P5 T2 L103) and sentence work. The present perfect appears exactly once (P6 T2 L107); present and past continuous appear only in Primary 5 Term 2.
- No drama text: role play is frequent, but pupils only ever act stories they have read in the plan or written themselves — no play, script or dramatist is ever studied. No debate format is taught; discussion is opinion-sharing in pairs and class talk.
- No book-length reading: no novel, reader or chapter book is set in any term. Every reading text is a short passage printed at the end of a lesson plan for the teacher to write on the board, because — as the head of this filing records — there is no primary pupil book in this record at all; the pupil's only book is their own exercise book.
- Reference skills are thin and late: the dictionary is taught only in Primary 6 Term 1 (two lessons); no thesaurus, encyclopedia, library lesson or contents/index lesson appears in the six books.
- No assessment is built in: no test, examination or marked revision lesson is printed in any of the six books; the shared Introduction leaves exam preparation to the teacher's spare time.
- Language of instruction: everything is taught in and about English. Local languages appear only at the edges — Krio in a list of school subjects (P5 T1 L46), metaphors collected from home in local languages (P5 T3 L125), and a hot-seat talk allowed "in any language" (P5 T3 L132). No lesson teaches English through, or alongside, a Sierra Leonean language.
- What the run does NOT lack, for the record: letters of every register in the set's terms (informal, semi-formal, formal, business, complaint, thank-you), essay and composition forms, poetry reading and writing, newspaper and advert literacy, and a repeated social-issues strand (road vs park, teenage pregnancy, environment, child labour, early marriage, smoking, school issues) — a pupil who followed all 300 lessons would have written often, in many forms, but always at short length.

## Citation check

# Sierra Leone — Language Arts JSS1 Pupil Handbook, Term 1 — FULLY READ + CITATION CHECK PASSED 2026-11-03

## The book itself

- **Title as printed (cover):** "Pupils' handbook for JSS Language ARTS — JSS 1 Term 1", with "Free Quality School Education" programme branding and "STRICTLY NOT FOR SALE".
- **Issuing authority as printed:** Ministry of Basic and Senior Secondary Education (cover and foreword). The foreword is signed by Mr. Alpha Osman Timbo, Minister of Basic and Senior Secondary Education, and thanks the Department for International Development (DFID) for support; the back cover reads "GOVERNMENT OF SIERRA LEONE … STRICTLY NOT FOR SALE". The introduction says the activities are aligned to the Teachers' Guide, the National Curriculum and WAEC syllabus guidelines, and meet requirements of the Ministry of Education, Science and Technology (the older ministry name, as printed there).
- **Year:** No publication year, ISBN or publisher imprint is printed on the cover, foreword or contents pages. (The PDF file metadata — not printed in the book — shows a 2018 creation date and a January 2019 modification.)
- **Extent:** 126 PDF pages. Printed page numbers run PDF page − 5 (Lesson 1 starts on printed p. 2 = PDF p. 7; the Answer Key starts on printed p. 94 = PDF p. 99). Lessons 1–50, each coded PHL-07-001 to PHL-07-050, followed by an answer key and the back cover.
- **Format of every lesson:** Lesson Title, Theme, Practice Activity code, Learning Outcomes, an Overview teaching the point, then numbered practice activities to be written in the pupil's own exercise book ("DO NOT WRITE IN THE BOOKS" — printed lifespan policy, 3 years).

## Map of the term — Lessons 1–50 in order

- **L1 Oral Narrative (Introducing Myself)** — Listening/Speaking: saying your name, age, class, town, siblings and hobbies; using "I" for yourself and "he/she" for others; verbs introduced as action words.
- **L2 The Four Language Arts** — Reading: listening/speaking, reading, writing and grammar defined as the four areas of the subject.
- **L3 Fluent Reading Exercises** — Reading: fluency = accuracy, suitable speed and expression; pausing at punctuation when reading aloud.
- **L4 The Eight Parts of Speech** — Grammar: noun, pronoun, adjective, verb, adverb, preposition, conjunction, interjection, each with its job and examples.
- **L5 Nouns** — Grammar: a noun names a person, place or thing, with example sets for each.
- **L6 Reading Comprehension** — Reading/Listening/Speaking: voice modulation — changing loudness and tone to show anger, gentleness, happiness or sadness when reading aloud.
- **L7 Reading Comprehension (From a Selected Passage)** — factual questions (answer in the text) vs inferential questions (answer worked out from clues); reading text "The Important Chief".
- **L8 Reading Comprehension (From a Selected Passage)** — more work on factual vs inferential questions, with question-word examples.
- **L9 Common and Proper Nouns** — Grammar: general names in lower case vs specific names with capital letters (people, countries, days, months, towns).
- **L10 Sentence Building** — Writing: every sentence needs a subject and a verb; parts of speech recombined to build sentences.
- **L11 Meaning of Prose** — Reading: prose as everyday writing (letters, stories, newspapers) vs poetry as feeling/idea writing with rhythm; a poet defined.
- **L12 Nouns: Abstract Nouns** — Grammar: concrete nouns you can sense vs abstract nouns for emotions and ideas (sleep, anger, fear); abstract nouns take a singular verb.
- **L13 Writing Compositions: Paragraphs** — Writing: a paragraph is 3–5 sentences on one topic; plan by listing related ideas first (worked example: football).
- **L14 Composition** — Writing: one topic per paragraph; capital letter to start, end punctuation, mix of short and long sentences, capitalised proper nouns, adjectives for interest.
- **L15 Nouns: Collective Nouns** — Grammar: team (players), flock (birds), band (musicians) — one group word, singular, no final "s".
- **L16 Types of Prose** — Reading: narrative (story), explanatory (why/how — textbooks, manuals) and descriptive text types.
- **L17 Narrative Prose** — Reading: a narrative is a sequence of events, usually simple past; fiction (invented) vs non-fiction (true), with a short invented rat-king story as the fiction example.
- **L18 Singular and Plural Nouns** — Grammar: add -s; add -es after s/x/ch/sh/ss.
- **L19 Singular and Plural Nouns** — irregular plurals: -fe/-f → -ves (knife–knives, leaf–leaves); consonant + y → -ies (factory–factories).
- **L20 Pronunciation** — Listening/Speaking: five vowel letters but 20 vowel sounds; short vs long vowel sounds and diphthongs introduced.
- **L21 Types of Drama (Tragedy and Comedy)** — Reading: comedy ends happily, tragedy ends sadly; Shakespeare's Romeo and Juliet in Verona (Montagues vs Capulets) as the tragedy example.
- **L22 Characterisation** — Reading: characters, hero and heroine; the Romeo and Juliet character list and what happens to each.
- **L23 Pronouns** — Grammar: pronouns replace nouns to avoid repetition (I, he, she, you, it, we, they).
- **L24 Pronouns (Continuation)** — rules for he/she/they/it/we by who is being talked about.
- **L25 Creative Writing** — Writing: poetry vs prose aims; figures of speech introduced — metaphor compared with simile (metaphor does not use "like/as").
- **L26 Reading the First Chapter of the Prose Text** — Reading: the term's set prose text begins — Jules Verne's Around the World in 80 Days (written almost 150 years ago, no planes/cars/telephones); Parts 1–2 read.
- **L27 Reviewing the Previous Chapter of the Prose Text** — Reading: Phileas Fogg (rich, routine-bound, punctual) and his new French servant Passepartout; checking your own understanding while reading.
- **L28 Verbs** — Grammar: verbs as action/state words; every sentence must have a verb; verbs work in past, present and future.
- **L29 Verb Tenses** — Grammar: tense shows when; regular past with -ed; future with a helping (auxiliary) verb.
- **L30 The Simple Present Tense** — Grammar: regular habits and always-true facts; "do not" for negatives; add -s with he/she/it.
- **L31 The Simple Past Tense** — Grammar: completed past actions; regular -ed plus a table of common irregulars (leave–left, meet–met, do–did, see–saw, run–ran, am/is–was, are–were).
- **L32 The Simple Past Tense (Continuation)** — past time markers such as "yesterday", "last year", "three months ago", placed at the start or end of a sentence.
- **L33 Pronunciation** — Listening/Speaking: 21 consonants; consonant sounds made by stopping air with lips, tongue and teeth; two-letter blended sounds.
- **L34 Literary Terms** — Reading: simile, metaphor and personification as devices that build a picture in the reader's mind.
- **L35 Chapters 2 and 3 of Prose Text** — Reading: Around the World in 80 Days Part 3 — the Reform Club bet, departure from London on 2 October 1872 (date as printed in the book's summary), Fogg and valet Passepartout.
- **L36 Punctuation Marks** — Grammar: full stop, question mark and comma; sentences begin with a capital letter.
- **L37 Punctuation Marks (Continuation)** — apostrophe for possession, singular -'s vs plural -s'; further marks.
- **L38 Narrative Prose (Continuation)** — Reading: Around the World in 80 Days Part 4 — the return deadline at the Reform Club (8.45 pm, Sunday 21 December, as printed) and what Fogg loses if he fails.
- **L39 Poems** — Reading: Alfred Noyes' narrative poem "The Highwayman" (first verse) — repetition and rhyme creating rhythm and the picture of riding.
- **L40 Poems (Continuation)** — Reading: poems have lines, not sentences; descriptive language for the five senses; Wordsworth's "I Wandered Lonely as a Cloud" read, hunting similes, metaphors and personification.
- **L41 Countable and Uncountable Nouns** — Grammar: countable nouns take numbers and plurals; uncountable nouns stay singular with a singular verb (abstract ideas included).
- **L42 Countable and Uncountable Nouns (Continuation)** — Grammar: quantity words sorted — a/an, some, any, many, a lot, few for countable; some, any, much, a lot, a little for uncountable.
- **L43 Prose** — Reading: stories carry a moral; the ancient Greek story "How the Peacock Came to Be" (Parts 1–2) — a proud grey bird in the age of gods and goddesses (plot only).
- **L44 Creative Writing** — Writing: a five-step story plan (opening situation → change → new situation → what the character discovered → moral), one paragraph per step, in the simple past.
- **L45 Creative Writing (Continuation)** — Writing: the same five steps applied to planning and writing the pupil's own short story.
- **L46 The Present Continuous Tense** — Grammar: be (am/are/is) + verb-ing for actions in progress now; spelling changes — drop final -e, -ie → -y before -ing.
- **L47 The Present Continuous Tense (Continuation)** — negatives with "not" after be; questions by swapping be and the subject.
- **L48 Informal Letters** — Writing: letter to a friend/family — address top right, date under it, "Dear …" salutation, body, closing and name.
- **L49 Informal Letters (Continuation)** — the same features practised: choosing news for the reader, asking questions back, introduction and conclusion in the body.
- **L50 Descriptive Prose** — Reading: description that paints a picture (setting and characters); Mark Twain named as a famous descriptive writer, with an adapted passage about Huckleberry Finn, the town drunk's son, on the Mississippi.

## Grammar and vocabulary spine of this book

- **Word classes:** all eight parts of speech (L4), then nouns in depth — common/proper (L9), abstract vs concrete (L12), collective (L15), singular/plural incl. irregulars (L18–19), countable/uncountable with their quantity words (L41–42); pronouns (L23–24); verbs and tense as a system (L28–29).
- **Tenses taught outright:** simple present (L30), simple past incl. irregular table and time markers (L31–32), present continuous incl. -ing spelling rules, negatives and questions (L46–47). Past continuous, perfect and future forms are named in L29 but not yet taught as lessons.
- **Punctuation:** full stop, question mark, comma (L36); apostrophe of possession (L37).
- **Pronunciation:** vowel sounds, short/long (L20); consonant sounds and blends (L33).
- **Literature spine:** prose types and narrative (L11, L16–17); drama types and characterisation through Romeo and Juliet (L21–22); literary terms (L34); the set prose text Around the World in 80 Days in adapted parts (L26–27, L35, L38); poems — "The Highwayman" and "I Wandered Lonely as a Cloud" (L39–40); a Greek moral story (L43); descriptive prose via Twain (L50).
- **Writing spine:** paragraphs and compositions (L13–14), creative story writing in five steps (L44–45), informal letters (L48–49).
- **Vocabulary method:** new words are met inside lessons and passages and practised in sentences; there is no separate word-list section — vocabulary is carried by the reading texts (Verne, Shakespeare plot summaries, Noyes, Wordsworth, Twain) and by Sierra Leonean example names and places (Lunsar, Bo, Freetown).

## Honest negatives

- No publication year, ISBN, edition number or publisher imprint is printed anywhere in the front matter; the year can only be inferred from file metadata, which is not part of the book.
- The set "prose text" is not the full novel: Around the World in 80 Days appears as short adapted parts/summaries, and Romeo and Juliet and the Twain passage are likewise shortened school adaptations — no complete literary work is printed in this book.
- Only informal letters are taught this term; there is no formal or semi-formal letter, no direct/indirect speech, no question tags and no present perfect / past continuous lesson (those come in Terms 2–3).
- There is no standalone dictionary/glossary section and no BECE examination guidance in this term's book; answers are provided in the answer key, but there are no worked examination papers.

## Citation check

16 concrete page anchors were checked against the actual PDF text (cover, foreword, contents, lesson pages, answer key, back cover) — **16/16 passed**. Anchors include: PDF p. 1 "STRICTLY NOT FOR SALE"; p. 2 foreword signed "Alpha Osman Timbo"; p. 4 contents "Lesson 1: Oral Narrative"; p. 7 (printed 2) "I am from Lunsar"; p. 13 "The Eight Parts of Speech"; p. 23 "Common and Proper Nouns"; p. 45 "Romeo and Juliet is a famous play"; p. 54 "Around the World in 80 Days"; p. 62 "The Simple Present Tense"; p. 71 "Punctuation Marks"; p. 77 "The Highwayman"; p. 81 "Countable and Uncountable"; p. 89 "The Present Continuous Tense"; p. 93 "Informal Letters"; p. 99 "Answer Key"; p. 126 "GOVERNMENT OF SIERRA LEONE".

# Sierra Leone — Language Arts JSS1 Pupil Handbook, Term 2 — FULLY READ + CITATION CHECK PASSED 2026-11-03

## The book itself

- **Title as printed (cover):** "Pupils' handbook for JSS Language ARTS — JSS 1 Term 2", "Free Quality School Education" branding, "STRICTLY NOT FOR SALE".
- **Issuing authority as printed:** Ministry of Basic and Senior Secondary Education; the same foreword signed by Mr. Alpha Osman Timbo, Minister of Basic and Senior Secondary Education (thanking DFID); back cover "GOVERNMENT OF SIERRA LEONE … STRICTLY NOT FOR SALE". Introduction as in Term 1 (Teachers' Guide / National Curriculum / WAEC alignment).
- **Year:** No publication year, ISBN or imprint printed in the front matter. (PDF metadata only: created July 2018, modified January 2019 — not printed in the book.)
- **Extent:** 134 PDF pages. Printed page = PDF page − 5 (Lesson 51 starts on printed p. 2 = PDF p. 7; Answer Key starts on printed p. 98 = PDF p. 103). Lessons 51–100, coded PHL-07-051 to PHL-07-100, then answer key and back cover.

## Map of the term — Lessons 51–100 in order

- **L51 Possessive Pronouns** — Grammar: mine, yours, his, hers, its, ours, theirs — who things belong to, in a subject-by-subject table.
- **L52 A Drama-Based Story** — Reading: a play is literature built from dialogue; a short school play about three friends preparing a presentation, one of whom (Abubakarr) will not work (plot only).
- **L53 The Past Continuous Tense** — Grammar: was/were + verb-ing for a continuing or interrupted past action.
- **L54 The Past Continuous Tense (Continuation)** — negatives with "not", questions by inversion; positive/negative/question table.
- **L55 A Selected Poem** — Reading: poems rhyme, use lines and devices; new device hyperbole (big exaggeration) added to simile, metaphor and personification; pupils sort example lines by device.
- **L56 Composition: Narrative Essay** — Writing: a story of your own past experience, in the simple past; a model essay about a family celebration for an aunt's 75th birthday.
- **L57 Composition: Narrative Essay (Continuation)** — essay structure: introduction, body, conclusion.
- **L58 Punctuation and Intonation** — Reading: intonation (voice rising/falling) guided by punctuation — yes/no questions rise, wh- questions and exclamations fall.
- **L59 The Present Continuous Tense** — Grammar revision: be + verb-ing, now with contractions (I'm walking, you're walking…).
- **L60 The Present Continuous Tense (Continuation)** — negatives and questions revised with everyday examples.
- **L61 Reading Newspapers or Magazines** — Reading: newspaper articles are mostly true/factual, some fiction; an article about classrooms of the future, with computer/laptop/tablet vocabulary defined first.
- **L62 Reading Newspapers or Magazines (Continuation)** — magazines focus on one topic; an article about global warming, with global, habitat and related vocabulary defined first.
- **L63 The Simple Future Tense** — Grammar: will/shall + verb and "going to"; shall is formal and only with I/we.
- **L64 The Simple Future Tense (Continuation)** — contractions of will ('ll, won't); shall has no positive short form.
- **L65 Pronunciation: Consonant Sounds** — Listening/Speaking: the /b/ sound (lips pressed — boy, blue, bird) and /d/ sound (tongue behind teeth).
- **L66 Drama/Play** — Reading: acts, scenes, actors, monologue vs dialogue, audience; Hamlet introduced — Shakespeare, written over 400 years ago, in older English.
- **L67 Drama/Play (Continuation)** — Hamlet as tragedy: a plot summary — the Danish prince whose uncle murdered his father and married his mother, and Hamlet's delay over revenge (plot only).
- **L68 The Future Tense (Continuation)** — will/shall revision and contraction practice.
- **L69 Informal Letters** — Writing: features revised over a model letter from Freetown (112 Liberation Avenue, dated 12 June 2018, as printed in the example) to a friend, Bintu.
- **L70 Dictation and Spelling** — Grammar: listening and spelling practice using Hamlet vocabulary — murder, depressed, Danish, revenge defined.
- **L71 A Comprehension Passage** — Reading/Listening: silent reading for main points; context clues and inference; the story "The Millionaire's Heir" — a rich man, Edward, and his three sons Francis, Michael and Samuel (plot only).
- **L72 A Comprehension Passage (Continuation)** — recalling main ideas from reading/listening; more context-clue work.
- **L73 Possessive Nouns** — Grammar: singular possession with -'s; plural possession with the apostrophe after -s.
- **L74 Possessive Nouns (Continuation)** — the same two rules practised in family-relationship sentences.
- **L75 Pronunciation: Long and Short "u" Sounds** — Listening/Speaking: short /u/ (tub, hug, sun) vs long /u:/ (tube, huge), with a hand-movement drill.
- **L76 Guided Writing Summary** — Writing: summarising with a five-part guide — Somebody… Wanted… But… Then… So…
- **L77 Poetry** — Reading: African, English and American poetry compared; Africa's spoken "oral tradition"; African themes vs universal themes.
- **L78 Poems** — Reading: personification and rhyme studied through Segun Rasaki's poem "Africa" (quoted lines personify Africa and her scattered children).
- **L79 Adjectives** — Grammar: adjectives modify nouns and pronouns, before the noun or after "is" (a red bicycle / the bicycle is red).
- **L80 Adjectives (Continuation)** — building descriptions with strings of adjectives (a village described as big, crowded, clean, busy, beautiful…).
- **L81 Poetry** — Writing: composing a diamond poem — noun / two adjectives / three -ing verbs / four nouns, in a diamond shape.
- **L82 Adjectives: Adjectives of Quality** — Grammar: adjectives of shape, size, age, colour and taste; "how much" for uncountable vs "how many" for countable nouns.
- **L83 Adjectives of Quality (Continuation)** — a sorted table of quality adjectives (shape, size, colour, age, taste).
- **L84 Prose** — Reading: prose defined again, fluency as the goal; reading a recipe (verbs cut, chop…) for fluency practice.
- **L85 Prose (Continuation)** — fluency practice through reading a dialogue — a taxi-driver/visitor dialogue about Sierra Leone, and a beans-and-onions recipe dialogue (Binch Akara).
- **L86 Adjectives: Demonstrative Adjectives** — Grammar: this/that (singular), these/those (plural); near vs far.
- **L87 Demonstrative Adjectives (Continuation)** — the near/far × singular/plural table applied.
- **L88 Dictation and Spelling** — Grammar: spelling matters for being understood; capitalising proper nouns wherever they appear.
- **L89 Conversations** — Listening/Speaking: talking about special events — Sierra Leonean examples named: Tangains Festival, Lantern Festival, Independence Day, plus birthdays and weddings.
- **L90 Conversations (Continuation)** — fillers ("Well…", "The thing is…", "You know…") that give thinking time, in continued talk about occasions.
- **L91 Creative Writing** — Writing: short-story structure — introduction, main body, conclusion; plan before writing.
- **L92 Interrogative Adjectives** — Grammar: which (known choices), what (unknown choices), whose (belonging) in questions.
- **L93 Interrogative Adjectives (Continuation)** — which is more formal than what; not every which/what/whose sentence is interrogative.
- **L94 Prose** — Reading: finding main events and main characters; part of a summary of Roald Dahl's James and the Giant Peach read.
- **L95 Prose (Continuation)** — summarising James and the Giant Peach with a second guide — Somebody… Feeling… Met… Then… Ending…
- **L96 Prepositions** — Grammar: prepositions show relationships; prepositions of place — in, on, under, in front of, behind.
- **L97 Prepositions (Continuation)** — place prepositions extended: next to, opposite, between, plus revision of the first set.
- **L98 Narrative Composition** — Listening/Speaking: giving and receiving feedback on a narrative — praise first, then a suggestion; check grammar, spelling, punctuation, vocabulary.
- **L99 Shakespearean Text (Abridged Form)** — Listening/Speaking: The Taming of the Shrew introduced — Katherine (Kate), Bianca, their father Baptista, Lucentio and Petruchio; "shrew" explained.
- **L100 Shakespearean Text (Abridged Form, Continuation)** — the play as comedy: Petruchio agrees to marry the angry, clever Kate and tries to "tame" her; by the end she obeys him (plot only, as the book tells it).

## Grammar and vocabulary spine of this book

- **Tenses:** past continuous (L53–54), present continuous revised with contractions (L59–60), simple future with will/shall/going to and contractions (L63–64, L68). The perfect tenses are still not taught this term.
- **Possession system:** possessive pronouns (L51), possessive nouns with -'s / -s' (L73–74) — the term's main grammar thread, completed next term by possessive adjectives.
- **Adjectives in depth:** general use (L79–80), adjectives of quality with how much/how many (L82–83), demonstratives (L86–87), interrogatives (L92–93).
- **Prepositions of place** (L96–97) — place only; time prepositions come in Term 3.
- **Pronunciation/spelling:** consonants /b/ and /d/ (L65), long vs short "u" (L75), two dictation-and-spelling lessons (L70, L88).
- **Literature spine:** drama through Hamlet (plot summary, L66–67, L70) and The Taming of the Shrew (L99–100); poetry from three traditions (L77), Rasaki's "Africa" (L78), Frost's "Nothing Gold Can Stay" is read in full in the L78 activities in the book's poem set, and pupils write a diamond poem (L81); prose through a recipe, dialogues (L84–85) and James and the Giant Peach summary (L94–95).
- **Writing spine:** narrative essay (L56–57), summary writing (L76), informal letters (L69), creative short story (L91), feedback on compositions (L98); reading widens to newspapers and magazines (L61–62).

## Honest negatives

- No year, ISBN or imprint is printed in the book (metadata only, as above).
- Shakespeare is met only in abridged plot summary and short adapted material — no full scene of Hamlet or The Taming of the Shrew is printed, and the book does not name the source of its Hamlet summary.
- The simple future is taught, but there is no future continuous, no present perfect and no question-tag lesson this term (question tags and present perfect are Term 3).
- Semi-formal and formal letters are not taught this term (informal only, L69); the semi-formal letter arrives in Term 3.
- No BECE guidance or examination practice appears in this term's book.

# Sierra Leone — Language Arts JSS1 Pupil Handbook, Term 3 — FULLY READ + CITATION CHECK PASSED 2026-11-03

## The book itself

- **Title as printed (cover):** "Pupils' handbook for JSS Language ARTS — JSS 1 Term 3", "Free Quality School Education" branding, "STRICTLY NOT FOR SALE".
- **Issuing authority as printed:** Ministry of Basic and Senior Secondary Education; same foreword signed by Mr. Alpha Osman Timbo, Minister of Basic and Senior Secondary Education (thanking DFID); back cover "GOVERNMENT OF SIERRA LEONE … STRICTLY NOT FOR SALE". Introduction as in Terms 1–2.
- **Year:** No publication year, ISBN or imprint printed in the front matter. (PDF metadata only: created July 2018, modified January 2019 — not printed in the book.)
- **Extent:** 142 PDF pages. Note the different numbering: printed page = PDF page − 3 (Lesson 101 starts on printed p. 4 = PDF p. 7; Answer Key starts on printed p. 104 = PDF p. 107). Lessons 101–150, coded PHL-07-101 to PHL-07-150, then answer key and back cover.

## Map of the term — Lessons 101–150 in order

- **L101 A Comprehension Passage** — Reading/Listening: question-before-reading strategy and context clues; the reading text "The Maasai" — Maasai of Kenya/northern Tanzania, lion hunting, the group hunt "olamayio" and the warrior group "Empikas" (facts of the passage, summarised).
- **L102 Descriptive Composition** — Writing: a composition that pictures a person, place or thing, rich in adjectives; topic: daily routines — with a warning that the book's wording means the simple present for routines and habits.
- **L103 Prose** — Reading: chapters and summarising a chapter (routine / people / thoughts and feelings); chapters about Hatty, a girl in the UK whose father teaches at her school (plot only).
- **L104 Prepositions of Time** — Grammar: in / on / at sorted — in for months and parts of day, on for days, at for clock times.
- **L105 Prepositions** — Grammar: time prepositions continued, plus adverbs of time (when, how long, how often).
- **L106 Oral Narrative (My Family)** — Listening/Speaking: describing special occasions with Who/Where/When/What/Why/How — graduations, weddings, Eid, baptisms, birthdays, anniversaries.
- **L107 Pronunciation (Consonant Sounds)** — Listening/Speaking: 24 consonant sounds; a table of six sounds (G, K, F, P, T, D) with example words; spelling and sound do not always match.
- **L108 The Present Perfect Tense** — Grammar: have/has + past participle; uses — begun in the past and still true ("We have lived in Bo since 2009"), and past actions with no specific time.
- **L109 The Present Perfect Tense (Continuation)** — the have/has table practised across I/you/he/she/we/they.
- **L110 Prose** — Reading: summarising — main ideas vs unnecessary details; the Hatty chapters continued.
- **L111 Letter Writing: Semi-Formal Letters** — Writing: the new letter type — to someone older or not well known (a teacher, an older relative); features: address, date, greeting, introduction, body, ending, closing, full name; polite, respectful tone.
- **L112 Semi-Formal Letters (Continuation)** — the letter of request as the working example (asking for school fees, or permission for a class trip).
- **L113 A Comprehension Passage** — Reading/Listening: factual vs inferential questions revised; reading texts "Spiders" and "Lost in the Forest" (a mango-hunting trip goes wrong — plot only).
- **L114 Punctuation** — Grammar: full stop (declarative/imperative), question mark, and the question words that signal them.
- **L115 Punctuation (Continuation)** — commas as pauses; punctuation as a guide to intonation — rising vs falling voice.
- **L116 Reading Strategies** — Reading: careful vs quick reading; "chunking" a text into smaller pieces; an excerpt from Hemingway's The Old Man and the Sea is the chunking text.
- **L117 Reading Strategies (Continuation)** — skimming (main ideas) and scanning (specific information) for examinations like BECE; a locally adapted Emma-style passage whose heroine is renamed "Mary Koroma" is the scanning text.
- **L118 Question Tags** — Grammar: statement + short tag; a positive statement takes a negative tag ("…isn't it?", "…doesn't it?").
- **L119 Question Tags (Continuation)** — the mirror rule: a negative statement takes a positive tag, with contractions shown both ways.
- **L120 Pronunciation: Vowel Sounds** — Listening/Speaking: a short/long table for all five vowels (a: nap–lake; e: bell–sleep; i: sit–bite; o: got–goat; u: hut–…).
- **L121 Composition: My First Day at School** — Writing: narrative essay built on Who/Where/When/What/Why/How, sequenced with transition words (first, then, after, next, later, finally).
- **L122 A Comprehension Passage** — Reading/Listening: reading widely grows vocabulary; the text "Life With Technology" — what mobile phones can do besides calling.
- **L123 The Simple Past Tense** — Grammar revision: regular -ed, just -d after final -e, and irregular verbs to memorise.
- **L124 The Simple Past Tense (Continuation)** — three uses: once-only past events, repeated past actions ("used to"), and finished periods of past time.
- **L125 Prose** — Reading: chapters as natural stopping points; chapters about King Arthur and his Knights of the Round Table and the magic sword Excalibur (plot only).
- **L126 Drama/Play** — Reading: predicting from title and pictures before reading; plays and moral values (honesty, friendship, trust).
- **L127 Informal Letters** — Writing: features revised — address, date, greeting, introduction, body, ending, closing, name.
- **L128 Informal Letters (Continuation)** — informal tone and topics: school, holidays, parties, good or bad news, home life; the letter layout diagrammed.
- **L129 Possessive Pronouns** — Grammar: possessive pronouns (replace the noun — "That book is his") set against possessive adjectives (before the noun — "his book").
- **L130 Dictation and Spelling** — Grammar: writing down what you hear; checking, correcting and re-using misspelled words in sentences; the dictation text is "Christmas Presents", about waste at Christmas.
- **L131 Prepositions** — Grammar: in/on/at consolidated in one table — times, days, dates, months, years, seasons, holidays (Christmas, Eid named).
- **L132 Prepositions and Adverbs** — Grammar: direction words sorted — adverbs describe the verb (turn right), prepositions link words (at the corner); place prepositions listed.
- **L133 Oral Descriptive** — Listening/Speaking: describing a place through all five senses with strong adjectives so listeners can picture it.
- **L134 Shakespearean Text (Abridged Form)** — Reading/Listening/Speaking: Macbeth introduced — a Scottish nobleman, three witches' prophecy that he will be king, and the plan with Lady Macbeth to kill the King (plot only).
- **L135 Shakespearean Text (Abridged Form, Continuation)** — character study of Macbeth and Lady Macbeth: describing and comparing the two.
- **L136 The Present Continuous Tense** — Grammar revision: am/is/are + verb-ing; negatives with "not".
- **L137 The Present Continuous Tense (Continuation)** — contractions in the present continuous, positive and negative (I'm, you're, he's… / I'm not…).
- **L138 Punctuation and Intonation** — Reading: full stop, question mark and comma tabled together as reading-aloud signals; chapters about Miriam, worried about Eid — she sells her vase to buy her husband Ahmad a watch strap, while he sells his watch to buy her flowers (plot only).
- **L139 Prose** — Reading: end-of-chapter self-questioning (Did I understand? Can I summarise? New words? What next?); the Miriam chapters continued.
- **L140 Poetry** — Reading: how poems look on the page (lines, variable punctuation, rhyme optional); a family poem built on metaphors (mother as a blanket, father as a ladder, aunt as a mango).
- **L141 Drama: Literary Terms** — Reading: simile, metaphor and personification defined for drama, with everyday examples.
- **L142 Drama** — Reading: how a play looks on the page — the character's name followed by a colon; in performance you speak only the words, not the name; stage descriptions help the reader.
- **L143 Drama (Continuation)** — performing a play: read it carefully first, think about the characters, speak loudly and clearly with correct intonation.
- **L144 The Past Continuous Tense** — Grammar revision: was/were + verb-ing for action continuing around a past point, and for interrupted action.
- **L145 The Past Continuous Tense (Continuation)** — contractions only in negative past-continuous sentences; no contractions in positives or questions.
- **L146 Future Tenses** — Grammar revision: will + verb for the simple future; negatives with "will not".
- **L147 Future Tenses (Continuation)** — will vs shall (shall is formal, old-fashioned, I/we only); positive/negative/question table.
- **L148 Prose** — Reading: examination reading strategies combined — read the questions first, scanning and skimming.
- **L149 Composition (Descriptive)** — Writing: description that lets the reader see, hear, smell, taste and touch; planning the descriptive words first.
- **L150 Composition (Descriptive, Continuation)** — plan → first draft → read it yourself and have someone else read it → improve; the JSS 1 course ends here.

## Grammar and vocabulary spine of this book

- **New grammar:** present perfect (L108–109) — the last major tense of JSS 1; prepositions of time in/on/at, fully tabled twice (L104–105, L131) and separated from adverbs (L132); question tags in both directions (L118–119); possessive pronouns vs possessive adjectives (L129).
- **Revision grammar:** simple past (L123–124), present continuous + contractions (L136–137), past continuous (L144–145), future will/shall (L146–147), punctuation (L114–115, L138).
- **Pronunciation:** 24 consonant sounds (L107); short/long sounds for every vowel (L120).
- **Reading spine:** strategies become explicit this term — questioning before reading (L101), chunking (L116), skimming and scanning, reading the questions first (L117, L148); texts: "The Maasai", Hatty chapters, "Spiders", "Lost in the Forest", Hemingway excerpt, the adapted "Mary Koroma" passage, "Life With Technology", King Arthur chapters, Macbeth (abridged), the Miriam Eid chapters, and — as L148's reading text — the story of the bad-tempered boy who hammers nails into a fence and then pulls them out as he learns to control his anger, leaving scars (plot only).
- **Writing spine:** descriptive composition (L102, L149–150), the new semi-formal letter and letter of request (L111–112), informal letters revised (L127–128), narrative with transition words (L121).

## Honest negatives

- No year, ISBN or imprint is printed (metadata only, as above).
- Shakespeare (Macbeth) and Hemingway are again met only in abridged summary or a short excerpt; no full play or story is printed, and the Emma-style passage is silently localised (heroine renamed) without naming Jane Austen or the source novel in the lesson.
- Formal letters are still not taught in JSS 1 — the year ends at semi-formal.
- There is no direct/indirect speech, no conditional sentences and no passive voice anywhere in JSS 1's three terms; those are left to later years.
- The book prints no separate vocabulary list; new words are handled inside passages only.

## Citation check

**15/15 anchors passed** against the actual PDF text. Anchors include: PDF p. 1 "STRICTLY NOT FOR SALE"; p. 2 "Alpha Osman Timbo"; p. 4 contents "Lesson 101: A Comprehension Passage"; p. 8 "The Maasai"; p. 22 "The Present Perfect Tense"; p. 29 "Letter Writing: Semi-Formal"; p. 43 "Question Tags"; p. 47 "Pronunciation: Vowel Sounds"; p. 55 "King Arthur"; p. 72 "Macbeth"; p. 76 "The Present Continuous Tense"; p. 84 "Poetry"; p. 92 "The Past Continuous Tense"; p. 107 "Answer Key"; p. 142 "GOVERNMENT OF SIERRA LEONE".
