# Sierra Leone — Language Arts JSS2 Pupil Handbook, Term 1 — FULLY READ + CITATION CHECK PASSED 2026-11-03

## The book itself

- **Title as printed (cover):** "Pupils' handbook for JSS Language ARTS — JSS 2 Term 1", "Free Quality School Education" branding, "STRICTLY NOT FOR SALE".
- **Issuing authority as printed:** Ministry of Basic and Senior Secondary Education; same foreword signed by Mr. Alpha Osman Timbo, Minister of Basic and Senior Secondary Education (thanking DFID); back cover "GOVERNMENT OF SIERRA LEONE … STRICTLY NOT FOR SALE". Introduction as in JSS 1 (Teachers' Guide / National Curriculum / WAEC alignment).
- **Year:** No publication year, ISBN or imprint printed in the front matter. (PDF metadata only: created July 2018, modified January 2019 — not printed in the book.)
- **Extent:** 142 PDF pages. Printed page = PDF page − 5 (Lesson 1 starts on printed p. 2 = PDF p. 7; Answer Key starts on printed p. 105 = PDF p. 110). Lessons 1–50 for JSS 2, coded PHL-08-001 to PHL-08-050, then answer key and back cover.
- **Character of the year:** JSS 2 is openly a revision-and-extension year — most lesson titles say "Revision of…" and BECE (the end-of-JSS 3 examination) is named from Lesson 6 onward.

## Map of the term — Lessons 1–50 in order

- **L1 Introducing Myself** — Listening/Speaking: self-introduction with sentence starters (I belong to… / I have studied… / I enjoy… / I live in… / I would like to learn…).
- **L2 Composition** — Writing: compositions defined (essay, story, poem, play); paragraphs with one main idea plus supporting details; choosing a title (worked example: "Living in Freetown").
- **L3 Revision of Some Parts of Speech (Nouns)** — Grammar: the eight parts of speech listed; proper vs common nouns; abstract nouns within common nouns.
- **L4 Pronouns** — Grammar: subject pronouns (I, you, he, she, it, we, they) vs object pronouns (me, you, him, her, it, us, them).
- **L5 Verbs** — Grammar: transitive verbs need an object (bring some coffee) vs intransitive verbs that stand alone or take an adverb.
- **L6 Comprehension Passage** — Reading/Listening/Speaking: BECE unseen-passage method — read more than once, main idea of passage and of each paragraph, context clues; the passage is a story about Solomon and his son Daniel, who give money to a sugarcane seller whose wife is unwell (plot only).
- **L7 Reading Comprehension** — Reading: "The Rice Farmer" — retired post-office worker Mr Bundu, who must keep working (plot only); main-idea method: read the whole passage, check first and last paragraphs.
- **L8 Verb Tenses** — Grammar: the tense system on one verb — simple present, present continuous, present perfect, simple past, past continuous, past perfect, simple future ("go to the market" set).
- **L9 The Simple Present Tense** — Grammar revision: habits and facts; questions with do/does + subject + verb.
- **L10 Types of Letters (Informal Letters)** — Writing: the informal letter's eight features — address, date, greeting, introduction, body, ending, closing, signature/name.
- **L11 Features or Format of Informal Letters** — Writing: informal letters can carry jokes, slang and contractions; three kinds — descriptive, persuasive, narrative.
- **L12 Features or Format of Informal Letters (Continuation)** — the three kinds and the eight features practised together.
- **L13 The Simple Past Tense** — Grammar revision: regular -ed/-d/-ied; irregulars listed (drink–drank, think–thought, begin–began, fall–fell, cut–cut).
- **L14 The Present Continuous Tense** — Grammar revision: am/is/are + verb-ing, in question, positive and negative tables.
- **L15 The Past Continuous Tense** — Grammar revision: interrupted action, past weather/conditions, and action at a specific past time.
- **L16 Comprehension Passage** — Reading: BECE method revised (re-read, main idea, context clues).
- **L17 Comprehension Passage** — Reading: characters and prediction — a hospital story about Grandpa Amad, who cannot see the window, and Grandpa Jesse, who describes a park, a lake and a cotton tree that are not really there, to keep him happy (plot only).
- **L18 The Simple Future Tense** — Grammar revision: two forms — be + going to, and will/shall + verb.
- **L19 Revision of the Simple Past and Simple Future Tenses** — regular/irregular past against future forms, side by side.
- **L20 Revision of the Three Main Tenses** — simple present, past and future contrasted on the same example (read a book).
- **L21 The Future Continuous Tense** — Grammar (new): shall be / will be + verb-ing for an action in progress at a future time ("At 10 am tomorrow, I shall be walking to school").
- **L22 Revision of the Past and Future Continuous Tenses** — was/were + -ing against will be + -ing.
- **L23 Writing an Informal Letter** — Writing: three main paragraphs — introduction (well-being, updates, reason), body, ending.
- **L24 Writing an Informal Letter (Continuation)** — informal vs formal purpose; contractions, jokes and slang as informal markers.
- **L25 Revision of an Informal Letter** — chatty model sentences ("Anyway, I have to go now!") checked against the full feature list.
- **L26 Poetry; Meaning of Poetry; Literary Devices** — Reading: how a poem looks (lines, optional rhyme); poets write to express emotion; devices introduced.
- **L27 Revision of Some Literary Devices** — parallelism (repeated sentence structure) and alliteration (repeated first sounds) defined with examples.
- **L28 Reading of Poems** — Reading: reading a poem aloud — pause at punctuation, not at line ends; stress nouns, verbs and adjectives; the poem is Christina Rossetti's "What is Pink?" (colours matched to sky, swan, pears, grass, clouds, orange).
- **L29 Conversations** — Listening/Speaking: agreeing and disagreeing politely ("I see what you are saying but I feel…", "I beg to differ", "In my opinion…"); the working text discusses arranged marriages vs love matches.
- **L30 Conversations (Continuation)** — speak clearly, wait for the other person, answer their point; more discussion phrases.
- **L31 Synonyms** — Grammar: words of similar meaning; the synonym must fit the sentence ("sweet" → "nice", not "sugary").
- **L32 Semi-Formal Letters** — Writing: the semi-formal letter's features, closings "Yours sincerely / With best wishes", signature.
- **L33 Writing a Semi-Formal Letter** — Writing: respectful wording — incorrect/correct pairs ("Hey, how've you been?" → "I hope you have been well"; "I want you to reply" → "I look forward to your reply").
- **L34 Writing a Semi-Formal Letter (Continuation)** — punctuation discipline in letters: full stops, commas, capitals for sentences and proper nouns, new paragraph for a new point.
- **L35 Drama** — Reading: characters and dialogue; you learn a character from what they say and do.
- **L36 Antonyms** — Grammar: opposite meanings; some antonyms are made with prefixes/suffixes (careful–careless, necessary–unnecessary, interested–disinterested, fold–unfold).
- **L37 Antonyms (Continuation)** — two routes: a different word (kind–mean) or a prefix/suffix (helpful–helpless); "hard" shows an antonym must match the sense in the text.
- **L38 Revision of Synonyms and Antonyms** — both systems together; meaning-in-context first.
- **L39 Drama (Continuation)** — Reading: Shakespeare's The Merchant of Venice introduced as a 16th-century romantic comedy about a merchant in Venice, Italy.
- **L40 Drama (Continuation)** — reading old play language aloud with expression to understand it; the Merchant of Venice lessons continue.
- **L41 Possessive Pronouns** — Grammar revision: noun + is/are + possessive pronoun ("This book is hers").
- **L42 Possessive Pronouns (Continuation)** — possessive adjective vs possessive pronoun in one table (my spoon / the spoon is mine; their spoon / theirs).
- **L43 Compositions** — Writing: composition types restated; imaginative types (story, play, narrative) among them; titles and linked paragraphs.
- **L44 Composition – Descriptive Writing** — Writing: description through the five senses; three parts — introduction, main body, conclusion.
- **L45 Revision of Possessive Pronouns** — pronoun vs adjective position revised (after the noun vs before the noun).
- **L46 Adjectives** — Grammar: proper adjectives made from proper nouns (Sierra Leone → Sierra Leonean, China → Chinese, Sweden → Swedish), always capitalised; brands included.
- **L47 Positive and Comparative Adjectives** — Grammar: positive form, then comparatives — add -er, y → i + -er (prettier), double the final consonant (hotter).
- **L48 Adjectives** — Grammar: superlatives added — comparatives compare two, superlatives compare more than two; "more" before longer adjectives.
- **L49 Comprehension** — Reading: using a dictionary — alphabetical order worked step by step (the example word is "citizen", found between "ch" and "ci" words); the reading passage is "Jellyfish".
- **L50 Comprehension (Continuation)** — dictionary vs context clues; the reading passage is "The Olympics".

## Grammar and vocabulary spine of this book

- **Tense system completed and revised:** all seven forms named in L8; simple present/past/future revised (L9, L13, L18–20); present and past continuous revised (L14–15, L22); the one new tense is the future continuous (L21). Present perfect and past perfect are named in L8's list but get no lesson of their own this term.
- **Word classes:** nouns and pronouns revised (L3–4); transitive/intransitive verbs (L5); synonyms and antonyms as a vocabulary system (L31, L36–38); proper adjectives and comparison of adjectives (L46–48); possession completed (L41–42, L45).
- **Letters:** the informal letter is taken apart and rebuilt over six lessons (L10–12, L23–25); the semi-formal letter is taught over three (L32–34) — one year earlier in the pupil's life than the same content appears in the JSS 1 Term 3 book's sequence of topics.
- **Literature:** poetry devices (parallelism, alliteration — L26–27), Keats' "The Naughty Boy" read in the L26–27 lesson set (a boy runs to Scotland and finds everything the same — plot only), Rossetti's "What is Pink?" (L28); drama through The Merchant of Venice in summary/adapted form (L35, L39–40).
- **Study skills:** BECE unseen-passage method (L6–7, L16–17) and dictionary skills (L49–50) are the term's explicit examination spine.

## Honest negatives

- No year, ISBN or imprint is printed (metadata only, as above).
- Despite the L8 list, there is no lesson teaching the present perfect or past perfect in this term, and no question tags, prepositions or punctuation lessons — JSS 2 Term 1 is deliberately narrow: tenses, letters, synonyms/antonyms, adjectives and comprehension.
- The Merchant of Venice is met in summary and short adapted dialogue only; no full Shakespeare scene is printed.
- There is no formal letter this term, and no direct/indirect speech yet (that arrives in JSS 2 Term 3).

## Citation check

**15/15 anchors passed** against the actual PDF text. Anchors include: PDF p. 1 "STRICTLY NOT FOR SALE"; p. 2 "Alpha Osman Timbo"; p. 4 contents "Lesson 1: Introducing Myself"; p. 7 "Introducing Myself"; p. 20 "Verb Tenses"; p. 46 "The Future Continuous Tense"; p. 56 "Literary Devices"; p. 61 "Christina Rossetti"; p. 68 "Synonyms"; p. 84 "The Merchant of Venice"; p. 99 "Adjectives"; p. 106 "Jellyfish"; p. 110 "Answer Key"; p. 142 "GOVERNMENT OF SIERRA LEONE"; plus the second anchor on PDF p. 84 (the Lesson 39 drama lesson itself, headed "Drama").

# Sierra Leone — Language Arts JSS2 Pupil Handbook, Term 2 — FULLY READ + CITATION CHECK PASSED 2026-11-03

## The book itself

- **Title as printed (cover):** "Pupils' handbook for JSS Language ARTS — JSS 2 Term 2", "Free Quality School Education" branding, "STRICTLY NOT FOR SALE".
- **Issuing authority as printed:** Ministry of Basic and Senior Secondary Education; same foreword signed by Mr. Alpha Osman Timbo, Minister of Basic and Senior Secondary Education (thanking DFID); back cover "GOVERNMENT OF SIERRA LEONE … STRICTLY NOT FOR SALE". Introduction as in JSS 1.
- **Year:** No publication year, ISBN or imprint printed in the front matter. (PDF metadata only: created July 2018, modified January 2019 — not printed in the book.)
- **Extent:** 130 PDF pages. Printed page = PDF page − 5 (Lesson 51 starts on printed p. 2 = PDF p. 7; Answer Key starts on printed p. 96 = PDF p. 101). Lessons 51–100, coded PHL-08-051 to PHL-08-100, then answer key and back cover.
- **Notable inclusion:** Lesson 86 is a full statement of what the BECE examination tests — the only lesson of its kind in the six books read here.

## Map of the term — Lessons 51–100 in order

- **L51 Poetry** — Reading: literary devices revised (simile, metaphor), rhyming words, and theme as a poem's main idea; the poem is Lewis Carroll's "The Crocodile Poem" — a crocodile grins and "welcomes" little fishes (the questions expose the irony: he means to eat them).
- **L52 Creative Writing** — Writing: writing poems with simile, metaphor and personification.
- **L53 Creative Writing (Continuation)** — alliteration added ("She sells sea shells on the seashore"); writing the pupil's own poem.
- **L54 Spelling Drills** — Grammar: words that sound alike but differ — there/their/they're and two/too/to explained and drilled.
- **L55 Reading Comprehension** — Reading: main idea of text and of each paragraph, for summary; an Aesop's fable — "The Boy Who Cried Wolf" (a bored shepherd boy, false alarms, and the real wolf — plot only).
- **L56 Question Tags** — Grammar: negative statement + positive tag ("She is not going to the market, is she?").
- **L57 Question Tags (Continuation)** — positive statement + negative tag, in contractions ("…isn't it?", "…didn't they?").
- **L58 Fluent Reading** — Reading: reading quickly, correctly and with expression; stress, intonation and punctuation pauses; a night-time encounter with a policeman is the reading text.
- **L59 Reading Comprehension** — Reading: an unseen-text checklist — main idea, main characters, new words, supporting details, tone and mood; the text is "Dowry in India", about gifts and money from a bride's family to a groom's family.
- **L60 Oral Reading** — Reading: matching voice to meaning — whispering vs demanding in two mother-and-daughter chocolate-bar sentences; the passage is "The Old Man's Secret" (a dying old man and his son — plot only).
- **L61 Pronunciation** — Listening/Speaking: 24 consonant sounds; the same sound from different letters (c/k in cap/kite) and one letter with different sounds (c in cap/fence).
- **L62 Review of Plural Nouns** — Grammar: regular plurals — add -s; add -es after s/ss/x/ch/sh.
- **L63 Review of Plural Nouns (Continuation)** — exceptions: -f/-fe → -ves (wife–wives, shelf–shelves); consonant + y → -ies (activity–activities); vowel + y keeps -s.
- **L64 Semi-Formal Letters** — Writing: features revised; a new use named — a semi-formal letter of invitation to a party or event.
- **L65 Semi-Formal Letters (Continuation)** — tone rules: no contractions (write "do not", "will not"), respectful but not stiff.
- **L66 Spelling Drills** — Grammar: homophones defined and drilled (hear–here, ate–eight, write–right).
- **L67 Reading Newspapers or Magazines** — Reading: articles are about current events; read for topic, people/places/times, message and new words.
- **L68 Reading Newspapers or Magazines (Continuation)** — Writing: writing your own article on a current event, with opinion phrases ("I believe that…", "In my opinion…", "Based on these facts, I think that…").
- **L69 Drama/Play** — Writing: writing a play — characters, actors, dialogue; a badly written vs well written contrast (telling "Amina was sad" vs showing her crying and speaking).
- **L70 Drama/Play (Continuation)** — Reading: dramatising — expression, loudness, clarity, reading a part several times first; the play is "The Missing Ball" (Alusain's ball falls in a fast river; Lamin threw it — plot only).
- **L71 Conversation** — Listening/Speaking: narrating an incident in the order it happened, with feelings and the five senses; past tense because it already happened.
- **L72 Conversation (Continuation)** — real conversation as turn-taking: questions, interruptions, comments, replies.
- **L73 Synonyms** — Grammar: BECE substitution practice — the synonym must keep the sentence's meaning ("mad" = angry, not crazy, in the worked example).
- **L74 Antonyms** — Grammar: words can have several antonyms (clever — foolish/unintelligent); choose by context.
- **L75 Spelling and Dictation** — Reading/Listening/Speaking: listening for main points and new words; note-taking while listening.
- **L76 Revision of Possessive Adjectives** — Grammar: my, your, his, her, our, their before the noun, singular and plural holders.
- **L77 Review of Possessive Pronouns and Possessive Adjectives** — the distinction drilled: adjectives stand before a noun; pronouns (mine, yours, his, hers, its, ours, theirs) stand alone and replace it.
- **L78 Continuous Reading of the Prose Text** — Reading: prose = sentences and paragraphs; read aloud with expression; a prose text is moral — "The Walk Back Home", a trader, his son and their horse, and the crowd's changing criticism (the answers name the moral; plot only).
- **L79 Continuous Reading of the Prose Text (Continuation)** — literal vs inferential questions in examinations; the text is a summary of Jules Verne's A Journey to the Centre of the Earth — Professor Lidenbrock, his nephew Axel and an ancient Icelandic text (plot only).
- **L80 Question Types** — Grammar: five question types tabled — yes/no, choice, wh- questions and two more forms, with the answer shape each one expects.
- **L81 Descriptive Composition** — Writing: describe a thing, process, person or event; colourful language, adjectives and adverbs, the five senses.
- **L82 Descriptive Composition (Continuation)** — structure (title, introduction, body, conclusion) and stock phrases for each part ("Once,…", "Then,…", "In the end, I realised that…").
- **L83 Pronunciation** — Listening/Speaking: short /i/ (in, pin, tick) vs long /i:/ — with the warning that the long sound can be spelled with other letters, e.g. "y" in rhyme.
- **L84 Prose** — Reading: fluent reading + summary method (main ideas, WH questions, key words); the text is "When My Parents Were Locked Out" — a ten-year-old left in charge of a four-year-old (plot only).
- **L85 Prose (Continuation)** — objective questions (one clear answer in the text) vs subjective questions (reasons and opinions); the same locked-out story in a different version.
- **L86 BECE Requirements** — Reading/Writing: what BECE English tests — lexis and structure (tenses, concord, conditionals, phrasal verbs, active/passive, direct and reported speech, question tags, phrases and clauses, idioms, parts of speech) among the areas listed, across two papers, at the end of JSS 3, with SSS or vocational school as the next step.
- **L87 Review Types of Dramas** — Reading: comedy and tragedy both traced to Ancient Greece, 5th century BC; comedy — relatable characters, everyday situations, laughter.
- **L88 Review Types of Dramas (Continuation)** — identifying type by language and mood: light/funny vs serious/dark; Romeo and Juliet recalled as a great tragedy.
- **L89 Review of Verbs** — Reading: verbs show action/state and time; the infinitive ("to read") as the dictionary form, and "to" before it is not a preposition there.
- **L90 Review of Verb Tenses (Continuation)** — Grammar: the full tense table on "to enjoy" — simple present/past/future, present/past continuous and future continuous.
- **L91 Reading Comprehension Prose** — Reading: working out new words from contextual clues ("devastated" beside "sad" and "crying"), with a dictionary as the check; the text is "Examination Timetable".
- **L92 Reading Comprehension Prose (Continuation)** — summarising from main ideas and important details; the examination-timetable text continued.
- **L93 Oral Description** — Listening/Speaking: giving instructions for a process (sewing a dress, making a basket) in logical order; imperative sentences can start with the verb — "How to Boil Water" is the worked example.
- **L94 Oral Description (Continuation)** — giving directions: picture the route, use landmarks (school, famous shop, government office).
- **L95 Oral Description (Continuation)** — direction phrases drilled (walk past…, go over/under…, take a left before/after…, behind/between/next to).
- **L96 Poetry** — Reading: symbols in poems (white = peace, red = love or war, lion = strength, tree = wisdom); two poems — Wordsworth's "My Heart Leaps Up" (1807, joy at a rainbow) and a poem by Rabindranath Tagore.
- **L97 Poetry (Continuation)** — how to understand a difficult poem (re-read, watch punctuation, think about what words symbolise, notice your feelings); Tagore's "Freedom" is read — freedom from fear for his motherland.
- **L98 Sentence Practice** — Grammar: the eight parts of speech as a sentence's building blocks, categories within categories.
- **L99 Paragraph Development** — Writing: topic sentence, supporting sentences, concluding sentence, in a worked paragraph about pupils making a timetable.
- **L100 Paragraph Development (Continuation)** — introduction, body and conclusion paragraphs, each well-developed (beginning topic sentence, middle support and example).

## Grammar and vocabulary spine of this book

- **Grammar:** question tags both ways (L56–57); plural nouns fully revised incl. exceptions (L62–63); possessive adjectives vs pronouns settled (L76–77); question types (L80); verbs and the infinitive (L89); the six-form tense table (L90); parts of speech as sentence building blocks (L98).
- **Spelling strand (new weight this term):** homophone sets there/their/they're and two/too/to (L54), homophones defined (L66), dictation and listening note-taking (L75).
- **Pronunciation:** consonant-sound traps (L61); short vs long /i/ (L83).
- **Vocabulary method:** synonyms/antonyms now taught as an examination substitution skill (L73–74); context clues with dictionary checking (L91).
- **Literature spine:** Carroll's crocodile poem and irony (L51); Aesop's "The Boy Who Cried Wolf" (L55); an original school play, "The Missing Ball" (L70); folk/prose tales (L78) and a Verne summary (L79); Wordsworth and Tagore poems on rainbows and freedom (L96–97); drama types with Greek origins (L87–88).
- **Writing spine:** poems (L52–53), semi-formal letters with a no-contractions rule (L64–65), writing a newspaper article and a play (L68–69), descriptive composition (L81–82), paragraph development (L99–100) — and the BECE map of everything (L86).

## Honest negatives

- No year, ISBN or imprint is printed (metadata only, as above).
- Although L86 lists conditionals, passive voice and phrasal verbs as BECE areas, none of them is taught in this term's lessons; the book names the gap rather than filling it.
- There is no new tense taught this term (revision only), no formal letter, and no direct/indirect speech yet — that is held for Term 3.
- The Verne text is a summary, not Verne's own text, and Tagore's poem is printed without its collection or original-language information beyond the poet's name and the lesson's framing.

## Citation check

**15/15 anchors passed** against the actual PDF text. Anchors include: PDF p. 1 "STRICTLY NOT FOR SALE"; p. 2 "Alpha Osman Timbo"; p. 4 contents "Lesson 51: Poetry"; p. 7 "The Crocodile Poem"; p. 14 "The Boy Who Cried Wolf"; p. 29 "Review of Plural Nouns"; p. 42 "The Missing Ball"; p. 57 "The Walk Back Home"; p. 59 "Journey to the Centre of the Earth"; p. 73 "BECE"; p. 83 "Reading Comprehension Prose"; p. 90 "William Wordsworth"; p. 93 "Rabindranath Tagore"; p. 101 "Answer Key"; p. 130 "GOVERNMENT OF SIERRA LEONE".

# Sierra Leone — Language Arts JSS2 Pupil Handbook, Term 3 — FULLY READ + CITATION CHECK PASSED 2026-11-03

## The book itself

- **Title as printed (cover):** "Pupils' handbook for JSS Language ARTS — JSS 2 Term 3", "Free Quality School Education" branding, "STRICTLY NOT FOR SALE".
- **Issuing authority as printed:** Ministry of Basic and Senior Secondary Education; same foreword signed by Mr. Alpha Osman Timbo, Minister of Basic and Senior Secondary Education (thanking DFID); back cover "GOVERNMENT OF SIERRA LEONE … STRICTLY NOT FOR SALE". Introduction as in JSS 1.
- **Year:** No publication year, ISBN or imprint printed in the front matter. (PDF metadata only: created July 2018, modified January 2019 — not printed in the book.)
- **Extent:** 130 PDF pages. Printed page = PDF page − 5 (Lesson 101 starts on printed p. 2 = PDF p. 7; Answer Key starts on printed p. 100 = PDF p. 105; the answer key runs to the last text page, PDF p. 128). Lessons 101–150, coded PHL-08-101 to PHL-08-150.
- **Character of the term:** the grammar-heaviest term of the six books — tense tables for present, past and future families, regular/irregular verbs, direct and indirect speech, and simple/compound/complex sentences — while the reading spine is built from named African and English novels, mostly in summary.

## Map of the term — Lessons 101–150 in order

- **L101 Verb Tenses** — Grammar: past continuous (was/were + verb-ing) in positive, negative and question forms, against the simple past.
- **L102 Verb Tenses (Continuation)** — the past perfect: had + past participle for the earlier of two past actions (the book's overview wording also slips once and calls the form "present perfect" while describing had — noted as printed); regular participles with -ed, irregulars to learn.
- **L103 Comprehension** — Reading: opinion passages vs information passages; main idea of the whole and of each paragraph.
- **L104 Comprehension** — Reading: understanding characters and their feelings to understand actions and new words (a sad character makes "gloomy" clear).
- **L105 Comprehension (Continuation)** — context clues and dictionary, then making your own sentences with new words so they stick.
- **L106 Composition (Descriptive and Narrative)** — Writing: the two composition kinds compared in a table — narrative reports events (often 1st person, with dialogue); descriptive pictures a person/object/place through the senses.
- **L107 Composition** — Writing: narrative rules — events in order, planned paragraphs, clear who-said-what, "I" as narrator.
- **L108 Composition (Continuation)** — descriptive writing that shows, not tells, with adjectives and adverbs (a cat's whiskers and bushy tail; a classroom's green walls and artwork).
- **L109 Poetry** — Reading/Speaking: reading poems aloud — expression, pauses, speed and volume matched to mood (sad slowly and quietly, happy with a smile).
- **L110 Poetry (Continuation)** — devices for writing poems: simile, metaphor, personification, alliteration, each with an example.
- **L111 Verb Tenses** — Grammar: the present family in one table — simple present, present continuous, present perfect, with formation and use.
- **L112 Verb Tenses (Continuation)** — the past family table — simple past, past continuous, past perfect.
- **L113 Verb Tenses (Continuation)** — the future perfect (new): will + have + past participle for an action complete by a future time, usually with a time expression.
- **L114 Composition** — Writing: writing an article for a newspaper or magazine — formal language, facts and opinions, quotes/facts/statistics, short sentences, a strong title.
- **L115 Composition (Continuation)** — articles about social issues (pollution, drug abuse, crime, lack of education, poverty named) with suggestions for the reader.
- **L116 Comprehension: Prose** — Reading: a novel is one long fictional story with characters and a plot; background on Chimamanda Ngozi Adichie and her novel Half of a Yellow Sun before reading about it.
- **L117 Comprehension: Prose (Continuation)** — chapters as mini-stories; a summary of Chinua Achebe's Things Fall Apart — the book notes it was his first novel, published in 1958 (plot handled in summary only).
- **L118 Question Tags** — Grammar revision: tags with helping verbs, contraction table (isn't, aren't, wasn't, weren't, won't…).
- **L119 Question Tags (Continuation)** — tags in the simple present use do/does ("He speaks English, doesn't he?").
- **L120 Question Tags (Continuation)** — tags in the past tense (did/didn't).
- **L121 Comprehension** — Reading: from main ideas to a written summary that uses important words from the passage.
- **L122 Comprehension (Continuation)** — inferring new words from context, helped by characters, tone and mood.
- **L123 Possessive Adjectives** — Grammar: the full table — I→my, you→your, he→his, she→her, it→its, we→our, they→their.
- **L124 Possessive Adjectives (Continuation)** — possessive adjectives before nouns, then demonstratives alongside (this/that/these/those, near/far — the book calls the four words demonstrative pronouns in this lesson's table, as printed).
- **L125 Possessive Adjectives (Continuation)** — singular-noun set (my, your, his, her, its) vs plural-noun set (our, your, their).
- **L126 Comprehension: Prose** — Reading: prose defined; a summary of Adichie's Purple Hibiscus — set in Nigeria in a time of civil unrest, which the book explains in plain words.
- **L127 Comprehension: Prose (Continuation)** — a summary of Mariama Bâ's So Long a Letter — written in French, set in Senegal before and after independence; the epistolary novel (a story told in letters) explained.
- **L128 Comprehension: Prose (Continuation)** — antonyms inside prose: a word's meaning in the text decides its opposite ("light" against heavy, or against dark).
- **L129 Prepositions** — Grammar: prepositions of time, place and position; the in/on/at table again (times, days, seasons, months, years).
- **L130 Verb Tenses: Regular Verbs** — Grammar: simple past spelling rules — -ed, -d after -e, consonant + y → -ied, double the final consonant (stop–stopped).
- **L131 Verb Tenses: Irregular Verbs** — Grammar: no rule — memorise; simple past vs past participle, the participle used with has/have ("Nelson Mandela had spent years in prison…" is the book's example sentence).
- **L132 Verb Tenses: Irregular Verbs (Continuation)** — grouping irregulars: verbs that do not change (put, cut, hit, let), verbs that change once, verbs that change twice.
- **L133 Verb Tenses: Irregular Verbs (Continuation)** — third-person simple-present spelling: consonant + y → -ies (cry–cries); add -es after o/ch/sh/ss/x (watch–watches).
- **L134 Comprehension** — Reading: predicting before reading; the story "Edward's Bad Day" — a forgetful man; vocabulary glossed first (toss and turn, glance, panic, embarrassed, wander).
- **L135 Comprehension (Continuation)** — a passage from Charles Dickens' Oliver Twist — an orphan boy in mid-19th-century England; period vocabulary glossed (vast, lad, bred, intimation).
- **L136 Direct and Indirect Speech** — Grammar (new): direct speech quotes exact words inside inverted commas; indirect speech reports them, without inverted commas and with a past verb form.
- **L137 Direct and Indirect Speech (Continuation)** — the differences listed; "that" after "said" in reported speech.
- **L138 Direct and Indirect Speech (Continuation)** — the conversion method: change the pronoun, change present tense to past, change time words (today → that day).
- **L139 Comprehension** — Reading: a text about tourism — vocabulary first (revenue, infrastructure, showcase, vibrant, citizens, dispel).
- **L140 Comprehension: Prose** — Reading: a summary of Ngũgĩ wa Thiong'o's A Grain of Wheat, set just before Kenyan independence; vocabulary glossed (sooty, thatch, rebellion, haunted, captured, hanged, deny).
- **L141 Sentences** — Grammar (new): the simple sentence — one independent clause (subject + verb); the compound sentence — two independent clauses joined by and/but/for/or/so/yet.
- **L142 Sentences (Continuation)** — simple-sentence features drilled (one clause, stands alone).
- **L143 Sentences** — Grammar: compound sentences identified in short texts, joining word found (a text about cassava in Africa is one example).
- **L144 Comprehension** — Reading: a text about big African cities — Lagos largest, then Cairo, Kinshasa, Luanda and Nairobi (as printed); finding the most important ideas in a long text.
- **L145 Comprehension: Prose** — Reading: Oliver Twist again — Oliver reaches the edge of London and meets Jack Dawkins, whose description is the passage's focus.
- **L146 Sentences** — Grammar: the complex sentence — one independent clause plus a dependent clause that cannot stand alone ("when my aunt cooks it").
- **L147 Sentences (Continuation)** — simple vs compound revised side by side.
- **L148 Verb Tenses** — Grammar: the three future tenses revised — simple future, future continuous, future perfect, with their formation rules (the book's simple-future rule is printed as "subject + will/shall + be + main verb", as printed).
- **L149 Comprehension** — Reading: a text about Malawi — south-eastern Africa, third-largest lake in Africa (as printed); inferring word meanings from context (consumption, smallholder, commercial value glossed).
- **L150 Comprehension: Prose** — Reading: a summary of Oliver Twist completes the year — Fagin trains orphan boys to pick pockets; Oliver is sent out, is taken in after a robbery at a rich household, and the questions end on the story's moral (plot only).

## Grammar and vocabulary spine of this book

- **Tense families completed:** present family (L111), past family incl. past perfect (L101–102, L112), future family incl. the new future perfect (L113, L148); regular-verb spelling rules (L130) and irregular verbs grouped for memorising, past vs participle (L131–133).
- **New sentence grammar:** direct and indirect speech with a three-step conversion method (L136–138); simple, compound and complex sentences and clauses (L141–143, L146–147).
- **Revised grammar:** question tags across present and past (L118–120); possessive adjectives in full, with demonstratives (L123–125); prepositions in/on/at (L129).
- **Reading spine — the strongest of the six books:** Adichie's Half of a Yellow Sun (L116) and Purple Hibiscus (L126), Achebe's Things Fall Apart (L117), Bâ's So Long a Letter (L127), Ngũgĩ's A Grain of Wheat (L140), Dickens' Oliver Twist in passages and a closing summary (L135, L145, L150), plus non-fiction on tourism, big African cities and Malawi (L139, L144, L149). Every text is pre-taught with a glossed vocabulary list.
- **Writing spine:** narrative vs descriptive compositions (L106–108) and formal-language newspaper articles, including on social issues (L114–115). No letter-writing lesson this term — letters were Terms 1–2 work.

## Honest negatives

- No year, ISBN or imprint is printed (metadata only, as above).
- The famous novels are read as summaries (in the book's own words) rather than as authors' text, except for the Oliver Twist passages; pupils meet plots and themes, not the novels themselves.
- Two small slips stand in the printed book and are recorded here rather than silently corrected: L102's overview once mislabels the had-form while teaching the past perfect, and L148's printed simple-future rule includes an extra "be". Neither changes what the lessons' tables and exercises actually practise.
- There is no conditional-sentence, passive-voice or formal-letter lesson in JSS 2 Term 3 either, although BECE (per Term 2, Lesson 86) tests conditionals and passive forms — a real gap between this series' teaching and its own stated examination list.
- No Shakespeare text appears this term (drama was Terms 1–2), and there is no pronunciation lesson in Term 3.

## Citation check

**15/15 anchors passed** against the actual PDF text. Anchors include: PDF p. 1 "STRICTLY NOT FOR SALE"; p. 2 "Alpha Osman Timbo"; p. 4 contents "Lesson 101: Verb Tenses"; p. 7 "past continuous tense"; p. 37 "Half of a Yellow Sun"; p. 39 "Things Fall Apart"; p. 56 "Purple Hibiscus"; p. 58 "So Long a Letter"; p. 76 "Direct and Indirect Speech"; p. 84 "A Grain of Wheat"; p. 86 "simple sentence"; p. 95 "complex sentence"; p. 101 "Malawi"; p. 105 "Answer Key"; p. 130 "GOVERNMENT OF SIERRA LEONE".

Sierra Leone English — JSS 3 and SSS 1 Pupil Handbooks. Six books read whole, page by page, 2026-11-03. All summaries below are my own; reading texts are named and summarised in a line at most, never transcribed. Page references give the printed page first and the PDF page where an anchor was checked. One cross-book fact the books themselves show: in Junior Secondary the subject is called **Language Arts** (covers, contents and lesson headers all say so, class JSS 3, codes PHL-09-…), and in Senior Secondary the same subject is called **English Language** (covers say “English Language”, class SSS 1, codes PHL1-L…). The exam target changes with it: JSS books prepare pupils for the BECE; SSS books prepare pupils for the WASSCE.

# Sierra Leone — Language Arts JSS3 Pupil Handbook, Term 1 — FULLY READ + CITATION CHECK PASSED 2026-11-03

## The book itself

- Title as printed on the cover: “Pupils’ handbook for JSS — Language ARTS — JSS 3 — Term 1”, under the “Free Quality School Education” banner, with “STRICTLY NOT FOR SALE”.
- Issuing authority as printed: Ministry of Basic and Senior Secondary Education (cover and foreword). The foreword is signed “Mr. Alpha Osman Timbo, Minister of Basic and Senior Secondary Education” and thanks the Department for International Development (DFID). The introduction, in older wording, says the activities meet requirements established by the “Ministry of Education, Science and Technology”.
- Year: no publication year is printed on the cover or in the front matter of the book itself. (File metadata, not printed in the book: created July 2018, modified January 2019.)
- PDF pages: 158. Printed pagination: printed page = PDF page − 3 throughout the numbered run (contents pages are printed 1–2, introduction printed 3, lessons printed 4–110, Answer Key printed 111–152). PDF pp. 156–157 are blank; PDF p. 158 is the back cover (“Government of Sierra Leone / Funded by / In partnership with / Strictly not for sale” — the funder/partner marks there are logos, not readable text).
- Form: a practice-activity handbook, not a reading textbook. Every lesson has the same frame: Lesson Title, Theme, Practice Activity code (PHL-09-001 to PHL-09-050), Class JSS 3, Learning Outcomes, an Overview that teaches the point, then numbered Practice Activities to be written in the pupil’s own exercise book (“DO NOT WRITE IN THE BOOKS” — printed books are to last 3 years). An Answer Key for all 50 lessons closes the book.
- Contents: Lessons 1–50, continuing the JSS numbering from Terms 1–2 of JSS 1–2 (the year’s JSS 3 run is Lessons 1–150 across the three terms).

## Map of the term — Lessons 1–50 in order (printed start page; what each teaches)

- L1 (p.4) Oral Narrative (Self-Introduction) — Speaking: what a good self-introduction contains (name, age, home town, family, schooling, hobbies, future plans) and delivering one fluently.
- L2 (p.6) Fluent Reading Exercises — Reading/Speaking: intonation (rise and fall of the voice) and word/sentence stress in reading aloud.
- L3 (p.7) Revision of the Eight Parts of Speech — Grammar: naming and identifying noun, pronoun, verb, adjective, adverb, preposition, conjunction, interjection in sentences.
- L4 (p.9) Nouns — Grammar: concrete nouns (known through the senses) vs abstract nouns (ideas such as peace, health, wisdom).
- L5 (p.11) Reading Comprehension — Reading/Speaking: voice modulation in oral reading; story “Brima’s Bad Idea” (a schoolboy is drawn into bad behaviour after a principal’s discipline talk; plot only) with comprehension questions.
- L6 (p.13) Reading Comprehension from a Selected Passage — Reading: literal vs inferential questions; essay-passage “Buried in Trash” on litter.
- L7 (p.15) Reading Comprehension from a Selected Passage (Continuation) — Reading: recalling facts, inferring, “reading between the lines”; story “Domoso’s Decision” (a village elder’s decision after evening prayers; plot only).
- L8 (p.17) Consolidation — Writing: five punctuation marks (full stop, comma, question mark, exclamation mark, quotation marks/inverted commas) and a punctuated composition.
- L9 (p.19) Consolidation (Continuation) — Writing: the colon and the exclamation mark.
- L10 (p.21) Spelling Drills — Writing: five spelling rules, starting with “i before e except after c”; BECE marks are deducted for misspelling.
- L11 (p.23) Revise Adjectives — Grammar: types of adjectives (descriptive/quality and others) and correct use.
- L12 (p.25) Verb Tenses — Grammar: simple present for facts, repeated actions, customs and habits.
- L13 (p.27) Poetry — Reading: literary devices (rhythm, rhyme, alliteration, rhetorical questions; metaphor, simile, personification) worked on excerpts from “The Blind Boy” by Colley Cibber (a blind boy asks sighted people what light is; plot only).
- L14 (p.29) Poetry (Continuation) — Reading/Writing: simile, metaphor, personification defined and practised; short poem “The Radio” by Emman T. Anamatemfiok (the radio personified as a talkative woman/bee; plot only), credited in the book to a Macmillan Sierra Leone pupils’ book of 2006.
- L15 (p.31) Poetry (Continuation) — Reading: interpreting a poem in your own words, finding its theme; the poem “Drought” by Denys Lefebvre (heat-parched land and suffering oxen; plot only).
- L16 (p.33) Revise the Types of Prose — Reading: narrative, descriptive and explanatory prose; fiction vs non-fiction; character, setting, plot, theme.
- L17 (p.35) Revision of the Prose Examination Requirements for BECE — Reading: how BECE Language Arts is built (English Language: grammar, reading, writing; Literature: drama, poetry, prose) and the two set prose choices named here, “So Long a Letter” and “Kossoh Town Boy”.
- L18 (p.37) Revision of Prose Examination Strategies for BECE — Reading: choose one of the two set prose texts and answer 15 short, direct questions on it.
- L19 (p.40) Essay Writing — Writing: features of an essay; descriptive, narrative and other essay types; plan before writing, edit after.
- L20 (p.42) Developing Paragraphs in Essay Writing — Writing: one idea per paragraph, main idea/topic sentence, indenting or spacing, no numbering of paragraphs.
- L21 (p.44) Verb Tenses — Grammar: past continuous (a longer action in progress, interrupted by a shorter simple-past action), helping verbs.
- L22 (p.46) Verb Tense (Continuation) — Grammar: past perfect (had + past participle) for the earlier of two past actions.
- L23 (p.48) Informal Letters (Revision) — Writing: features of a letter to family/friends (address, date, greeting, body, closing, name); chatty tone, contractions allowed.
- L24 (p.50) Informal Letters (Continuation) — Writing: outlining and developing an informal letter (school, holidays, celebrations, news, requests).
- L25 (p.52) Reading Comprehension — Reading: summarising a passage in your own words and building a word map for new words; passage “Machines Have Changed Our Lives” (from human muscle power to steam, petrol and mass mechanisation; plot only).
- L26 (p.54) The Simple Past Tense — Grammar: regular formation with -d/-ed and its spelling rules (y → i + ed; final e + d; doubling).
- L27 (p.56) The Simple Past Tense (Continuation) — Grammar: review, plus time markers such as yesterday, last week, last year.
- L28 (p.58) Features of an Article for Publication — Writing: what an article is; school magazine vs local newspaper audiences and topics; the practice text is a real newspaper article, “Improving Hygiene in Our Society” by Mustapha Sesay, headed Standard Times, Monday February 12, 2018 (hand-washing and safe water storage cut diarrhoeal deaths; plot only), whose features pupils identify.
- L29 (p.60) Article for Publication — Introduction, Body and Conclusion — Writing: title, author line and three paragraphs for a school-magazine article.
- L30 (p.62) Article for Publication — Edit Final Draft — Writing: editing and writing a newspaper article; the writer must stay calm and polite however strong the issue.
- L31 (p.64) Reading and Comprehension — Reading: explaining a passage in your own words; context clues for new words; an untitled extract describing night in an African bush town (moonlight dancing, log-fire light, fire warnings; plot only).
- L32 (p.67) Creative Writing — Writing: the five features of a story (setting, characters, plot, climax, outcome); outline for a story ending with the set line about a friendship ending.
- L33 (p.69) Creative Writing (Continuation) — Writing: composing a full short story in the proper format.
- L34 (p.71) Pronouns — Grammar: the types of pronouns, singular/plural, subject vs object personal pronouns.
- L35 (p.75) Pronouns (Continuation) — Grammar: using pronouns and replacing nouns with pronouns; the book’s list of eight pronoun types.
- L36 (p.78) The Present Continuous Tense — Grammar: am/is/are + verb-ing for actions happening now, with time expressions such as now, at the moment.
- L37 (p.80) Reading Strategies — Reading: fluent, purposeful reading aloud; interpreting messages and morals; extract “Preparing for School” from “Kossoh Town Boy”, identified in the lesson as the boyhood memoir of Sierra Leonean surgeon Robert Benjamin Ageh Wellesley Cole.
- L38 (p.82) How to Answer Comprehension Questions about a Text — Reading: BECE technique — read questions and text carefully, find answers in the text, use own words where required.
- L39 (p.84) Question Tags — Grammar: positive statement takes a negative tag (verb + not + pronoun + ?).
- L40 (p.86) Question Tags (Continuation) — Grammar: negative statement takes a positive tag; tags for confirmation and emphasis.
- L41 (p.88) Poetry — Reading: BECE poetry revision; the three selected poems are named as “The Blind Boy” by Colley Cibber, “Drought” by Denys Lefebvre and “The Village Schoolmaster” by Oliver Goldsmith; the lesson revises “The Blind Boy” (five stanzas, monologue, the boy born blind who longs for sight).
- L42 (p.91) Poetry (Continuation) — Reading: finding the theme of a poem (theme vs subject); worked on “Abiku” by John Pepper Clark (the Yoruba child who is born, dies and returns; plot only).
- L43 (p.94) Comparative Adjectives — Grammar: comparative for two, superlative for a group; -er/-est and irregular forms.
- L44 (p.97) Comparative Adverbs — Grammar: comparative and superlative adverbs (more/most + adverb; -er/-est where possible).
- L45 (p.99) Spelling and Dictation — Reading/Speaking/Writing: prefixes and suffixes (im-, un-, dis-, anti-, re-, ex-) and how they change word meaning; dictation practice.
- L46 (p.102) Semi-Formal Letter — Writing: features of a polite letter to an older relative; outline and paragraphs.
- L47 (p.104) Semi-Formal Letter (Continuation) — Writing: writing a semi-formal letter to a teacher; between informal and formal in tone.
- L48 (p.106) Semi-Formal Letter (Continuation) — Writing: a semi-formal letter of request to a parent or guardian (e.g. school fees, permission for a class trip).
- L49 (p.107) Direct and Indirect Speech — Grammar: direct speech = the speaker’s exact words in inverted commas; indirect/reported speech reports them without inverted commas.
- L50 (p.109) Direct and Indirect Speech (Continuation) — Grammar: building both forms, including the back-shift and time-word changes (e.g. yesterday → the day before).
- Answer Key (p.111) — worked answers and example letters/stories for all 50 lessons.

## Grammar and vocabulary spine of the book

Grammar, in teaching order: eight parts of speech (L3, revised in L34–35 pronouns and L11/L43–44 adjectives/adverbs of comparison) → nouns concrete/abstract (L4) → tense system built piece by piece: simple present (L12), past continuous (L21), past perfect (L22), simple past with full spelling rules (L26–27), present continuous (L36) → question tags, positive and negative statements (L39–40) → comparative/superlative adjectives and adverbs (L43–44) → direct and indirect speech (L49–50). Language mechanics run alongside: punctuation in two blocks (L8–9, including colon and quotation marks), spelling rules and dictation (L10, L45), prefixes/suffixes (L45). Vocabulary is not taught as themed word lists in this book; it grows out of the reading passages (word maps, L25; context clues, L31) and the set literature. Writing spine: essay and paragraph structure (L19–20) → the three letter registers in ascending formality across the year — informal (L23–24) and semi-formal (L46–48) here, formal held for Term 2 — article for publication in three stages (L28–30), creative story writing (L32–33). Literature spine: prose types and the BECE set prose books (L16–18, L37), poetry devices and the three BECE set poems (L13–15, L41–42).

## Honest negatives — JSS 3 Term 1

- No publication year, ISBN or copyright page is printed anywhere in the book; the only dates inside are dates within example letters and the 12 February 2018 date on the example newspaper article.
- The book contains no full set text: “So Long a Letter” is named as a BECE prose option but no extract from it is printed in this term; “Kossoh Town Boy” appears only as the short “Preparing for School” extract (L37) plus exam guidance.
- L17’s overview misprints the exam name once as “BEBE” for BECE; the rest of the book says BECE.
- There is no separate vocabulary-list lesson and no phonics/pronunciation lesson in this term; oral work is self-introduction, fluent reading and voice modulation only.
- Two PDF pages before the back cover (PDF pp. 156–157) are blank.

## Citation check — JSS 3 Term 1

16 anchors checked against the PDF text; 16/16 passed.

- Cover, PDF p.1 (unnumbered): “JSS 3”, “Term 1”, “STRICTLY NOT FOR SALE”.
- Foreword, PDF p.2: “Basic Education Certificate Examination”; signed “Alpha Osman Timbo”.
- Contents, PDF p.4 (printed 1): “Lesson 1: Oral Narrative (Self-Introduction)” listed at p.4.
- L1 start, PDF p.7 (printed 4): code “PHL-09-001”.
- L5 story, PDF p.14 (printed 11): “Brima” (story “Brima’s Bad Idea”).
- L6 passage, PDF p.16 (printed 13): “Buried in Trash”.
- L13 poem, PDF p.30 (printed 27): “The Blind Boy”.
- L14 poem, PDF p.32 (printed 29): “The Radio”.
- L15 poem, PDF p.34 (printed 31): “Drought”.

# Sierra Leone — Language Arts JSS3 Pupil Handbook, Term 2 — FULLY READ + CITATION CHECK PASSED 2026-11-03

## The book itself

- Title as printed on the cover: “Pupils’ handbook for JSS — Language ARTS — JSS 3 — Term 2”, “Free Quality School Education” banner, “STRICTLY NOT FOR SALE”.
- Issuing authority as printed: Ministry of Basic and Senior Secondary Education; foreword signed “Mr. Alpha Osman Timbo, Minister of Basic and Senior Secondary Education”, thanking DFID. The introduction again uses the older “Ministry of Education, Science and Technology” wording.
- Year: no publication year printed in the book itself. (File metadata only: created July 2018, modified January 2019.)

Printed pagination: printed page = PDF page − 5 (the two contents pages and the notice/foreword pages are unnumbered in the printed run; the introduction is printed 1, lessons run printed 2–103, Answer Key printed 104–138). PDF pp. 144–145 are blank; PDF p. 146 is the back cover (Government of Sierra Leone / Funded by / In partnership with / Strictly not for sale — logos, not text).
- Form: same handbook frame as Term 1 — Lesson Title, Theme, code PHL-09-051 to PHL-09-100, Learning Outcomes, Overview, Practice Activities, and a closing Answer Key. Lesson numbering continues the year (51–100 of 150).

## Map of the term — Lessons 51–100 in order (printed start page; what each teaches)

- L51 (p.2) Continuous Reading of Prose — Reading: reading aloud slowly, clearly and with expression; stress and intonation; a story about Gibril, a boy given an empty wooden box by his father who turns it into a wheeled carrier (plot only).
- L52 (p.3) Continuous Reading of Prose (Continuation) — Reading: answering comprehension questions (read questions first, skim for main idea, re-read carefully); a folk tale in which a hare tricks an elephant over a borrowed stomach and is found out (plot only), with true/false and vocabulary questions.
- L53 (p.5) Revision of Adverbs — Grammar: adverbs of manner, place and time and their normal positions.
- L54 (p.7) Oral Descriptive Composition — Speaking/Writing: describing a person aloud so listeners can picture them.
- L55 (p.8) Phrasal Verbs — Grammar: verb + preposition/adverb acting as one word; meaning changes with the particle (e.g. the two senses of “put out”).
- L56 (p.10) Speech Writing — Speaking/Writing: planning and writing a speech for an occasion, e.g. introducing a chairman; welcome addresses and toasts.
- L57 (p.12) Oral Presentation — Writing/Speaking: drafting a speech and delivering it loudly, clearly and with confidence.
- L58 (p.14) Revision of Direct and Indirect Speech — Grammar: changing direct (quoted, inverted commas) into indirect (reported) speech, with pronoun and tense changes.
- L59 (p.16) How to Use ‘Wh’ Questions — Reading/Writing: who, whom, whose, which, what, why, when, where and how; worked on an extract about Ekwefi and Okonkwo from Chinua Achebe’s “Things Fall Apart” (a mother loses child after child to an ogbanje, a child who dies and returns; plot only — the extract is named and answered on in the lesson, and the book itself is named in the Answer Key).
- L60 (p.18) Listening to Local or International Radio Programmes — Listening: listening for, retelling and discussing the main points of a radio programme.
- L61 (p.19) Formal Letters — Writing: features of an official letter to a person in authority (chairman of a board, managers, councillors, chiefs, ministers); complaint, request, business and application letters.
- L62 (p.21) Formal Letters — Writing: writing formal letters for a range of purposes; formal style, no slang or contractions, respectful tone, both addresses.
- L63 (p.23) Parts of Speech — Grammar: the eight parts of speech identified and used, with a full definition/example table.
- L64 (p.25) Parts of Speech — Grammar: nouns, verbs and adjectives by function; the same word can be different parts of speech in different sentences (rain as noun, verb and adjective).
- L65 (p.26) Listening, Spelling and Dictation — Listening/Writing: learning new words by pronouncing, spelling and defining them; writing a dictated passage correctly.
- L66 (p.28) Vocabulary (Word Substitution) — Reading: replacing a word with an equivalent (noun for noun, etc.) without changing meaning.
- L67 (p.31) Adverbs (Position of Adverbs) — Grammar: adverbs at the beginning, middle or end of a sentence, and how position can change meaning.
- L68 (p.33) Idiomatic Expressions — Grammar: an idiom’s meaning differs from its words; explaining and using simple idioms.
- L69 (p.35) Comprehension — Writing: answering BECE objective (multiple-choice a–d) questions on a passage; only one option is correct.
- L70 (p.38) Composition — Writing: a narrative composition enlivened with direct speech and idiomatic expressions.
- L71 (p.40) Revision of Question Tags — Grammar: negative statement → positive tag; positive statement → negative tag; tags for confirmation and emphasis.
- L72 (p.42) Present Perfect Tense — Grammar: have/has + past participle; past connected to present; “for” and “since”.
- L73 (p.44) Prepositions — Grammar: between (two) vs among (more than two); to, through, inside, within.
- L74 (p.46) Poetry — Reading: BECE poetry requirements — answer all ten short questions at 2 marks each; the four studied poems are a sonnet (“Drought”), a descriptive poem (“The Village Schoolmaster”) and two narrative poems (“Abiku”, “The Blind Boy”); pupils must know poets, themes, devices, settings, subject matter and word meanings.
- L75 (p.50) Poetry — Reading: theme and content of “The Village Schoolmaster” by Oliver Goldsmith — part of the longer poem “The Deserted Village” (18th-century England), narrated by a former pupil describing his old teacher (plot only).
- L76 (p.52) Sentence Structure — Writing: basic sentence patterns; subject, predicate, object and complement.
- L77 (p.54) Changing Sentences from Active to Passive Voice — Grammar: active (subject does the action) vs passive (subject receives it), and converting between them.
- L78 (p.56) Revision of Verb Tenses — Grammar: simple present revised (facts, routines; Freetown as capital used as a fact example).
- L79 (p.58) Simple Future and Future Continuous Tenses — Grammar: will/shall + verb for the simple future (shall only with I/we); will/shall be + verb-ing for future continuous.
- L80 (p.60) Reading and Understanding Prose — Reading: mood/atmosphere in prose (happiness, anger, disappointment…); short passages including “Our Old House” (an unhappy memory of a drab, noisy house at Kingtom; plot only) answered with multiple-choice questions.
- L81 (p.62) Revision of Informal Letters — Writing: revising the features of informal letters.
- L82 (p.64) Informal Letters — Writing: writing a full three-paragraph informal letter with all features.
- L83 (p.66) The Future Perfect Tense — Grammar: will/shall have + past participle for an action completed by a future point.
- L84 (p.68) Adjectives — Grammar: descriptive adjectives modifying nouns and pronouns, felt through the five senses.
- L85 (p.70) Phrasal Verbs — Grammar: more phrasal verbs with meanings explained (e.g. break down, calm down) and used in sentences.
- L86 (p.72) Book Review — Writing: reviewing a novel — title, author, author’s nationality, publisher, plot summary without spoiling, and opinion.
- L87 (p.74) The Use of Articles — Grammar: a, an (indefinite) and the (definite), with countable and uncountable nouns.
- L88 (p.76) Conjunctions — Grammar: coordinating, subordinating and correlative conjunctions; correlatives work in pairs and govern verb agreement.
- L89 (p.78) Requirements for Drama for BECE — Reading: BECE drama has one prescribed text, always a Shakespearean play, read whole; context and objective questions on figures of speech, characters, mood, setting and main ideas.
- L90 (p.81) Drama: Answering Context Questions — Reading: answering context questions on an excerpt; worked on “The Merchant of Venice” (the opening of Act 1, Scene 1 is the Term 2 worked excerpt — Antonio says he does not know why he is sad; plot only).
- L91 (p.84) Revision of Punctuation Marks — Grammar: punctuation across sentence and essay writing, starting with the comma’s uses.
- L92 (p.86) Listening Comprehension — Listening/Speaking: listening attentively and summarising the main points of a passage or short story.
- L93 (p.88) Avoiding Double Negatives — Grammar: two negatives (no, not, never, none) cancel into a positive and confuse; correct them.
- L94 (p.90) Possessive Nouns — Grammar: singular possessive with apostrophe + s (Mr Kamara’s house).
- L95 (p.92) Possessive Nouns and Pronouns — Grammar: possessive pronouns (mine, yours, his, hers, ours, theirs) never take an apostrophe, unlike possessive nouns.
- L96 (p.94) Descriptive Essay — Writing: a descriptive essay about your school in at least three paragraphs — sensory details, introduction/body/conclusion, figurative language.
- L97 (p.96) Conditional Tenses — Grammar: the four “if” conditionals, from general truths (if + present, present) to future possibility (if + present, future).
- L98 (p.98) Figures of Speech — Reading: irony, euphemism and repetition, after the earlier simile, metaphor and personification.
- L99 (p.100) Narrative Composition in the Simple Past Tense — Writing/Grammar: outlining a narrative composition on a given topic, in the simple past.
- L100 (p.102) Narrative Composition — Writing/Grammar: writing the full narrative composition in the simple past (regular -d/-ed; irregular verbs unchanged or changed in form).
- Answer Key (p.104) — answers and example letters, speeches, essays and compositions for all 50 lessons.

## Grammar and vocabulary spine of the book

Grammar: parts of speech revisited twice (L63–64) and then drilled class by class — adverbs and their position (L53, L67), prepositions between/among and place prepositions (L73), articles with countability (L87), conjunctions including correlatives (L88), possessive nouns vs possessive pronouns and the apostrophe (L94–95), avoiding double negatives (L93). The tense line completes the JSS system: reported speech revised (L58), question tags revised (L71), present perfect (L72), simple present revised (L78), simple future + future continuous (L79), future perfect (L83), conditionals (L97), active/passive voice (L77), sentence structure — subject, predicate, object, complement (L76). Vocabulary: phrasal verbs in two lessons (L55, L85), idioms (L68, then used in composition L70), word substitution/synonymy (L66), spelling through dictation (L65). Writing/oral spine: speech writing and delivery (L56–57), formal letters (L61–62) completing the register ladder begun with informal/semi-formal letters, informal letters consolidated (L81–82), book review (L86), descriptive essay (L96), narrative composition in the past (L99–100). Literature spine is openly exam-shaped: BECE poetry requirements and “The Village Schoolmaster” (L74–75), BECE drama requirements and “The Merchant of Venice” context questions (L89–90), prose mood (L80), figures of speech (L98).

## Honest negatives — JSS 3 Term 2

- No publication year, ISBN or copyright page is printed in the book.
- The “Things Fall Apart” extract in L59 is not credited with its title or author inside the lesson overview as extracted — Achebe and the title surface in the Answer Key material; a pupil meeting the passage cold is not told on the lesson page whose book it is.
- No new letter register is left untaught after this term, but there is no dedicated summary-writing lesson in Term 2 (summary appears only inside listening, L92) and no phonics/pronunciation lesson.
- The four BECE poems are studied through questions and guidance; the full text of “The Village Schoolmaster” is not printed in this book (an extract-based lesson, L75).
- Two PDF pages before the back cover (PDF pp. 144–145) are blank.

# Sierra Leone — Language Arts JSS3 Pupil Handbook, Term 3 — FULLY READ + CITATION CHECK PASSED 2026-11-03

## The book itself

- Title as printed on the cover: “Pupils’ handbook for JSS — Language ARTS — JSS 3 — Term 3”, “Free Quality School Education” banner, “STRICTLY NOT FOR SALE”.
- Issuing authority as printed: Ministry of Basic and Senior Secondary Education; foreword signed “Mr. Alpha Osman Timbo, Minister of Basic and Senior Secondary Education”, thanking DFID. The introduction again uses the older “Ministry of Education, Science and Technology” wording.
- Year: no publication year printed in the book itself. (File metadata only: created July 2018, modified January 2019.)
- PDF pages: 146. Printed pagination: printed page = PDF page − 5 (introduction printed 1, lessons printed 2–108, Answer Key printed 109–137). PDF pp. 143–145 are blank; PDF p. 146 is the back cover (Government of Sierra Leone / Funded by / In partnership with / Strictly not for sale — logos, not text).
- Form: same handbook frame — Lesson Title, Theme, code PHL-09-101 to PHL-09-150, Learning Outcomes, Overview, Practice Activities, closing Answer Key. This term completes the JSS 3 year (Lessons 101–150 of 150) and is the most openly revision- and BECE-facing of the three JSS 3 books.

## Map of the term — Lessons 101–150 in order (printed start page; what each teaches)

- L101 (p.2) Features of a Hero or Villain — Reading: analysing characters — protagonist, antagonist, hero, villain, foil; worked on a “Merchant of Venice” courtroom extract in which Gratiano attacks Shylock (plot only), with multiple-choice and evidence questions.
- L102 (p.5) Revision of Verb Forms — Infinitive, Simple Past Tense and Past Participle — Grammar: the three verb forms; regular -ed forms vs irregular forms to be memorised.
- L103 (p.7) Revision of the Simple Form of the Three Simple Tenses — Grammar: switching between simple present, simple past and simple future.
- L104 (p.10) Descriptive Composition — Writing: describing an admirable person, in the simple present, through the five senses.
- L105 (p.11) Descriptive Composition (Continuation) — Writing: describing a place; how person-description and place-description differ.
- L106 (p.13) Poetry — Reading: poetic technique — diction, rhythm, rhyme and rhyme scheme, illustrated from “Drought” (its opening rhyme scheme is worked line by line).
- L107 (p.15) Poetry — Reading: repetition, hyperbole, simile, metaphor and synecdoche in a prescribed poem, illustrated from “Abiku” (repetition for the tired mother; the swordfish-beak simile for the child’s back; plot only).
- L108 (p.17) The Conditional Tense — Grammar: why it is called conditional; the four main conditional types and when each is used; the “if” clause as subordinate clause.
- L109 (p.19) Conditional Tense — Grammar: conditionals for unlikely past events and for habitual actions; “if + present + present” for general truths (boiling water example).
- L110 (p.21) Oral Presentation (a Debate) — Listening/Speaking: debating a motion, for and against; public-speaking technique.
- L111 (p.23) Idioms — Grammar: interpreting idioms (the book’s example: “raining cats and dogs” = raining heavily) and using them in writing.
- L112 (p.26) Narrative Composition — Writing: a narrative composition built on a given idiomatic expression, which must be used at the end of the composition.
- L113 (p.28) Phrasal Verbs — Grammar: finding the meaning of phrasal verbs (e.g. “bring up” = mention, or raise a child) and using them in writing.
- L114 (p.30) Shakespearean Text — Reading: “The Merchant of Venice” by William Shakespeare — the book summarises the plot (Antonio the merchant, his friend Bassanio who loves Portia, and the moneylender Shylock; plot only) and asks for understanding and opinions on characters and events.
- L115 (p.33) Oral Description of an Incident — Writing/Speaking: describing an incident aloud (robbery, fire, accident, argument…) with adjectives, so listeners want the ending.
- L116 (p.34) The Use of “Little”, “a Little”, “Few”, “a Few” and the Articles “a”, “an” and “the” — Grammar: quantity words with countable/uncountable nouns, and indefinite vs definite articles.
- L117 (p.37) Poetry — Reading: explaining a set poem in your own words; a stanza-by-stanza walk through “The Blind Boy” (the boy makes his own day and night by sleeping and playing; plot only).
- L118 (p.40) Prose Text — Reading: relating a prose text’s events to your own experience; arguing for the characters you like and dislike, with reasons.
- L119 (p.42) The Active and Passive Voices — Grammar: converting active sentences to passive and recognising when the doer is unknown.
- L120 (p.44) Expository Writing — Giving Instructions — Writing: an expository piece explaining how to prepare a dish or play a game, step by step.
- L121 (p.46) Conditional Tenses — Grammar: conditionals for what could or should have happened in the past but did not, and for likely, specific situations.
- L122 (p.48) Conditional Tenses (Continuation) — Grammar: identifying conditional clauses; BECE-style questions asking for the correct interpretation of a conditional sentence.
- L123 (p.50) Narrative Composition — Writing: a narrative composition using the conditional pattern “if + simple past + present conditional” for unlikely or impossible situations.
- L124 (p.52) Prose — Reading: answering BECE prose questions on a set text — short, direct answers; question topics include the author, and prose type (epistolary, autobiographical…).
- L125 (p.54) Translating Shakespearean English into Modern English — Reading: why Shakespeare is hard (dead words, borrowed words such as “signior”); translating passages into today’s English.
- L126 (p.56) Parts of Speech — Grammar: recalling all eight parts of speech and the function of each.
- L127 (p.59) Concrete and Abstract Nouns — Grammar: concrete vs abstract nouns, the types of abstract nouns, and proper nouns with capital letters (Freetown example).
- L128 (p.61) Expository Writing in a Letter — Writing: a letter to a friend describing a process (making soap or a basket, preparing a dish, playing a game).
- L129 (p.63) Oral Work on Exposition — Listening/Speaking: describing a process aloud to a classmate (sewing a dress, making a basket, planting a garden); features of expository writing.
- L130 (p.65) Revise Poetry — Reading: recalling “The Village Schoolmaster” (part of “The Deserted Village”, Oliver Goldsmith, 18th-century England; one stanza, humorous, narrated by a former pupil; plot only) and its literary devices.
- L131 (p.67) Parts of Speech — Pronouns — Grammar: pronoun types and functions; personal vs possessive pronouns; pronouns replacing repeated nouns.
- L132 (p.69) The Case of the Pronoun — Grammar: subject pronouns (doers) vs object pronouns (receivers) and choosing the right case.
- L133 (p.71) Indefinite Pronouns — Grammar: pronouns that do not name a specific person or thing (anybody, everyone, each, either…), singular and plural lists.
- L134 (p.74) Revision of Semi-Formal Letters — Writing: informal vs semi-formal features compared; writing a semi-formal letter to a parent, adult relative or adult friend.
- L135 (p.76) Reading Comprehension — Reading: improving reading and growing vocabulary from a passage; the passage is “Kangaroos” (a factual animal passage with pre-taught vocabulary such as hind, scarce, roam; plot only).
- L136 (p.78) Parts of Speech — Conjunctions and Interjections — Grammar: conjunctions joining words, phrases and clauses (the worked example uses animals gathering food with paws and forearms); interjections defined by function.
- L137 (p.80) Adjectives — Grammar: the types of adjectives (descriptive, quantitative/numerical, demonstrative, possessive, interrogative) and their functions.
- L138 (p.82) Reading Comprehension — Reading: summarising and recognising the writer’s point of view (first vs third person); worked on “The Lion Cub and the Egg” (Fadgo the curious lion cub takes a falcon’s egg home and is sent back to return it; plot only).
- L139 (p.84) Descriptive Composition — Describing a Person — Writing: appearance and behaviour, using adjectives to awaken the reader’s imagination.
- L140 (p.86) Descriptive Composition — Describing a Place — Writing: describing a visited place for the BECE so readers see it in their mind’s eye.
- L141 (p.88) Book Review — Writing: why reading beyond textbooks matters; summarising a book; the lesson names “So Long a Letter” as teaching about the rights of women and “The Merchant of Venice” as treating love, friendship, greed and revenge.
- L142 (p.90) Book Review (Continuation) — Writing: writing a full review — title, author, author’s nationality, publisher, year, contents without giving away the plot, and your attitude to a character or the story.
- L143 (p.92) Question Tags — Grammar: adding tags to positive and negative statements; statements without auxiliaries take “do” in the tag.
- L144 (p.95) Revision of Comparative and Superlative Adjectives — Grammar: -er/-est forms vs more/most forms vs irregular changes.
- L145 (p.97) Reading Comprehension — Reading: answering “Wh” questions after BECE reading; scanning as the strategy (read the questions first, hunt key words).
- L146 (p.99) Adverbs — Grammar: adverb functions and types (time, place…); telling adverbs from adjectives.
- L147 (p.101) Avoiding Double Negatives — Grammar: identifying and correcting double negatives; writing letters and essays without them.
- L148 (p.103) Prepositions — Grammar: the functions and types of prepositions, with a long example list (across, after, among, at, behind, beside…).
- L149 (p.105) Revision of Formal Letters — Writing: formal vs informal and semi-formal letters compared; who formal letters are written to.
- L150 (p.107) Revision of Formal Letters (Continuation) — Writing: developing paragraphs and writing a formal letter — two addresses, a heading, no contractions, formal language.
- Answer Key (p.109) — answers and example compositions/letters for all 50 lessons.

## Grammar and vocabulary spine of the book

This is a consolidation term. Grammar: verb forms and the three simple tenses revised first (L102–103), then the year’s largest single topic — conditionals in four lessons plus a conditional narrative (L108–109, L121–123) — active/passive voice (L119), quantity words and articles (L116), and a full parts-of-speech sweep in teaching order: all eight recalled (L126), concrete/abstract nouns (L127), pronouns, case and indefinite pronouns (L131–133), conjunctions and interjections (L136), adjectives and their comparison (L137, L144), adverbs (L146), double negatives (L147), prepositions (L148), question tags (L143), idioms and phrasal verbs (L111, L113). Writing: descriptive composition in three passes (person, place, person/place for the exam — L104–105, L139–140), narrative composition twice (L112, L123), exposition in three modes (instructions, letter, oral — L120, L128–129), book review (L141–142), debate (L110), oral incident description (L115), and the letter ladder closed by revising semi-formal (L134) and formal (L149–150) letters. Literature: character analysis (L101), the Shakespeare set play in three lessons (L114, L125, plus the L101 extract), and one set poem per literature block — “Drought” technique (L106), “Abiku” devices (L107), “The Blind Boy” explained (L117), “The Village Schoolmaster” revised (L130) — with prose-exam technique at L118 and L124.

- There is no listening-comprehension lesson with a printed passage in this term (listening appears only inside the debate and oral-exposition lessons), and no new tense is introduced — every grammar lesson is revision or extension of JSS work.
