# Sierra Leone — English Language SSS1 Pupil Handbook, Term 1 — FULLY READ + CITATION CHECK PASSED 2026-11-03

## The book itself

- Title as printed on the cover: “Pupils’ Handbook for Senior Secondary — English Language — SSS I — Term I”, “Free Quality School Education” banner, “STRICTLY NOT FOR SALE”. Note the change the books themselves make at this level: the subject is now **English Language**, not Language Arts, the class is SSS 1, and the lesson codes change to PHL1-L001 onwards.
- Issuing authority as printed: Ministry of Basic and Senior Secondary Education. The foreword is signed “Mr. Alpha Osman Timbo, Minister of Basic and Senior Secondary Education”; it states that 516 lesson plans and 516 practice activities were produced across the senior secondary English and Mathematics programme, and thanks “our partners” (DFID is not named in the SSS foreword, unlike the JSS one).
- Year: no publication year printed in the book itself. (File metadata only: author field “Alison Morris Oswald”, created 1 August 2018, modified January 2019.)
- PDF pages: 126. Pagination: front matter is paginated in Roman numerals — foreword printed i (PDF p.2), “DO NOT WRITE IN THE BOOKS” notice printed ii (PDF p.3), contents printed iii–iv (PDF pp.4–5), introduction printed v (PDF p.6), and a WASSCE examiner-report section printed vi–viii (PDF pp.7–9, with PDF p.9 carrying only the numeral viii). Body: printed page = PDF page − 9; lessons printed 1–92, Answer Key printed 93–114. PDF pp.124–125 carry only the printed numerals 115 and 116 (otherwise blank); PDF p.126 is the back cover.
- Extra front matter unique to the SSS books: “Key Takeaways from Sierra Leone’s Performance in West African Senior School Certificate Examination — English Language”, drawn from WAEC examiner reports, listing common pupil errors and suggested fixes under listening and speaking (sound identification, stress, intonation), writing (misread questions, poor planning, expression errors, prepositions, punctuation, spelling), reading comprehension (literal vs inferential questions, vocabulary and figures of speech, summarising large chunks, word replacement/synonyms) and more. The whole SSS course is framed as fixing those WASSCE weaknesses.
- Form: same handbook frame — Lesson Title, Theme, code, Learning Outcomes, Overview, Practice Activities, Answer Key — but lessons are numbered across the SSS 1 year (1–140 over three terms) and every Overview is pitched at WASSCE, not BECE.
- Contents: Lessons 1–48.

## Map of the term — Lessons 1–48 in order (printed start page; what each teaches)

- L1 (p.1) Introducing Self and Others — Listening/Speaking: preparing and giving a spoken introduction of yourself and of family members, with correct tenses and third-person endings.
- L2 (p.2) Simple Present Tense — Grammar: facts, repeated actions, emotions and wishes; subject–verb agreement, adding -s/-es for he/she/it.
- L3 (p.4) Simple Present Tense — Grammar: the negative (do/does not; am/is/are not) and forming and answering questions.
- L4 (p.6) Dialogue — Reading: reading a dialogue between two friends, summarising it, and keeping opinion distinct from fact in a summary.
- L5 (p.8) Parts of Speech — Grammar: the 8 parts of speech, their functions, and identifying them by the job a word does in a sentence.
- L6 (p.10) Parts of Speech — Grammar: the same ground revised with a full definition/example table, and writing sentences using all eight.
- L7 (p.12) Reading Skills Development — Skimming — Reading: reading quickly for gist (title, headings, first lines); practised on a text about beach pollution (dogs, birds and visitors leaving waste on beaches; plot only).
- L8 (p.14) Reading Skills Development — Scanning — Reading: hunting specific information fast; skimming vs scanning.
- L9 (p.16) Vocabulary Development: Family and Home — Reading: WASSCE general vocabulary, family/home field; reading text “Families in Three Countries” (average households in Sierra Leone, the UK and Japan compared; plot only), meanings found by context and inference.
- L10 (p.18) Vocabulary Development: Family and Home — Writing: choosing best definitions and writing with family/home vocabulary.
- L11 (p.20) Punctuation — Terminal Stops — Grammar: full stop, question mark and exclamation mark as end punctuation, read with expression.
- L12 (p.21) Punctuation — Terminal Stops — Grammar: using the three terminal stops correctly in your own writing.
- L13 (p.23) Present Continuous Tense — Grammar: actions happening now; simple present vs present continuous; negatives without double negatives; verbs that rarely take the continuous; questions.
- L14 (p.25) Present Continuous Tense (future use) — Grammar: the same form used for planned future events, told apart by context.
- L15 (p.27) Reading Comprehension — Fiction — Reading: summarising and inferring; story “Home Alone” (Patrick, alone in the house for the first time on a rainy night, is frightened by noises that turn out to be the family goats sheltering from the rain; plot only).
- L16 (p.29) Reading Comprehension — Autobiography — Reading: autobiography vs biography; text “They Call Me Mama Lulu” (Lucy McEwan, nearly 100, looks back over a long, hard life; plot only).
- L17 (p.31) Informal Letter — Writing: features and format of a letter to friends/family (address, date, greeting, introduction, body, conclusion, closing, signature) and drafting from an outline.
- L18 (p.33) Informal Letter — To a Classmate — Writing: writing a complete informal letter with correct grammar, spelling and vocabulary.
- L19 (p.34) Simple Past Tense — Regular Verbs — Grammar: completed past actions; -ed formation; the practice text is “Kamara’s Phone” (a young man late for a job interview loses, searches for, and finally finds his phone at home; plot only).
- L20 (p.37) Simple Past — Irregular Verbs — Grammar: irregular past forms that do not take -ed, in statements, questions and negatives.
- L21 (p.39) Simple Past — Irregular Verbs — Grammar: more irregular-verb practice, negatives without double negatives.
- L22 (p.41) Past Continuous — Grammar: an action continuing over a past period; past continuous vs simple past; negatives and questions.
- L23 (p.44) Formal Letter — Request to a Person of Authority — Writing: features and format of a formal request letter; polite, concise, direct language, no contractions or colloquial language.
- L24 (p.46) Formal Letters — Request to a Person of Authority — Writing: writing a full request letter (examples: asking a principal for more books; inviting a community figure to teach the class).
- L25 (p.48) Comprehending a Listening Passage — Listening: the features of narrative text, then questions and a summary on the story “A Hard Lesson” (Charles, who does not revise, and Samuel, who studies daily, face the examinations; plot only).
- L26 (p.50) Consonant Sounds — Listening/Speaking: the 24 consonant sounds of English; 21 consonant letters, the semi-vowels y and w, voiced vs unvoiced; sounds in initial, medial and end position.
- L27 (p.52) Simple Past — Irregular Verbs — Grammar: simple past vs past participle of regular and irregular verbs.
- L28 (p.54) Comprehending a Listening Passage — Listening: summarising from memory and notes (no re-reading); passage “A Dreadful Journey” (a journey interrupted by a terrible accident; plot only).
- L29 (p.56) Punctuation: Comma, Semi-colon, Colon, Dash — Grammar: the uses of the four marks (lists, enclosing extra information, joining clauses, introducing).
- L30 (p.58) Punctuation: Comma, Semi-colon, Colon, Dash — Grammar: using the four marks correctly in your own text.
- L31 (p.59) Reading Skills Development: Summarising — Reading: how to summarise a speech (tone, mood, argument, order; note, outline, draft, check).
- L32 (p.61) Reading Skills Development: Summarising — Reading: summarising a report (title and introduction, topic sentences, no more than a quarter of the original length, paraphrase, no opinions of your own); the practice report is “Mr Bangara Addresses the School Environmental Club” (a University of Manchester visitor advises a school club on cleaning the school and involving the community; plot only).
- L33 (p.63) Vocabulary Development: Agriculture — Reading: WASSCE agriculture word list with definitions (habitat, organic, pesticides, sow, livestock, smallholding…) and a reading paragraph about an uncle’s smallholding.
- L34 (p.65) Vocabulary Development: Agriculture — Writing: writing a text using the full agriculture word set.
- L35 (p.66) Simple Future (going to) — Grammar: “going to” for definite plans and for conclusions about the immediate future; positive, negative and question forms; practice from a character’s diary for next week.
- L36 (p.68) Simple Future (will vs shall) — Grammar: will vs shall, in statements, questions and negatives without double negatives.
- L37 (p.70) Reading Comprehension — Science Fiction — Reading: predicting and inferring; story “Examinations in the Year 2099” (a pupil in “Senior Secondary 8” faces final examinations in a future school system; plot only).
- L38 (p.72) Reading Comprehension — Science Fiction — Reading: science fiction is set in the future but told largely in the present tense, with invented vocabulary; story “A Party in the Future” (after eight years away at school the narrator will go home, and a robot will clean the house for a celebration party; plot only).
- L39 (p.74) Creative Writing — Writing: planning a story from an outline — setting, characters (appearance and personality), plot/problem, point of view, tone and mood.
- L40 (p.76) Creative Writing — Writing: writing the full story from the outline, with correct grammar and spelling.
- L41 (p.77) Comprehending a Listening Passage — Listening: summarising a heard text (names, numbers, key points); worked example and practice text is a radio interview with athlete Aminata Charles (a 100-metres sprinter who trains early, began by winning school races, wants to run for Sierra Leone at the Olympics and to train as a nurse; plot only).
- L42 (p.79) Consonant Clusters — Listening/Speaking: two or more consonants together with no vowel between, in initial, medial and end position.
- L43 (p.81) Consonant Clusters — Listening/Speaking: more cluster practice, including finding clusters in a listening passage.
- L44 (p.82) Spelling — Writing: the five basic spelling rules, starting with “i before e except after c” and the “ei pronounced ay” exception; correcting misspelt words.
- L45 (p.84) Vocabulary Development: Fishing — Reading: WASSCE fishing word list with definitions (bait, to bite, to cast…) learned in context.
- L46 (p.86) Vocabulary Development: Fishing — Writing: completing and writing a text with fishing vocabulary; the Answer Key’s model text is “A Day in the Life of a Fisherman”.
- L47 (p.88) Singular and Plural Nouns — Grammar: what nouns are (subject/object; people, places, animals, things, states); regular singular/plural formation; articles with nouns.
- L48 (p.90) Nouns — Spelling Irregular Plural Nouns — Grammar: the spelling patterns of plurals, including irregular plurals and words ending -f/-fe, -s/-sh/-ch.
- Answer Key (p.93) — answers, example letters and model texts for all 48 lessons.

## Grammar and vocabulary spine of the book

Grammar: the tense ladder is rebuilt systematically for WASSCE — simple present (affirmative, negative, questions; L2–3) → present continuous and its future use (L13–14) → simple past, regular then three lessons of irregular verbs including past participles (L19–21, L27) → past continuous (L22) → simple future in both forms, going to and will/shall (L35–36). Around it: the 8 parts of speech twice (L5–6), punctuation in two pairs (terminal stops L11–12; comma, semi-colon, colon, dash L29–30), nouns singular/plural and irregular plurals closing the term (L47–48), and a standing rule repeated in lesson outcomes — form negatives “avoiding double negatives”. Phonology, new at this level: consonant sounds (L26) and consonant clusters (L42–43). Vocabulary is now organised as WASSCE general-vocabulary fields, each taught as a read-then-write pair: Family and Home (L9–10), Agriculture (L33–34), Fishing (L45–46). Reading spine: skimming, scanning, summarising speeches and reports (L7–8, L31–32), fiction, autobiography and science fiction as named genres (L15–16, L37–38), dialogue (L4), and listening comprehension of narrative and interview texts (L25, L28, L41). Writing spine: informal letter pair and formal request-letter pair (L17–18, L23–24), creative writing pair (L39–40), spelling rules (L44).

- One small production quirk: L41 is printed “LLesson 41” in the contents list (a doubled letter), while the lesson itself is normal.
- The listening passages are printed in full as reading texts in the handbook (the pupil reads them); there is no audio component in the book itself.
- No poetry or drama lesson appears in SSS 1 Term 1 — literature genres are limited to fiction, autobiography and science fiction prose.

# Sierra Leone — English Language SSS1 Pupil Handbook, Term 2 — FULLY READ + CITATION CHECK PASSED 2026-11-03

## The book itself

- Title as printed on the cover: “Pupils’ Handbook for Senior Secondary — English Language — SSS I — Term II”, “Free Quality School Education” banner, “STRICTLY NOT FOR SALE”.
- Issuing authority as printed: Ministry of Basic and Senior Secondary Education; foreword signed “Mr. Alpha Osman Timbo, Minister of Basic and Senior Secondary Education”, with the same “516 lesson plans and 516 practice activities” statement as Term 1.
- Year: no publication year printed in the book itself. (File metadata only: author field “Alison Morris Oswald”, created 1 August 2018, modified January 2019.)
- PDF pages: 138. Pagination: front matter in Roman numerals exactly as Term 1 (foreword i, notice ii, contents iii–iv, introduction v, WASSCE key-takeaways section vi–viii, PDF pp.2–9; PDF p.9 carries only “viii”). Body: printed page = PDF page − 9; lessons printed 1–105, PDF p.115 carries only the printed numeral 106, Answer Key printed 107–128 (PDF pp.116–137), PDF p.138 is the back cover.
- Form: same handbook frame; lesson numbering continues the SSS 1 year (Lessons 49–96 of 140), codes PHL1-L049 to PHL1-L096.
- Contents: Lessons 49–96.

## Map of the term — Lessons 49–96 in order (printed start page; what each teaches)

- L49 (p.1) Verb Tense Review — Grammar: the five tenses taught in Term 1 (simple present, present continuous, simple past, past continuous, simple future) identified and used.
- L50 (p.5) Future Continuous Tense — Grammar: will be + verb-ing for an unfinished action at a future point; statements and questions.
- L51 (p.7) Reading Comprehension — Narrative — Reading: the features of narrative text; story “My Earliest Memory” about Olivette, born in a small village in the north of Sierra Leone and named after her mother’s favourite aunt (plot only; Part 1 here, continued in L52).
- L52 (p.9) Reading Comprehension — Narrative — Reading: the narrative checklist applied to the continuation of the Olivette story.
- L53 (p.12) Classification of Nouns — Common, Proper — Grammar: general (common) vs specific (proper) nouns; capital letters for proper nouns; articles with nouns.
- L54 (p.15) Classification of Nouns — Concrete, Abstract — Grammar: things known through the senses vs ideas and qualities.
- L55 (p.17) Narrative Text — Writing: planning a narrative from an outline (topic, characters, setting, plot).
- L56 (p.19) Narrative Text — Writing: writing the narrative essay — hook the reader, build suspense, do not give everything away in the first paragraph.
- L57 (p.21) Comprehending a Listening Passage — News Report — Listening: summarising a heard news report from notes, without re-reading.
- L58 (p.23) Comprehending a Listening Passage — News Report — Listening/Speaking: using WH questions to plan a news report, then presenting one and answering questions on it (topics: government, ministry, current and local news).
- L59 (p.25) Pure Vowel Sounds — Long and Short Vowels — Listening/Speaking: the five vowel letters, y acting as vowel or consonant; identifying short vowel sounds.
- L60 (p.27) Pure Vowel Sounds — Long and Short Vowels — Listening/Speaking: long vs short pure vowels, and how a vowel’s position decides its sound.
- L61 (p.29) Vocabulary Development — Animal Husbandry — Reading: WASSCE field defined (the science of breeding and caring for animals); word meanings by context and inference.
- L62 (p.32) Vocabulary Development — Animal Husbandry — Writing: writing a text using animal-husbandry vocabulary.
- L63 (p.34) Reading Skills Development — WH Questions — Reading: using who/what/when/where/why/how questions to check understanding of a text.
- L64 (p.37) Reading Skills Development — WH Questions — Reading: making your own WH questions when none are provided, to guide your reading.
- L65 (p.39) Classification of Nouns — Collective, Countable, Uncountable Nouns — Grammar: the three classes and their article/verb behaviour.
- L66 (p.41) Classification of Nouns — Compound Nouns — Grammar: nouns made of two or more words, hyphenated compounds, and where the plural -s goes (the book’s example type: sisters-in-law).
- L67 (p.43) Descriptive Text — Reading: the features of descriptive text (descriptive vocabulary painting a picture in the reader’s mind); drafting from an outline.
- L68 (p.46) Descriptive Text — Writing: writing a complete descriptive text with correct grammar and spelling.
- L69 (p.48) Pronouns — Grammar: pronouns replacing nouns; singular/plural; subject vs object pronouns.
- L70 (p.51) Types of Pronouns — Subject, Object Pronouns — Grammar: the two personal-pronoun cases in depth.
- L71 (p.53) Paragraphs — Topic Sentence and Supporting Sentence — Writing: a paragraph as 3–5 related sentences; what makes a good topic sentence and good supporting sentences.
- L72 (p.55) Vocabulary Development — Suffixes — Grammar: suffixes added to word endings to change meaning or word class; full tables of noun suffixes (-age, -hood, -ness, -ship…), verb suffixes (-ate, -en, -ify, -ise), adjective and adverb suffixes.
- L73 (p.58) Reading Skills Development — Context Clues (fable) — Reading: types of context clue (definition, contrast, logic…); practised on a fable-like story about Francis, a mistrustful man who buries his fortune under a tree, refuses loans to neighbours who need them, and is overheard hiding the money (plot only).
- L74 (p.61) Reading Skills Development — Context Clues (fable) — Reading: more clue types, and the continuation of the Francis story (his selfishness is thrown back at him, and someone is seen digging under his tree; plot only).
- L75 (p.64) Types of Pronouns — Possessive and Demonstrative — Grammar: possessive pronouns replacing possessive nouns; demonstratives (this, that, these, those).
- L76 (p.67) Types of Pronouns — Relative, Reflexive — Grammar: relative pronouns (who, which, whose) joining clauses; reflexive pronouns (myself, yourself, themselves…) when subject and object are the same person.
- L77 (p.69) Reading Comprehension — Drama — Reading: the features of drama (characters, plot, theme, dialogue, genre, audience, script); play “A Birthday Present” (Fatu and Fallubah argue over a flowery vs a plain blue shirt for their father’s birthday; plot only), read, role-played and questioned.
- L78 (p.72) Listening Comprehension — Drama — Listening: how a script is laid out (names with colons, direct speech, stage directions in italics); listening questions and summary on a second short play extract (vocabulary pre-taught includes “peace offering” and “take into account”).
- L79 (p.75) Drama — Writing: writing a play script with correct formatting.
- L80 (p.77) Drama — Speaking/Listening: presenting a play — reading with expression, tone, stress and intonation; noting how your character feels at each point.
- L81 (p.78) Types of Pronouns — Reciprocal, Emphatic, Interrogative — Grammar: “each other/one another”; emphatic pronouns; question pronouns.
- L82 (p.80) Types of Pronouns — Indefinite and Problems with Pronouns — Grammar: indefinite pronouns in -body/-one/-thing, and common pronoun errors.
- L83 (p.82) Vocabulary Development — Sports — Reading: WASSCE sports vocabulary through a reading text, meanings by context and inference.
- L84 (p.85) Vocabulary Development — Sports — Writing: completing and writing a text with sports vocabulary (players, officials, equipment, scoring as organising questions).
- L85 (p.86) Comprehending a Listening Passage — Debate — Listening: the features of a debate speech (a formal, structured argument for or against a motion).
- L86 (p.88) Debate — Writing: planning an argument for debate from an outline.
- L87 (p.90) Debate — Writing: writing the debate speech — formal language, no contractions, written for the audience you must convince.
- L88 (p.92) Debate — Listening/Speaking: delivering the debate speech fluently and persuasively, with tone and intonation.
- L89 (p.94) Reading Skills Development — Reasoning — Reading: inductive vs deductive reasoning; using reasoning to assume and predict.
- L90 (p.96) Reading Skills Development — Reasoning — Reading: reasoning applied to texts; reading on the lines, between the lines and beyond the lines.
- L91 (p.98) Adjectives — Grammar: adjectives modifying nouns and pronouns; attributive (before the noun) vs predicative (after the verb) position.
- L92 (p.99) Simple Past — Irregular Verbs — Grammar: a further irregular-verbs lesson, kept in the sequence as examination revision.
- L93 (p.100) Vocabulary Development — Idioms — Reading: idioms as groups of words whose meaning is not literal; explaining idioms in your own words.
- L94 (p.102) Vocabulary Development — Idioms — Writing: using idioms to express ideas in a text.
- L95 (p.103) Vocabulary Development — Idioms — Reading: a second reading pass over idioms in context.
- L96 (p.105) Vocabulary Development — Idioms — Writing: writing with idioms; the Answer Key’s example answers use, among others, “fell on deaf ears”, “a piece of cake” (glossed: it was very easy) and “burning the candle at both ends” (short fragments only).
- Answer Key (p.107) — answers and model texts for all 48 lessons, including an Ebola-2014 news-report model (a report-writing example discussing Sierra Leone’s response to the 2014 Ebola outbreak, in the key to the news-report lessons).

## Grammar and vocabulary spine of the book

Grammar: one new tense only — future continuous (L50) after a full Term 1 tense review (L49) and a late irregular-verbs revision (L92). The term’s grammar weight sits on two classification systems: nouns in four lessons (common/proper L53, concrete/abstract L54, collective/countable/uncountable L65, compound L66) and pronouns in five lessons (introduction L69, subject/object L70, possessive/demonstrative L75, relative/reflexive L76, reciprocal/emphatic/interrogative L81, indefinite and pronoun problems L82), plus adjectives attributive/predicative (L91) and suffixes as word-building (L72). Phonology: pure vowels, long and short (L59–60), continuing Term 1’s consonant work. Vocabulary fields (WASSCE, read-then-write pairs): Animal Husbandry (L61–62), Sports (L83–84), and idioms in a four-lesson block (L93–96) — the largest vocabulary block in the SSS 1 year so far. Reading spine: narrative comprehension (L51–52), WH questions as a reading tool (L63–64), context clues on a fable (L73–74), reasoning — inductive/deductive and on/between/beyond the lines (L89–90). Genre spine: narrative writing (L55–56), descriptive text (L67–68), a four-lesson drama block — read, listen, write, perform (L77–80) — a four-lesson debate block (L85–88), news reports heard and presented (L57–58), and paragraph craft (topic/supporting sentences, L71).

- The fable used in L73–74 is not given a printed title in the lessons as extracted; I identify it by its main character (Francis) rather than inventing a title.
- L73’s Learning Outcomes are mis-numbered in the book (they run 1, 2, 4 — there is no outcome 3 printed).
- There is no poetry lesson and no letter-writing lesson in Term 2; letters pause after Term 1 and return in other forms (news report, debate speech, drama script).

## The book itself

- Title as printed on the cover: “Pupils’ Handbook for Senior Secondary — English Language — SSS I — Term III”, “Free Quality School Education” banner, “STRICTLY NOT FOR SALE”.
- Issuing authority as printed: Ministry of Basic and Senior Secondary Education; foreword signed “Mr. Alpha Osman Timbo, Minister of Basic and Senior Secondary Education”, with the same “516 lesson plans and 516 practice activities” statement as Terms 1–2.
- Year: no publication year printed in the book itself. (File metadata only: author field “Alison Morris Oswald”, created 1 August 2018, modified January 2019.)
- PDF pages: 126. Pagination: front matter in Roman numerals exactly as Terms 1–2 (foreword i, notice ii, contents iii–iv, introduction v, WASSCE key-takeaways vi–viii, PDF pp.2–9; PDF p.9 carries only “viii”). Body: printed page = PDF page − 9; lessons printed 1–95, PDF p.105 carries only the printed numeral 96, Answer Key printed 97–114 (PDF pp.106–123). PDF pp.124–125 carry only stray single numerals in the text layer and are otherwise blank; PDF p.126 is the back cover.
- Form: same handbook frame; this term completes the SSS 1 year (Lessons 97–140 of 140), codes PHL1-L097 to PHL1-L140 — with one exception noted below.

## Map of the term — Lessons 97–140 in order (printed start page; what each teaches)

- L97 (p.1) Grammar Review: Nouns, Pronouns, and Adjectives — Grammar: the noun, pronoun and adjective types taught across the year, identified in a passage and used correctly.
- L98 (p.4) Comparative Adjectives — Grammar: comparing two nouns (bigger, more + adjective, irregular forms).
- L99 (p.6) Superlative Adjectives — Grammar: comparing one subject with a group of three or more; comparative vs superlative.
- L100 (p.8) Rhyme — Listening/Speaking: rhyme is sound, not spelling; rhyme patterns/schemes (ABAB vs AABB worked through a short love poem); odd-one-out rhyme sets; poem “October Hills” (adapted), by John Rollin Ridge (purple hills rising to peaks; plot only).
- L101 (p.11) Vocabulary Development: Health — Reading: WASSCE health field organised by category (people at health institutions — doctor, nurse, midwife, matron, dispenser, porter, surgeon, dentist, pharmacist, patient, general practitioner; types of illness — epidemic, contagion, flu, aches…); meanings by context.
- L102 (p.13) Vocabulary Development: Health — Writing: completing and writing a text with health vocabulary; the reading passage follows a patient’s visit to hospital (a worried wait, a nurse, a private room; plot only).
- L103 (p.15) Reading Skills Development: Substitution of Words — Reading: replacing words with synonyms without changing meaning, judging by context; the practice text is a story about two friends, Doris and Marie, who fall out over whether to sing or dance in a school talent show and are reconciled by their teacher, Mrs Jalloh, who suggests doing both (plot only).
- L104 (p.17) Reading Skills Development: Identifying Topic Sentences — Reading: topic + controlling idea; using topic sentences to summarise; the practice article is “Honey Bee Decline Spreading Globally” (a UNEP report on collapsing bee colonies and pollination, adapted from Wikinews, 12 March 2011, as credited in the book; plot only).
- L105 (p.19) How to Write a Five-Paragraph Essay — Writing: introduction, three body paragraphs, conclusion; topic sentences, titles, well-developed ideas.
- L106 (p.21) How to Write a Five-Paragraph Essay — Writing: the same structure applied — building the full outline and essay. (This lesson’s code is printed differently from every other lesson in the year: “PHL1-T3-W27-L106”.)
- L107 (p.23) Reading Comprehension — Descriptive Essay — Reading: the features of a descriptive essay (five senses, figurative language, picture in the reader’s mind), with a plain vs descriptive paragraph comparison (a party scene, plain then enriched; plot only).
- L108 (p.25) Reading Comprehension — Descriptive Essay — Reading: descriptive language defined — simile, metaphor, personification — with examples.
- L109 (p.27) Descriptive Essay — Writing: outlining a five-paragraph descriptive essay.
- L110 (p.29) Descriptive Essay — Writing: writing the essay (set topic in the practice: “My favourite place”), checking title, structure, topic sentences and sensory language.
- L111 (p.31) Types of Adjectives — Demonstrative, Interrogative — Grammar: adjectives that point out which noun (this, those…) and adjectives that ask (which, what…).
- L112 (p.33) Types of Adjectives — Quantitative, Distributive — Grammar: adjectives of quantity, definite (exact amounts) and indefinite; distributive adjectives (each, every, either…).
- L113 (p.36) Comprehending a Listening Passage — Dialogue — Listening: listening to an informal dialogue, noting speakers and intonation; questions and summary.
- L114 (p.38) Order of Adjectives — Grammar: putting stacked adjectives in the accepted order (descriptive adjectives of colour, size, number and kind).
- L115 (p.40) Types of Adjectives — Possessive — Grammar: possessive adjectives (my, your, his…) before the noun they modify; no apostrophe.
- L116 (p.42) Types of Sentences — Simple, Complex, Compound — Grammar: a simple sentence as subject + verb + complete thought; combining simple sentences into compound and complex sentences with conjunctions and subordinate clauses.
- L117 (p.44) Present Perfect — Grammar: have/has + past participle connecting a past action to the present; negatives without double negatives; questions.
- L118 (p.47) Present Perfect Tense (with ever, never, already, yet) — Grammar: the four time words placed and used with the present perfect.
- L119 (p.49) Reading Comprehension — WASSCE Text — Reading: facing an unseen text as WASSCE does; predicting and inferring; literal vs inferential questions.
- L120 (p.51) Reading Comprehension — WASSCE Text — Reading: a second unseen-text lesson — main ideas and supporting details, self-questioning, visualising, prior knowledge, context clues.
- L121 (p.53) Past Perfect Tense — Grammar: had + past participle for the action completed before another past action; questions and negatives.
- L122 (p.55) Past Perfect Tense (with simple past) — Grammar: past perfect and simple past used together in one sentence, in statements, questions and negatives.
- L123 (p.57) Concord — Subject-Verb Agreement — Grammar: singular subject takes a singular verb, plural subject a plural verb; identifying agreement and writing with correct concord.
- L124 (p.59) Introduction to Adverbs — Grammar: an adverb modifies a verb, adjective or another adverb and answers when, where, how or how often.
- L125 (p.61) Future Perfect Tense — Grammar: will have + past participle for an event finished by a stated future time.
- L126 (p.63) Future Perfect Tense — Grammar: the negative (will not have…) without double negatives, and questions.
- L127 (p.65) Vocabulary Development: Phrasal Verbs — Reading: phrasal verbs as phrases whose meaning is not the sum of their words; meanings by context and inference.
- L128 (p.67) Vocabulary Development: Phrasal Verbs — Writing: using phrasal verbs to express ideas in a text.
- L129 (p.69) Reading Skills Development: Paraphrasing — Reading: putting a text into your own words to simplify it and avoid plagiarism; the practice article is “All US States Could Have Smoke-Free Laws by 2020” (CDC figures on indoor-smoking bans and second-hand smoke deaths, adapted from Wikinews, 22 April 2011, as credited in the book; plot only).
- L130 (p.71) Reading Skills Development: Using the Dictionary — Reading: what a dictionary entry holds (meaning, part of speech, pronunciation), alphabetical order, and using one to settle unknown words. (The lesson’s Theme is printed “Grammar” in the book although the lesson is a reading-skills lesson.)
- L131 (p.74) Report Writing — Writing: the features of a report — chronological order, title, introduction with date and summary, body of main ideas, closing, name and designation; drafting from an outline. The model report in the Answer Key covers a school’s celebration of the Day of the African Child in Bo District, 16 June 2017.
- L132 (p.76) Report Writing — Writing: writing the full report with topic sentences, correct grammar and spelling.
- L133 (p.78) Voiced and Unvoiced Consonants — Listening/Speaking: the 21 consonants divided by vocal-cord vibration; voiced/unvoiced sounds in initial, medial and end position and in sentences.
- L134 (p.80) Comprehending a Listening Passage — Dialogue — Listening: a dialogue about social pressure (in the Answer Key’s account, one speaker, Unama, thinks the other, Finda, is being silly; plot only); questions, summary, context clues.
- L135 (p.82) Active and Passive Voice — Grammar: active (subject performs the action) vs passive (subject receives it); converting sentences both ways.
- L136 (p.84) Active and Passive Voice — Grammar: emphasis — active stresses the doer, passive stresses the action or receiver; more conversions.
- L137 (p.86) Types of Adverbs — Manner — Grammar: adverbs telling how something happens, and their features.
- L138 (p.88) Types of Adverbs — Degree and Frequency — Grammar: adverbs telling how much (degree) and how often (frequency, used for routines).
- L139 (p.91) Vocabulary Development: Collocations — Reading: word partnerships that “sound natural” to native speakers and must be memorised or inferred; no rules produce them.
- L140 (p.93) Vocabulary Development: Collocations — Writing: writing a text using collocations; the SSS 1 year ends here.
- Answer Key (p.97) — answers and model texts for all 44 lessons.

## Grammar and vocabulary spine of the book

Grammar: adjectives are the term’s centre of gravity — comparison (L98–99), then five lessons of adjective types and behaviour (demonstrative/interrogative L111, quantitative/distributive L112, order of adjectives L114, possessive L115) after an opening review of nouns, pronouns and adjectives (L97). The tense ladder is completed: present perfect with its four time words (L117–118), past perfect alone and with the simple past (L121–122), future perfect in statements, negatives and questions (L125–126) — with SSS 1, every tense in the JSS/SSS system has now been taught. Sentence-level grammar closes the year: sentence types and combining sentences (L116), concord/subject–verb agreement (L123), active and passive voice (L135–136), and adverbs in three lessons (introduction L124, manner L137, degree/frequency L138). Phonology: rhyme and rhyme schemes (L100) and voiced/unvoiced consonants (L133), completing the sound work begun with consonants, clusters and pure vowels. Vocabulary fields: Health (L101–102), phrasal verbs (L127–128) and collocations (L139–140) — the year ends on the two vocabulary types that cannot be derived by rule and must be learned whole. Reading/writing spine: word substitution and topic sentences (L103–104), the five-paragraph essay in four lessons (plan L105–106, read L107–108, write L109–110), unseen-text WASSCE technique (L119–120), paraphrasing and dictionary use (L129–130), and report writing (L131–132) as the year’s new formal genre.

## Honest negatives — SSS 1 Term 3

- No publication year, ISBN or copyright page is printed in the book.
- Term 3 has 44 lessons against 48 in Terms 1–2; the year total is 140 lessons, and the book does not explain the shorter term.
- L106’s practice-activity code breaks the year’s pattern (“PHL1-T3-W27-L106” instead of “PHL1-L106”), and L130’s Theme is printed as “Grammar” for a reading-skills lesson — small production inconsistencies, recorded as printed.
- Printed p.96 (PDF p.105) is blank apart from its numeral; PDF pp.124–125 are effectively blank; PDF p.9 (printed viii) is numeral-only, as in Terms 1–2.
- There is no letter-writing, drama or debate lesson in Term 3; the year’s formal-writing close is the report, and its literature close is rhyme (L100) — no set literary text is studied in SSS 1 at all in any term.

## Citation check — SSS 1 Term 3

15 anchors checked against the PDF text; 15/15 passed.

- Cover, PDF p.1: “English Language”, “SSS I”, “Term III”.
- Contents, PDF p.4 (printed iii): “Lesson 97: Grammar Review: Nouns, Pronouns, and Adjectives” at p.1.
- L97 start, PDF p.10 (printed 1): code “PHL1-L097”.
- L100 poem, PDF p.18 (printed 9): “October Hills”.
- L101 start, PDF p.20 (printed 11): code “PHL1-L101” (health vocabulary).
- L104 practice article, PDF p.27 (printed 18): “Honey Bee Decline Spreading Globally”.
- L119 start, PDF p.58 (printed 49): code “PHL1-L119” (WASSCE text).
- L123 start, PDF p.66 (printed 57): code “PHL1-L123” (concord).
- L129 article, PDF p.79 (printed 70): “All US States Could Have Smoke-Free Laws by 2020”.
- L131 start, PDF p.83 (printed 74): code “PHL1-L131” (report writing).
- L133 lesson, PDF p.87 (printed 78): “Voiced and Unvoiced” consonants.
- L135 lesson, PDF p.91 (printed 82): “Active and Passive Voice”.
- L139 lesson, PDF p.100 (printed 91): “Collocations”.
- L140 start, PDF p.102 (printed 93): code “PHL1-L140”.
- Answer Key start, PDF p.106 (printed 97): “Answer Key”.

# Sierra Leone — English Language SSS2 Pupil Handbook, Term 1 — FULLY READ + CITATION CHECK PASSED 2026-11-03

## The book itself

- Title as printed on the cover: *Pupils' Handbook for Senior Secondary English Language — SSS II, Term I*, in the Free Quality School Education series, marked "STRICTLY NOT FOR SALE".
- Issuing authority as printed: Ministry of Basic and Senior Secondary Education (MBSSE), Sierra Leone. The Foreword (printed p. i) is signed by Mr. Alpha Osman Timbo, Minister of Basic and Senior Secondary Education. The back cover reads "GOVERNMENT OF SIERRA LEONE — FUNDED BY — IN PARTNERSHIP WITH — STRICTLY NOT FOR SALE"; the funder and partner names appear only as logos, not as printed text.
- Year: no publication year is printed anywhere in the book. The PDF file metadata (not print) gives author Alison Morris Oswald, created in Microsoft Word 2013 on 1 August 2018 and modified in January 2019.
- PDF pages: 158. Printed pagination: front matter in roman numerals (Foreword i, textbook-policy page ii, Contents iii–iv, Introduction v, "Key Takeaways" vi–vii; printed p. viii is blank), then lessons on printed pp. 1–120 and an Answer Key on printed pp. 121–148, then the back cover.
- Offset: printed page = PDF page − 9 throughout the lesson body (Lesson 1, printed p. 1, is PDF p. 10).
- What the book is: a practice-activity companion to the teacher Lesson Plans, not a standalone textbook. The Introduction states the activities are aligned to the Lesson Plans and based on the National Curriculum and the West Africa Examination Council (WAEC) syllabus guidelines. Every lesson follows the same pattern: header (Lesson Title, Theme, Practice Activity code PHL2-L001 to PHL2-L048, Class: SSS 2), Learning Outcomes, an Overview that teaches the point, then numbered Practice Activities to be written in the pupil's own exercise book (the policy page orders "DO NOT WRITE IN THE BOOKS" — books must last three years). Answers are checked against the Answer Key at the back.
- Shared front matter: the "Key Takeaways from Sierra Leone's Performance in WASSCE — English Language" section (printed pp. vi–vii, derived from WAEC Examiner Reports) names the national weak points the whole series keeps targeting: sound identification (pure vowels, diphthongs, triphthongs, consonants, clusters), stress and intonation errors; misunderstood essay questions and poor planning; weak format/style, missing transition vocabulary, topic-sentence problems; grammar errors in number, subject–verb agreement, pronoun–antecedent agreement, verb conjugation, perfect continuous tenses, phrases and clauses, and omitted articles; preposition misuse; poor punctuation and spelling; and, in comprehension, weak inference, figurative-language vocabulary, word-replacement (synonym/antonym) questions and grammatical-name-and-function questions. The Foreword states the series totals 516 lesson plans and 516 practice activities and exists to prepare pupils for WAEC examinations.

## Map of the term — lessons in order

Lessons 1–48 (printed pp. 1–120).

- **L1 Oral Presentation: Self-Introduction** (p. 1): preparing and delivering a spoken self-introduction; sentence frames for background, study, interests, hopes and strengths.
- **L2 Comprehending a Listening Passage** (p. 2): a biography of the Greek philosopher Plato (adapted from a Newsela text) read as a listening text; comprehension questions, summarising in own words, vocabulary by context clues.
- **L3 Tenses** (p. 4): present tenses — simple, continuous and perfect; uses (facts, habits, commentary), third-person -s, negatives without double negatives.
- **L4 Tenses** (p. 7): past tenses — simple (regular -ed and irregular forms), continuous and perfect.
- **L5 Tenses** (p. 10): future tenses — simple (will/shall and "going to"), continuous and perfect.
- **L6 Tenses** (p. 13): consolidation of all nine tense forms in one table (model verb "dance"), question forms, positive and negative forms.
- **L7–L8 Personal Narrative** (pp. 15, 17): planning with an outline and writing a first-person personal narrative — plot shape (introduction, build-up, climax, resolution), chronological order, theme/moral, figurative language, linking words between paragraphs.
- **L9 Reading Comprehension Fiction – Traditional Tale** (p. 19): features of traditional tales; the Anansi tale "How We Got the Name 'Spider Tales'" (Anansi/Spider tricks Nyankupon, chief of the gods, by capturing creatures including a boa constrictor measured against a stick). Explicit exam warning: for the WASSCE, pupils are not allowed to write stories with animal characters.
- **L10 Reading Comprehension – Fable** (p. 21): features of fables; "The Hare and the Lion" — animals send one of their number daily to the lion Basu, and the hare's lateness and excuse drive the plot. Same WASSCE animal-character warning.
- **L11–L12 Vocabulary Development: Environment** (pp. 23, 25): reading then writing with environment vocabulary — greenhouse gas emissions, renewable energy, sea ice, litter, plastic, recycling, waste, marine, biodegradable and similar terms, presented as a WASSCE-tested field.
- **L13 Parts of Speech** (p. 27): the 8 parts of speech, their functions, identifying them in sentences and writing with them.
- **L14–L15 Prepositions** (pp. 31, 34): simple vs complex prepositions; time/place/duration/movement uses; common errors corrected in the second lesson.
- **L16 Reading Skills Development: Skimming and Scanning** (p. 37): when and how to skim for gist and scan for specific information.
- **L17 Vowel Sounds Review** (p. 40): pure vowel sounds (monophthongs), long and short vowels, IPA symbols, and how the same sound is spelled many ways; pure vowel vs diphthong contrast.
- **L18 Consonant Sounds Review** (p. 43): the 24 consonant sounds of English, their spellings, and initial/medial/end positions in words.
- **L19 Adjectives – Irregular Comparatives and Superlatives** (p. 46): absolute/comparative/superlative degrees and the irregular sets that do not take -er/-est.
- **L20 Differences between Adjectives and Adverbs** (p. 49): what each modifies, and telling modifiers apart in sentences.
- **L21–L22 Informal Letters – Persuasive** (pp. 51, 53): features and types of informal letters (descriptive, persuasive, narrative); outlining, then writing, a persuasive informal letter with at least two supported arguments.
- **L23–L24 Conjunctions** (pp. 56, 58): coordinating vs subordinating conjunctions; second lesson builds compound and complex sentences with them.
- **L25–L26 Reading Skills Development: Factual and Inferential Questions** (pp. 61, 64): literal questions (who/what/when/where, answer stated in the text) vs inferential questions (how/why, answer deduced); applied to passages. (The book twice misprints the exam's name here as "WASCCE".)
- **L27–L28 Formal Letter – Letter of Complaint** (pp. 67, 69): formal-letter features and components (addresses, subject, tone), types (complaint, request, application); outline then write a letter of complaint.
- **L29–L30 Vocabulary Development: Traditional Religion** (pp. 71, 74): reading then writing — traditional religions, spirit, divination, ritual, deity, animism, cult, charm, amulet and related terms.
- **L31–L32 Vocabulary Development: Ceremonies** (pp. 76, 79): ceremony and culture terms; second lesson centres on weddings (bride, groom, spouse, in-laws, ancestors, bridal party, groomsmen).
- **L33 Punctuation – Inverted Commas** (p. 82): single inverted commas for direct speech, titles of short works, irony/scorn and technical terms; double inverted commas contrasted.
- **L34 Reading Skills Development: Substitution of Words** (p. 85): the WASSCE word-replacement task — choosing a synonym that fits the context, not merely a similar word.
- **L35–L36 Vocabulary Development: Synonyms and Antonyms** (pp. 87, 90): definitions, antonyms formed with prefixes (dis-, im-, in-, ir- and others), and using synonyms/antonyms in writing without breaking context.
- **L37 Reading Comprehension – Drama** (p. 92): features of a play (act, scene, characters); an excerpt from Shakespeare's *Romeo and Juliet*, Act 2 Scene 2 (the balcony scene; plot only — Juliet speaks, unaware Romeo is listening).
- **L38 Reading Comprehension – Drama** (p. 95): an excerpt from Shakespeare's *The Tragedy of Julius Caesar*, Act 2 Scene 1 (Brutus's scene, per the lesson overview).
- **L39 Parts of a Sentence** (p. 98): subject and predicate; simple, complete and compound subjects; subjects formed from infinitives, phrases and clauses.
- **L40 Writing Complete Sentences** (p. 100): what makes a sentence complete; correcting fragments and run-on sentences.
- **L41–L42 How to Write a Five-Paragraph Essay** (pp. 102, 104): the series' core essay model — at least 5 paragraphs (introduction, body, conclusion), at least/about 450 words, paragraph shape (topic sentence, supporting sentences, concluding sentence), and writing titles.
- **L43 Reading Comprehension – Expository Essay** (p. 106): features of expository writing (facts, statistics, expert quotes, impersonal and unbiased); the passage explains how newspaper reporters build a story from a "lead", editorial approval, field evidence and quotes from all sides.
- **L44 Reading Comprehension – Expository Essay** (p. 109): second expository passage, on lack of access to safe drinking water in Asia and Africa — daily water-carrying distances and loads, health effects, and "water wives" in India.
- **L45–L46 Expository Essay** (pp. 112, 114): essay types (how-to/process, compare and contrast, cause and effect, and others); outline a five-paragraph expository essay, then write it.
- **L47 Comprehending a Listening Passage** (p. 116): a radio interview script, "Chitty Chat with Abdul" — Abdul talks about watching football at West Ham and Arsenal, university football and accountancy work in London, and returning to "our very own Salone" (plot summary only).
- **L48 Word Stress** (p. 119): syllables (mono- to five-syllable words), one stressed syllable per word, and word-stress rules by word type.

## Grammar and vocabulary spine

- Grammar: a nine-form tense system (simple/continuous/perfect × present/past/future) taught form by form then consolidated; the 8 parts of speech; prepositions; conjunctions (coordinating/subordinating) and compound/complex sentences; subject–predicate structure and complete sentences; irregular comparatives and superlatives; adjective vs adverb; inverted commas.
- Vocabulary fields: environment; traditional religion; ceremonies. Plus the word-level skills of synonyms, antonyms and context-fitting substitution.
- Oral strand: pure vowels, the 24 consonant sounds, word stress — all framed as WASSCE Paper 3 (oral) preparation.
- Writing strand: personal narrative; persuasive informal letter; formal letter of complaint; the five-paragraph/450-word essay model and the expository essay.
- Reading strand: biography, traditional tale, fable, two Shakespeare excerpts, expository passages, a radio script; skills of skimming/scanning, factual vs inferential questions, substitution, summarising.

## Honest negatives

- There is no lesson explaining the structure of the WASSCE itself in this book; exam orientation is the front-matter Key Takeaways plus warnings inside lessons (for example, the animal-character ban in L9–L10).
- The perfect continuous tenses are not taught here: the tense lessons cover simple, continuous and perfect only, even though the Key Takeaways name perfect continuous as a known Sierra Leonean weak point. Conditionals, passive voice, reported speech, modal verbs and question tags get no lessons in this book (reported speech and conditionals arrive later in SSS2; the rest wait for SSS3).
- "Listening" lessons are printed scripts to be read; the book contains no audio component.
- The final two Answer Key pages (printed pp. 147–148, PDF pp. 156–157) yielded only their folio numbers to text extraction, so their answer content could not be verified from the text layer; everything before them extracted cleanly.
- No Sierra Leonean language (Krio, Temne or Mende) is used or taught; Sierra Leone appears in content mainly through the Key Takeaways, the radio interview's return to "Salone", and locally set examples.

## Citation check

16 anchors verified against the actual PDF text, 16/16 passed: cover "STRICTLY NOT FOR SALE" (PDF p. 1); Foreword signature "Mr. Alpha Osman Timbo" (PDF p. 2); PHL2-L001 self-introduction header, printed p. 1 (PDF p. 10); Plato biography (PDF p. 11); simple present tense (PDF p. 13); "How We Got the Name" (PDF p. 28); "The Hare and the Lion" (PDF p. 30); comparatives and superlatives (PDF p. 55); formal-letter overview sentence (PDF p. 76); "quotation marks" (PDF p. 91); Romeo and Juliet (PDF p. 102); Julius Caesar (PDF p. 105); features of a 5-paragraph essay (PDF p. 111); "Chitty Chat with Abdul" (PDF p. 125); Word Stress (PDF p. 128); Answer Key (PDF p. 130). Printed folios matched the PDF-page − 9 offset on every content-page anchor.

# Sierra Leone — English Language SSS2 Pupil Handbook, Term 2 — FULLY READ + CITATION CHECK PASSED 2026-11-03

## The book itself

- Title as printed: *Pupils' Handbook for Senior Secondary English Language — SSS II, Term II*, Free Quality School Education series, "STRICTLY NOT FOR SALE".
- Issuing authority as printed: Ministry of Basic and Senior Secondary Education, Sierra Leone; Foreword signed by Mr. Alpha Osman Timbo, Minister of Basic and Senior Secondary Education; back cover "GOVERNMENT OF SIERRA LEONE — FUNDED BY — IN PARTNERSHIP WITH", with funder/partner names as logos only.
- Year: none printed. PDF metadata (not print): author Alison Morris Oswald, Word 2013, created 1 August 2018, modified January 2019.
- PDF pages: 170 — the longest of the six. Printed pagination: same roman-numeral front matter as Term 1 (Foreword i, policy ii, Contents iii–iv, Introduction v, Key Takeaways vi–vii, blank viii), lessons on printed pp. 1–128, Answer Key on printed pp. 129–158, then two pages whose text layer carries only the numerals "1" and "2", then the back cover.
- Offset: printed page = PDF page − 9. Lesson numbering continues the SSS2 year: Lessons 49–96, practice codes PHL2-L049 to PHL2-L096. Format, Introduction and Key Takeaways front matter are the same as Term 1 (see the Term 1 section for their contents).

## Map of the term — lessons in order

Lessons 49–96 (printed pp. 1–128).

- **L49 Comprehending a Listening Passage – Giving Instructions** (p. 1): the listening text "Giving First Aid" (helping an injured person); grammar point is the imperative form for instructions and its negative ("do not" / "don't").
- **L50 Sentence Stress** (p. 3): stress inside sentences — in substance a lesson on emphatic stress: saying a word more slowly or loudly, and its functions (new terminology, specific questions, contrast).
- **L51 Types of Phrases** (p. 6): a phrase has no subject–predicate relationship and is not a complete thought; survey of phrase types, starting with noun and verb phrases.
- **L52 Phrases – Noun Phrases and Adjectival Phrases** (p. 9): noun phrases as subject, object and complement; adjectival phrases.
- **L53–L54 Figurative Language: Simile, Metaphor, Personification** (pp. 11, 14): reading lesson (definitions, figuring out meaning from context), then a writing lesson using the three devices.
- **L55 Reading Comprehension – Poetry** (p. 16): features of poems (stanzas, imagery, rhythm, rhyme, figurative language); two poems — William Wordsworth's "I Wandered Lonely as a Cloud" (the daffodils poem) and an excerpt from Emily Dickinson's "'Hope' is the Thing with Feathers" (plot/theme summary only).
- **L56 Phrases – Verb Phrases and Adverbial Phrases** (p. 19): verb phrases (main verb + auxiliaries, including a passive-voice example) and adverbial phrases.
- **L57 Vocabulary Development: Prefixes** (p. 21): prefixes that reverse meaning, mark time or describe physical aspects; table including anti-, pro-, de-, dis- and the il-/im-/in-/ir- set.
- **L58 Vocabulary Development: Suffixes** (p. 24): suffixes that change meaning or word class; noun suffix table (-age, -al, -ance/-ence, -dom, -ee, -er/-or, -hood, -ism, -ity/-ty, -ment, -ness, -ship, -sion/-tion and others).
- **L59–L60 Vocabulary Development: Science and Technology** (pp. 27, 29): reading then writing — gene, genetic engineering, geneticists, technology and related terms, framed as WASSCE-tested vocabulary.
- **L61 Determiners (definite and indefinite articles – a, an, the)** (p. 31): determiners specify what a noun refers to; article types and uses.
- **L62 Determiners vs Adjectives** (p. 34): adjectives describe quality, quantity and relationship; how determiners differ from adjectives.
- **L63 Conditional Tense – Zero Conditional** (p. 37): conditionals answer "what if"; five types are named (zero, first, second, third, mixed) but only the zero conditional is taught — if/when/unless + simple present for general and universal truths.
- **L64 Conditional Tense – First Conditional** (p. 39): if/unless + simple present, with simple future in the main clause, for a possible condition and its probable result.
- **L65–L66 Figurative Language – Irony** (pp. 41, 43): verbal, situational and dramatic irony — reading lesson, then writing with irony.
- **L67 Reading Skills Development: Identifying Theme – Fable** (p. 46): theme as the underlying message (concepts such as courage, greed, wisdom), inferred not stated; practice fables include "The Lion in Love" (a lion in love with a woodcutter's daughter demands her hand in marriage — plot summary only).
- **L68 Paragraphs – Topic Sentence and Supporting Sentences** (p. 48): a topic sentence mentions the topic, carries the main idea and a controlling idea; building one from a topic (example: preparing for an examination).
- **L69–L70 Speech** (pp. 51, 53): speech features — vocatives addressing the audience, first person, rhetorical questions, references to sources; outline/draft, then write a speech (worked example stance: examinations should be held twice a year).
- **L71–L72 Intonation – Rising Pattern and Falling Pattern** (pp. 56, 59): intonation as stress plus pitch; rising intonation for yes/no questions and unfinished or uncertain speech; falling intonation; second lesson applies both to a listening passage.
- **L73–L74 Idioms and Phrasal Verbs** (pp. 61, 64): reading then writing; phrasal-verb table (to add up, bring up, crop up, drop out and others) with meanings and example sentences.
- **L75 Direct Speech** (p. 67): direct speech quotes exact words inside inverted commas; contrast with indirect speech introduced.
- **L76 Indirect Speech** (p. 70): reported speech — no inverted commas, "that" as connector, and the tense/person changes made when reporting.
- **L77–L78 Concord – Subject-Verb Agreement** (pp. 73, 76): singular/plural agreement rules, including the subject before an "of" phrase; second lesson adds pronoun–antecedent agreement.
- **L79–L80 Reading Skills Development: Identifying Tone** (pp. 79, 82): tone as the writer's expression and attitude; mood; objective tone (formal/essay writing, facts) vs subjective tone (personal views in informal, descriptive and narrative writing).
- **L81–L82 Article for Publication in a Newspaper or Magazine** (pp. 85, 87): features (grabs attention, relevant issue, facts and evidence, formal register for a wide audience, less formal for a school audience); outline, then write.
- **L83 Comprehending a Listening Passage – Debate** (p. 90): debate concepts (motion, for/against the motion) taught explicitly as a WASSCE requirement; features of a debate speech (opinion, evidence, convincing the audience).
- **L84 Debate** (p. 93): writing a debate speech.
- **L85–L86 Debate** (pp. 95, 98): speaking lessons — fluency, tone and intonation, emotive language, rhetorical questions, and answering the opposing point of view.
- **L87 Reading Comprehension – Academic Text (science)** (p. 100): reading strategy for science texts (main ideas per paragraph, context clues, facts and figures, skimming/scanning); the text explains how the brain learns — a child's word "mummy" becomes fixed through repeated association forming neural pathways (summary only).
- **L88 Reading Comprehension – News Article (economic development)** (p. 103): economic development defined (industry, finances, living standards, jobs); the article, "Women in Agriculture", argues for empowering women farmers in Sierra Leone through agro-business, networks and management skills (summary only).
- **L89–L90 Vocabulary Development: Cultures and Institutions** (pp. 106, 109): culture defined (beliefs, customs, rituals, social behaviour — language, religion, cuisine, habits, music, arts) and institution defined (established law or practice, e.g. marriage, tribal institutions); reading then writing.
- **L91–L92 Descriptive Writing (culture, institutions and ceremonies)** (pp. 112, 114): descriptive essay features (detail, the five senses, figurative language); outline with descriptive word banks, then write, structured around a ceremony's setting and people.
- **L93–L94 Reading Comprehension – Literature** (pp. 116, 119): O. Henry's short story "The Gift of the Magi", read in two parts across the two lessons; the overview explains O. Henry (American author born 1862, known for twist endings) and the title (the Magi, the wise men who brought gifts at Jesus's birth). Plot not transcribed.
- **L95 Emphatic Stress** (p. 122): emphatic stress functions revisited with new examples (terminology, specific questions, contrast — "It is not my sister that I am angry with; it is my aunt").
- **L96 Comprehending a Listening Passage – Identifying Mood, Tone and Purpose** (p. 125): applying the tone/mood distinction (objective vs subjective tone) to an unseen listening text.

## Grammar and vocabulary spine

- Grammar: phrases (noun, verb, adjectival, adverbial); determiners and articles; zero and first conditionals only; direct and indirect speech; concord (subject–verb and pronoun–antecedent); prefixes and suffixes as word-building grammar.
- Figurative language: simile, metaphor, personification, then irony (verbal, situational, dramatic); idioms, phrasal verbs; theme and tone as reading concepts.
- Vocabulary fields: science and technology; cultures and institutions.
- Oral strand: sentence/emphatic stress, rising and falling intonation, debate speaking.
- Writing strand: speech, article for publication, debate (written and spoken), descriptive essay on culture/ceremonies; paragraph craft (topic and supporting sentences).
- Reading strand: Romantic and American poetry (Wordsworth, Dickinson), an Aesop-style fable, O. Henry's story in two parts, a science text on learning and the brain, and a Sierra Leone-set development article.

## Honest negatives

- Only two of the five named conditionals are taught in SSS2 (zero and first, L63–L64); second, third and mixed conditionals are named in a list and deferred to SSS3.
- Passive voice still has no lesson — it appears only inside an example in the verb-phrase lesson (L56). Modal verbs and question tags receive no lessons and modal verbs are not mentioned at all in SSS2 (verified by full-text search of all three SSS2 books: zero occurrences of "modal verb").
- There is no WASSCE paper-structure lesson in this book either; the exam appears as a requirement inside content lessons (debate, vocabulary fields) and in one passage footnote crediting a text as adapted from a WAEC examination (2009 passage noted in the book).
- The two pages after the Answer Key (PDF pp. 168–169) contain only the numerals "1" and "2" in the text layer and no images; what, if anything, is printed on them could not be determined from the file.
- Poetry and prose in this book are British and American (Wordsworth, Dickinson, O. Henry); the Sierra Leonean presence is the development article's subject matter, not a named Sierra Leonean author.

# Sierra Leone — English Language SSS2 Pupil Handbook, Term 3 — FULLY READ + CITATION CHECK PASSED 2026-11-03

## The book itself

- Title as printed: *Pupils' Handbook for Senior Secondary English Language — SSS II, Term III*, Free Quality School Education series, "STRICTLY NOT FOR SALE".
- Issuing authority as printed: Ministry of Basic and Senior Secondary Education, Sierra Leone; Foreword signed by Mr. Alpha Osman Timbo; back cover "GOVERNMENT OF SIERRA LEONE — FUNDED BY — IN PARTNERSHIP WITH", funder/partner as logos only.
- Year: none printed. PDF metadata (not print): author Alison Morris Oswald, Word 2013, created 1 August 2018, modified January 2019.
- PDF pages: 138 — the shortest of the six, because Term 3 carries 44 lessons (Lessons 97–140, codes PHL2-L097 to PHL2-L140) instead of 48. Printed pagination: same front matter as Terms 1–2, lessons on printed pp. 1–102, Answer Key on printed pp. 103–128, back cover.
- Offset: printed page = PDF page − 9.

## Map of the term — lessons in order

Lessons 97–140 (printed pp. 1–102).

- **L97 Consonant Clusters** (p. 1): two or more consonants together with no vowel between, in initial, medial and end positions; pronunciation advice (say the sounds together).
- **L98 Comprehending a Listening Passage – Formal Letter** (p. 3): formal-letter features revised; the listening/reading text is a formal letter addressed from 145 Aberdeen Street, Freetown, dated 13 September 2017.
- **L99–L100 Reading Comprehension – Persuasive Essay** (pp. 6, 8): features of persuasive/argument essays (convincing language, facts and statistics, expert quotes, first person, answering the opposing view). L99's essay is "Why Morality and Ethics Should be Taught in Schools" (summary only: it argues schools, as a "second home", must teach right and wrong alongside academic skills). L100's essay argues against the death penalty (summary only: it attacks the logic of punishing murder with death and calls the practice a medieval survival).
- **L101 Interjections** (p. 10): interjections show emotion and do not affect the sentence's grammar; distinguished from exclamatory sentences; positions in a sentence.
- **L102–L103 Using Conjunctions, Prepositions and Interjections to Improve Sentence Quality** (pp. 12, 15): the three word classes combined as tools for making writing flow and interest the reader; second lesson improves a whole text with them.
- **L104 Reading Skills Development: Using the Dictionary** (p. 17): dictionary entries (meaning, part of speech, pronunciation), alphabetical order, and choosing the right meaning from several by context.
- **L105–L106 Informal Letter (persuasive)** (pp. 20, 22): return to the informal persuasive letter; tone calibration (casual with friends and siblings, respectful with elders); outline, then write.
- **L107–L108 Vocabulary Development: Homonyms** (pp. 24, 26): words spelled or sounding the same with different meanings, across word classes (ring, seal, right, wave, break, bow, present, peer and others in tables); reading, then writing with them.
- **L109–L110 Punctuation Review: Comma, Colon, Semi-Colon, Dash** (pp. 28, 31): uses of each mark, pauses when reading aloud; one example sentence is dated "16th April, 2018" — the only 2018 date appearing inside any of the six books' lesson text.
- **L111–L112 Reading Skills Development: Summarising Strategies** (pp. 34, 37): summary as shortened own-words overview — find main ideas, author's purpose, supporting ideas and essential examples, answer the WH questions, cut unnecessary detail; paraphrasing.
- **L113–L114 Diphthongs** (pp. 39, 41): diphthongs as two pure vowel sounds in one syllable with mouth movement; the first four — /eɪ/ "day", /aɪ/ "eye", /ɔɪ/ "boy", /aʊ/ "mouth" — against monophthongs.
- **L115–L116 Collocations** (pp. 44, 47): word partnerships that "sound correct" (have a good time, do one's best, make progress, break the ice, break the rules, pay attention, save energy, keep in touch and more); reading, then writing with them.
- **L117 Reading Comprehension – Formal Letter** (p. 49): reading a formal letter of complaint with its features (authority addressee, formal tone) and answering questions on it.
- **L118 Reading Comprehension – Prose (development issue)** (p. 51): development issues defined (infrastructure, economy, poverty, education, living standards; examples — drug abuse, hunger, unsafe water, corruption). The prose text "Corruption in Healthcare" opens with a grieving mother, Aminatu Mende, in a healthcare-corruption story (plot summary only).
- **L119–L120 Expository Essay** (pp. 53, 55): return to the expository essay — types including how-to/process (example: how to plant a sapling) and compare-and-contrast; outline, then write.
- **L121 Introduction to Clauses** (p. 56): a clause contains a subject and a verb; clause vs phrase; main and subordinate clauses introduced.
- **L122 Types of Clauses** (p. 59): main/independent vs subordinate/dependent clauses; subordinate types — noun clauses, adjectival clauses and adverbial clauses.
- **L123–L124 Diphthongs** (pp. 61, 63): the remaining diphthongs — /ɪə/ "ear", /ʊə/ "pure", /eə/ "pair", /əʊ/ "slow" — completing the full set of 8, then identification in a listening passage.
- **L125 Reading Comprehension – Creative Writing (WASSCE)** (p. 65): creative writing defined; in the WASSCE a short story may be set to illustrate an idiom or saying (worked example sayings include "The early bird catches the worm"). The reading text tells of schoolboys who mock their classmate Monday's ambition to work with computers, nicknaming him "computer mechanic", while Monday quietly takes daily computer lessons and is ahead of them by SSS 3 (plot summary only).
- **L126 Creative Writing** (p. 68): writing a short story illustrating an idiom or saying (worked example: "Strike while the iron is hot") — outline, draft, correct grammar and spelling.
- **L127 Adjectival Clauses** (p. 70): subordinate clauses that modify a noun, usually following it (relative-style clauses with who/which/in which).
- **L128 Adverbial Clauses** (p. 72): subordinate clauses that modify a verb, adjective or adverb, with functions including time and place.
- **L129 Present Participle** (p. 74): the -ing form; uses — forming continuous tenses and forming adjectives.
- **L130 Past Participle** (p. 77): forms in -en/-ed/-n/-t/-d; uses — forming perfect tenses and as adjectives; irregular forms flagged for memorisation.
- **L131–L132 Vocabulary Development: Building and Building Construction** (pp. 79, 81): building, construction, blueprint, foundation, iron rods, substructure, cement and more; reading, then completing/writing a text. A WASSCE-tested field.
- **L133–L134 Past Participle – Irregular Verbs** (pp. 83, 86): three patterns — all three forms the same (cut, put, hit, let, hurt), simple past = past participle (sleep/slept, leave/left), and all three forms different (speak/spoke/spoken, hide and others).
- **L135 Auxiliary Verbs** (p. 88): the three core auxiliaries "have", "be" and "do" as helping verbs and as main verbs; further uses of "be".
- **L136 Auxiliary Verbs** (p. 91): auxiliaries inside the continuous tenses — present "am/are + -ing", past "was/were + -ing", future "will be + -ing".
- **L137 Comprehending a Listening Passage (report)** (p. 93): report features (chronological, informative, formal language, colloquialisms only inside inverted commas); the text is a "Report on the Progress of the Beach Clean-Up Project" by SSS 2 pupils, begun 16 April (summary only).
- **L138–L139 Report** (pp. 95, 97): report components (title in Title Case, introductory paragraph with date/time/place, body, closing); outline and draft, then write a report — explicitly framed as a possible WASSCE task.
- **L140 Rhyme** (p. 99): rhyming words judged by sound, not spelling (who/true rhyme; host/lost do not); rhyme schemes ABAB, AABB, ABCB, ABBA. The SSS2 year ends here.

## Grammar and vocabulary spine

- Grammar: clauses (main/subordinate; noun, adjectival and adverbial clauses), present and past participles, irregular-verb participle patterns, and auxiliary verbs (have/be/do) including their work inside continuous tenses — together with Terms 1–2 this completes the SSS2 grammar core: tenses, parts of speech, phrases, clauses, concord basics, determiners, speech forms and sentence craft. Interjections complete the 8 parts of speech coverage.
- Phonology: consonant clusters and the full set of 8 diphthongs finish the SSS2 sound system alongside Term 1's pure vowels and consonants.
- Vocabulary fields: building and construction; word-level work on homonyms and collocations; punctuation review (comma, colon, semi-colon, dash) and inverted commas from Term 1 make a full mechanics set.
- Writing strand: persuasive reading feeding argument writing, informal persuasive letter, expository essay (second pass), creative short story for an idiom/saying, and report writing.
- Reading strand: persuasive essays on school morality teaching and the death penalty, a Sierra Leone-set corruption prose text, dictionary and summarising skills.

## Honest negatives

- SSS2 closes without ever teaching modal verbs (the term does not occur in any SSS2 book), question tags, passive voice as a topic, or the second/third/mixed conditionals — all are held for SSS3. The perfect continuous tenses are likewise never taught as forms in SSS2.
- There is no mock WASSCE, no past-paper practice set and no exam-structure lesson in the SSS2 year; exam preparation is embedded (WASSCE warnings, WASSCE-style tasks such as the report and the idiom story) rather than assembled.
- The story in L125 is not given an author in the lesson overview, and no Sierra Leonean author is named anywhere in SSS2.
- Term 3's four missing lessons (44 against 48) are not explained in the book; the year simply ends at Lesson 140 with Rhyme.

## Citation check
