# Sierra Leone — English Language SSS3 Pupil Handbook, Term 1 — FULLY READ + CITATION CHECK PASSED 2026-11-03

## The book itself

- Title as printed: *Pupils' Handbook for Senior Secondary English Language — SSS III, Term I*, Free Quality School Education series, "STRICTLY NOT FOR SALE". SSS3 is the final school year; this book opens the WASSCE year.
- Issuing authority as printed: Ministry of Basic and Senior Secondary Education, Sierra Leone; Foreword signed by Mr. Alpha Osman Timbo; back cover "GOVERNMENT OF SIERRA LEONE — FUNDED BY — IN PARTNERSHIP WITH", funder/partner as logos only.
- Year: none printed. PDF metadata (not print): author Alison Morris Oswald, Word 2013, created 1 August 2018, modified January 2019.
- PDF pages: 154. Printed pagination: same front matter as SSS2 (Foreword i, policy ii, Contents iii–iv, Introduction v, Key Takeaways vi–vii, blank viii), lessons on printed pp. 1–117, Answer Key on printed pp. 118–142, then one page carrying only the folio "143" and one carrying only "1" in the text layer, then the back cover.
- Offset: printed page = PDF page − 9. Lessons restart at 1: Lessons 1–48, practice codes PHL3-L001 to PHL3-L048, Class: SSS 3. The character of the year changes: most lessons are explicitly reviews or exam preparation (see the WASSCE lesson, L2).

## Map of the term — lessons in order

Lessons 1–48 (printed pp. 1–117).

- **L1 Oral Presentation: Self-Introduction** (p. 1): the self-introduction again, now also introducing someone else; components (name, greetings, background, education, hobbies, future dreams) and delivery (loud, clear, paced — the practice even times readings at fast, slow and normal speed).
- **L2 WASSCE Guidelines** (p. 2): the exam-structure lesson the SSS2 books never had. As printed: the English Language exam has 3 papers; Papers 1 and 2 are taken in one 3-hour sitting and Paper 3 (oral) in a separate 45-minute sitting. Paper 1: 1 hour, 80 multiple-choice questions on vocabulary, idioms, figurative language and grammatical structure. Paper 2 Section A (essay): 50 minutes, choose 1 of 5 prompts, 450 words, in 9 formats — letter, speech, narrative essay, descriptive essay, expository essay, debate, report, article and creative writing. Paper 2 Section B: comprehension questions on a passage (including questions of the "why did the main character…" and figure-of-speech type). Paper 2 Section C (summary): 40 minutes, summarise one passage in own words. Paper 3: 45 minutes, 60 multiple-choice questions on listening comprehension, consonant and vowel sounds, rhyme, stress and intonation, with worked examples. Practice activities imitate each paper.
- **L3–L4 Parts of Speech Review** (pp. 6, 8): the 8 parts of speech in table form (noun, pronoun, verb, adjective, adverb, preposition, conjunction, interjection), then identifying and using all 8 in sentences.
- **L5–L6 Vocabulary Development: Government and Administration** (pp. 10, 12): policies, parliament, public servant, corruption and more; the second lesson's writing text is written from the perspective of Nigeria and adds local government and federalism (power divided between national, state and local leaders).
- **L7–L8 Active and Passive Voice** (pp. 14, 18): first lesson works through sentence functions (subject, object, indirect object, subject complement) and identifying voice in a text; second lesson teaches the conversion both ways (move object, add a "to be" verb form, add "by" + subject).
- **L9 Reading Comprehension – Descriptive Essay** (p. 20): descriptive writing that engages the five senses; the essay "America's Pastime" describes an American baseball game — national anthem, hot dog and soda at the seventh-inning stretch, singing "Take Me Out to the Ball Game", and players from around the world (summary only).
- **L10 How to Write a Five-Paragraph Essay** (p. 22): the essay model restated for the exam year — at least 5 paragraphs, at least 450 words, topic and supporting sentences, clear language, care with spelling, grammar and punctuation.
- **L11–L12 Descriptive Essay** (pp. 25, 27): planning with a senses outline (see/hear/taste/touch/smell plus descriptive words), then drafting; figurative uses of sense words (a "rotten" first day; a classroom "fragrant with fresh ideas").
- **L13 Tenses Review** (p. 29): present tenses — the overview now states the full system: 4 forms within each time (simple, continuous/progressive, perfect and perfect continuous); simple present uses and third-person -s.
- **L14 Tenses Review** (p. 33): past tenses, ordering past events against each other; simple past formation.
- **L15 Tenses Review** (p. 37): future tenses; simple future with will/shall and "going to".
- **L16 Tenses Review** (p. 40): all 12 tenses tabulated (model subject "Sandra") in positive and negative forms; questions across tenses, avoiding double negatives.
- **L17 Comprehending a Listening Passage** (p. 42): an international radio text on the 2016 African Nations Championship football tournament — after nearly three weeks, Mali and the Democratic Republic of Congo reach the final, the DRC having beaten Guinea on penalties and Mali in its first final (summary only).
- **L18 Creative Writing** (p. 44): creative-writing formats (poem, play, television show, movie, short story, novel, memoir) and shared elements — plot, theme, characters, setting, conflict, point of view; outlining a short story.
- **L19 Word Stress** (p. 47): syllables and stress rules, including guessing stress from root word, suffix or part of speech; two-syllable noun patterns.
- **L20 Emphatic Stress** (p. 49): content words (nouns, verbs, adjectives, adverbs, negative auxiliaries) are stressed and structure words (pronouns, prepositions, articles, conjunctions, auxiliaries) are not, with exceptions to clarify or correct.
- **L21–L22 Vocabulary Development: Law and Order** (pp. 51, 53): the reading text is a news article on Sierra Leone Police spending on fighting crime; writing lesson adds hotline, forensic, checkpoint, prosecute, defendant and more, inside a fictional story. Framed throughout as WASSCE vocabulary.
- **L23 Reading Comprehension – Report** (p. 56): report features; the text "My First Trip" reports a family trip on 28 August to visit an uncle in Freetown, the narrator's first trip to the capital (summary only).
- **L24 Report** (p. 58): writing a report from an outline (introduction, topic sentence, actions/reactions, body paragraphs).
- **L25–L26 Vocabulary Development: Simile, Metaphor, Personification** (pp. 60, 62): the three devices again at SSS3 level — reading (explaining examples in own words), then writing a paragraph with them.
- **L27–L28 Vocabulary Development: Idioms, Phrasal Verbs and Collocations** (pp. 64, 67): all three expression types together; collocations must be memorised as they follow no rule; phrasal-verb tables (e.g. "give back" — to return something); reading, then writing.
- **L29 Conditional Tense – Second Conditional** (p. 69): the conditional family restated (5 types); zero recapped, then the second conditional for hypothetical conditions (if-clause simple past; main clause present conditional forms).
- **L30 Conditional Tense – Third Conditional** (p. 71): hypothetical past events that did not happen — regret and relief; if-clause past perfect, main clause perfect conditional.
- **L31 Reading Skills Development: Skimming and Scanning** (p. 73): exam-focused return — comprehension questions ask for specific information or general ideas, not every word; strategies for finding main ideas fast.
- **L32 Consonant Sounds** (p. 76): the 24 consonant sounds again, with spellings and initial/medial/end examples, applied to a listening passage.
- **L33 Conditional Tense – Mixed Conditional** (p. 78): past hypothetical events and their present consequences — unlike the third conditional (a different past), the mixed conditional describes a different present; regret and relief again.
- **L34 Case in Nouns – Subjective** (p. 80): nouns and pronouns take subjective, objective or possessive case; subjective case for subjects and subject complements.
- **L35 Case in Nouns – Objective** (p. 82): objective forms (me, you, him, her, it, us, them, whom) for direct and indirect objects; implied subjects in commands.
- **L36 Case in Nouns – Possessive** (p. 84): paired possessive forms (my/mine, your/yours, her/hers, our/ours, their/theirs); possessives ending in -s take no apostrophe.
- **L37 Reading Comprehension – Drama** (p. 86): drama features (plot, themes, characters, setting, conflict, point of view; narrator/chorus; stage directions in brackets are acted, not spoken); the excerpt is from Susan Glaspell's play, printed as "The Trifles" — county officials search a farmhouse kitchen while the women wait by the stove (plot summary only; footnote: the play, 1916, is in the public domain).
- **L38 Drama** (p. 89): writing a drama — converting action into a dialogue-based script with directions for actors, from an outline.
- **L39 Question Tags – Present Tense** (p. 91): question tags turn statements into questions; positive statement takes a negative tag and vice versa; five ways to form present-tense tags across the four present forms. (The Contents page misprints the next lesson as "Lesson 30"; it is Lesson 40.)
- **L40 Question Tags – Past Tense** (p. 94): tags across the four past forms; "didn't" where there is no auxiliary, "wasn't/weren't" with the verb "to be".
- **L41 Conditional Tenses Review** (p. 96): all 5 conditional types in one table — use, if-clause form and main-clause form for zero, first, second, third and mixed.
- **L42 Reading Skills Development: Summarising** (p. 99): summarising do's and don'ts (own words, main idea, important information only; never copy or merely re-order) and three summarisation strategies, starting with a 1- to 2-word summary.
- **L43 Consonant Clusters** (p. 102): clusters of two or more consonant sounds before, after or between vowels; warning that some letter pairs that look like clusters are not (the "bl" inside "capable" or "suitable").
- **L44 Linking Verbs** (p. 104): action verbs vs linking (state-of-being) verbs; some verbs can be either, decided by use.
- **L45–L46 Finite and Non-finite Verbs** (pp. 107, 110): finite verbs carry tense and a subject; non-finite verbs do not. Second lesson details the three non-finite types — infinitive, gerund (verb as noun in -ing) and participles.
- **L47 Question Tags – Future Tense** (p. 113): future tags — "won't" wherever the statement uses "will", pronoun at the end; the "going to" pattern.
- **L48 Listening Comprehension – Question Tags** (p. 115): a dialogue between a man driving to Bo and a petrol-station employee giving directions; tags that confirm information vs rhetorical tags that need no answer.

## Grammar and vocabulary spine

- Grammar: the full 12-tense system reviewed (simple, continuous, perfect, perfect continuous × present, past, future); active and passive voice taught properly for the first time; conditionals completed (second, third and mixed, then a five-type review table); noun/pronoun case (subjective, objective, possessive); question tags for present, past and future; linking verbs; finite vs non-finite verbs (infinitive, gerund, participle).
- Vocabulary fields: government and administration; law and order. Figurative/expressive vocabulary: simile, metaphor, personification revisited; idioms, phrasal verbs and collocations combined.
- Oral strand: word stress, emphatic stress (content vs structure words), consonant sounds and clusters, listening with question tags.
- Writing strand: five-paragraph essay restated; descriptive essay; report; drama script; creative-writing elements.
- Exam strand: the WASSCE Guidelines lesson (L2) plus review framing throughout — this term is the pivot from learning the language system to rehearsing it for the examination.

## Honest negatives

- The Contents page misprints Lesson 40 (Question Tags – Past Tense) as "Lesson 30" (printed p. iv); the lesson itself is correctly numbered in the body.
- Two pages after the Answer Key (PDF pp. 152–153) carry only the numerals "143" and "1" in the text layer and no images; their printed content, if any, could not be determined from the file.
- Even in the exam year there is no full mock examination and no set of past WASSCE papers in this book; L2 describes the papers and imitates their question types, and other passages carry footnotes crediting WAEC examinations (the series' passages across the six books are footnoted as adapted from WAEC English Language Examinations of 2002, 2008, 2009, 2011, 2012, 2013 and 2014).
- The drama studied is American (Glaspell, 1916) and the descriptive essay is about American baseball; no Sierra Leonean playwright or essayist is named. Sierra Leonean content is the law-and-order news text, the Freetown trip report and the Bo dialogue.
- Modal verbs — a standard WASSCE grammar area — are still not taught in this term; they wait until Term 2.

## Citation check

15 anchors verified against the actual PDF text, 15/15 passed: cover (PDF p. 1); Foreword signature (PDF p. 2); WASSCE Guidelines (PDF p. 11); Government and Administration (PDF p. 19); Active and Passive Voice (PDF p. 23); "Pastime" (America's Pastime essay) (PDF p. 29); Tenses Review (PDF p. 38); African Nations Championship (PDF p. 51); Sierra Leone Police (PDF p. 60); "My First Trip" (PDF p. 65); Second Conditional (PDF p. 78); Case in Nouns (PDF p. 89); "Trifles" (PDF p. 95); Linking Verbs (PDF p. 113); Answer Key (PDF p. 127). Printed folios matched the PDF-page − 9 offset on every content-page anchor.

# Sierra Leone — English Language SSS3 Pupil Handbook, Term 2 — FULLY READ + CITATION CHECK PASSED 2026-11-03

## The book itself

- Title as printed: *Pupils' Handbook for Senior Secondary English Language — SSS III, Term II*, Free Quality School Education series, "STRICTLY NOT FOR SALE".
- Issuing authority as printed: Ministry of Basic and Senior Secondary Education, Sierra Leone; Foreword signed by Mr. Alpha Osman Timbo; back cover "GOVERNMENT OF SIERRA LEONE — FUNDED BY — IN PARTNERSHIP WITH", funder/partner as logos only.
- Year: none printed. PDF metadata (not print): author Alison Morris Oswald, Word 2013, created 1 August 2018, modified January 2019.
- PDF pages: 154. Printed pagination: same front matter as the other five books, lessons on printed pp. 1–116, Answer Key on printed pp. 117–142, then one page carrying only "143" and one carrying only "1" in the text layer, then the back cover.
- Offset: printed page = PDF page − 9. Lessons 49–96, practice codes PHL3-L049 to PHL3-L096, Class: SSS 3.

## Map of the term — lessons in order

Lessons 49–96 (printed pp. 1–116).

- **L49 WASSCE Guidelines and Tips** (p. 1): the exam lesson in its fullest form. Paper 1: 1 hour, 80 multiple-choice questions, worth 40 marks (half a mark each), covering vocabulary, idioms, figurative language, grammar and structure, with question-type practice (nearest in meaning, interpretation, gap-fill). Paper 2 Section A (essay): 1 of 5 prompts, 450 words, 9 formats (letter, speech, narrative, descriptive, expository, debate, report, article, creative writing), with tips — read the prompt, outline in the right format, re-read the prompt, then edit for grammar, punctuation, spelling, sentence variety and transitional phrases. Paper 2 Section B (comprehension): 30 minutes, graded on paraphrase, inference, grammatical structures and literary terms; tip — read the questions first, then the text several times. Paper 2 Section C: summary (from the Term 1 lesson: 40 minutes). Paper 3 (oral): 45 minutes, 60 multiple-choice listening questions on sounds, rhyme, stress and intonation, with the emphatic-stress tip that the stressed word is the odd one out among near-identical options. The lesson also names common examination mistakes. Practice includes a model essay task (a letter to the principal apologising for damage to school property, following a suspension).
- **L50 Rhyme** (p. 6): rhyme judged by sound, not spelling (boat/gloat/float rhyme; boot/foot do not); practice poem is Wilfred Owen's "Anthem for Doomed Youth" (opening lines about soldiers dying "as cattle" and the anger of the guns — plot/theme summary only).
- **L51 Intonation – Rising Pattern and Falling Pattern** (p. 8): intonation as stress plus pitch; falling intonation lands on the final stressed syllable; uses of each pattern.
- **L52 Comprehending a Listening Passage – Dialogue** (p. 11): listening to dialogue — track the speakers and their intonation; the dialogue "Searching for a Good Restaurant" has Dauda, travelling in a foreign country, asking a local citizen where to eat (summary only).
- **L53–L54 Vocabulary Development: Human Internal Body System and Function** (pp. 13, 16): reading then writing — nervous system, respiratory, blood circulation, muscular system, bones and more, with dictionary use advised for the scientific words. A WASSCE-tested field.
- **L55 Modal Verbs (will, would, may, might, can, could)** (p. 19): modal verbs taught for the first time in the six-book SSS2–SSS3 run. Modals introduced as a type of auxiliary verb, identified by the test that they take no third-person -s ("He wills go" is impossible); this set covers possibility and related meanings.
- **L56 Modal Verbs (shall, should, ought, must)** (p. 23): modals of need and obligation; "ought" takes "to"; negatives formed by adding "not" after the modal; modal tense does not change with the subject.
- **L57 Comprehending a Listening Passage – Idioms** (p. 26): idioms as figurative expressions (worked examples: "under the weather" — sick; "to get bent out of shape" — to get upset); the listening text is built from multiple idioms to be decoded by context.
- **L58 Triphthongs** (p. 28): the third vowel-sound class completes the phonology set — monophthongs (pure vowels, long and short), diphthongs and triphthongs contrasted; triphthongs identified and pronounced in words and sentences.
- **L59–L60 Vocabulary Development: Advertising** (pp. 31, 34): advertising as marketing; customer, promotion, incentive, marketing, digital media, advertisement, campaign and more; reading, then completing a text.
- **L61 Adjectival Phrases** (p. 37): phrases revised (no subject–predicate, not a complete thought); adjectival phrases modify nouns and can sit inside the noun phrase, with the adjective at the beginning, middle or end of the phrase.
- **L62 Adverbial Phrases** (p. 39): phrases that work as adverbs — they modify verbs (and adjectives/adverbs) and answer why, how, when and where.
- **L63 Comprehending a Listening Passage – Debate** (p. 41): debate features (argument, evidence, convincing the audience, emotive language, rhetorical questions, first person, answering the opposing view, directly addressing the audience) applied to a listening text.
- **L64 Debate** (p. 43): debate speaking — building a case and, in detail, rebuttal: summarising the other side's arguments and showing why they are false; the order of a debate speech (address the audience, argue, rebut).
- **L65 Debate** (p. 45): writing a debate speech from an outline (position first), with the full feature list restated.
- **L66 Alliteration and Assonance** (p. 47): alliteration (repeated opening sounds; worked examples include "She sells seashells by the seashore") and assonance (repeated vowel sounds); identifying, exemplifying and pronouncing both — new devices, first taught here.
- **L67–L68 Narrative Essay** (pp. 49, 51): narrative essay features (first person, chronological order, plot/setting/characters, figurative language, theme or moral); outlining "I never should have …", then devices that raise interest — L68 names foreshadowing among them.
- **L69 Coordinating Conjunctions** (p. 53): three conjunction families named (coordinating, subordinating, correlative); coordinating conjunctions link equal elements. The lesson text states "There are 6 coordinating conjunctions" and then lists seven — for, and, nor, but, or, yet, so (an error in the book; the list itself is the standard seven).
- **L70 Subordinating Conjunctions** (p. 55): a long list of common subordinating conjunctions (after, although, because, before, even if, if, in order to, since, so that, than, unless, until, when, whereas, wherever, whether, while and others) and their work marking time, place and cause-and-effect transitions.
- **L71 Correlative Conjunctions** (p. 59): paired conjunctions (both…and, either…or, neither…nor, not only…but also, no sooner…than, hardly/scarcely…when, whether…or, as…as, so…that and others) and the parallel-structure rule.
- **L72 Grammar Skills Development: Identifying Grammatical Name and Function** (p. 61): how grammar is tested at WASSCE — giving the grammatical name and the function of phrases and clauses in a text; phrase vs clause revised; the lesson works on noun phrases, adjectival clauses and adverbial clauses.
- **L73–L74 Persuasive Essay** (pp. 65, 67): persuasive/argumentative essay features (convincing language, facts and statistics, expert quotes, first person, answering the opposing view, recommendations); preparation steps (choose a position, think about the audience) and full drafting across both lessons.
- **L75–L76 Figurative Language – Irony** (pp. 69, 71): irony revisited at SSS3 level — situational (a pilot with a fear of heights), verbal (praising "wonderful weather" in a storm) and dramatic irony; reading, then writing with irony by showing the contrast between expectation and reality.
- **L77 Reading Comprehension – Academic Text (science)** (p. 72): academic reading strategy (information-rich texts, slow careful reading, context clues, dictionary, note new scientific words); the text is about the human microbiome — over 1,000 microbial species in the intestine alone, gut microbes protecting against disease microbes and helping the immune system, and how diet, antibiotics and stress change it (summary only).
- **L78 Reading Comprehension – Academic Text (history)** (p. 74): summarising techniques for historical documents (repeated key words, main idea, relevant vs irrelevant information, topic sentences); the text covers the transatlantic slave trade — some 11 to 12 million Africans forcibly carried to the Americas, nearly five percent to what became the United States, and the "Atlantic Creoles" (summary only).
- **L79 Apostrophe – Contractions** (p. 76): contractions shorten two words and the apostrophe replaces the missing letters; negative contractions (can't, isn't), future contractions with "will" (they'll) and ambiguous contractions.
- **L80 Apostrophes with Contractions and Plurals** (p. 78): the two apostrophe uses — contraction and possession; add 's to singular nouns, apostrophe only to plural nouns.
- **L81 Reading Skills Development: Identifying Topic Sentences** (p. 81): topic sentences, usually first in the paragraph, signal content through their nouns and verbs; supporting sentences develop them. Practice text is a descriptive essay, "Mr Ngobeh, My Mathematics Teacher" (a new teacher who does not at first look likeable — summary only).
- **L82 Reading Skills Development: Identifying Main Idea** (p. 83): stated vs implied main ideas, checked against topic sentences and subheadings; the text is an informative report about a type of star based on research by NASA scientists.
- **L83–L84 Figurative Language – Hyperbole** (pp. 86, 88): hyperbole as extreme, non-literal exaggeration ("a mountain of homework"; "I have told you a million times"); reading, then writing — with a recap of all five conditional types worked into the writing lesson.
- **L85–L86 Present Perfect Continuous Tense** (pp. 89, 91): the form "have/has been + -ing" for actions begun in the past and still going; second lesson covers questions and negatives without double negatives.
- **L87–L88 Past Perfect Continuous Tense** (pp. 93, 95): "had been + -ing" for actions that continued up to a past point — duration and reasons for past results; second lesson covers questions and negatives.
- **L89 Reading Skills Development: Factual and Inferential Questions** (p. 97): the distinction again — factual questions have one literal answer stated in the text (who/where/when/what); inferential questions need analysis and context clues (often why, or yes/no with explanation).
- **L90 Reading Comprehension – Article for Publication (news article)** (p. 100): article features (title, formal language, facts and evidence, community relevance); the article is "Fire in Central London Causes Traffic Chaos" — a fire on Aldwych in central London disrupting traffic (summary only).
- **L91 Article for Publication in a School Magazine** (p. 102): writing an article — the what/when/where/who of an event, audience awareness, formal register (less formal for a school audience).
- **L92 Article for Publication in a Newspaper** (p. 104): the same article craft aimed at a newspaper audience.
- **L93 Reading Skills Development: Giving Titles to Untitled Texts** (p. 106): a good title captures interest, signals content and promises gain (worked examples: "The Mysterious Letter", "The Story of Me"); pupils title untitled texts.
- **L94 Reading Comprehension – Poetry** (p. 109): poetry features (mood, sensory description, symbolism — sky for freedom, water for renewal, light for hope; stanzas and rhythm; rhyme, assonance and alliteration). Two practice poems: one on fear and "night monsters" consuming the speaker, and one on growing up — a child picking petals ("he loves me, he loves me not") who becomes a shy teenager wishing to escape "middle school drama" in a silk cocoon and emerge a butterfly (summaries only; no author is named for either poem in the lesson).
- **L95 Reading Skills Development: Identifying Main Idea** (p. 112): main-idea work done on a formal letter — in formal letters the heading and opening paragraph carry the main idea; formal-letter features are listed in full (both addresses, date, greeting, heading/subject, body, conclusion, subscript "Yours faithfully", signature, name, position/title).
- **L96 Formal Letter** (p. 115): writing a formal letter from a full outline (writer's address, date, recipient's address, "Dear Sir/Madam", subject in UPPERCASE or underlined Title Case, introduction onwards); purposes include complaints, requests, legal and business matters and communicating with authority.

## Grammar and vocabulary spine

- Grammar: modal verbs at last (possibility set, then obligation set); conjunctions completed as a three-family system (coordinating, subordinating, correlative with parallel structure); adjectival and adverbial phrases; apostrophes (contractions and possession); present and past perfect continuous tenses; grammatical name and function as an explicit WASSCE skill.
- Figurative language: irony (second pass), hyperbole, alliteration and assonance — the last three devices of the series' figurative set.
- Vocabulary fields: human internal body system and function; advertising. (With government/administration and law/order from Term 1, the SSS3 field set is civic, legal, bodily and commercial.)
- Oral strand: rhyme, intonation, triphthongs (completing pure vowels → diphthongs → triphthongs), debate listening and speaking.
- Writing strand: narrative essay, persuasive essay, debate speech (spoken and written), articles for school magazine and newspaper, formal letter.
- Reading strand: Owen's war poem, two unattributed poems, microbiome science, slave-trade history, a London news article, a descriptive essay about a mathematics teacher.

## Honest negatives

- The coordinating-conjunctions lesson (L69) contradicts itself in print: it claims there are 6 coordinating conjunctions and lists 7. The filing records the list, not the claim.
- Modal verbs arrive only in Term 2 of the final year — a pupil following this series meets no modal-verb teaching at all in SSS1–SSS2 or SSS3 Term 1 (within the six books read here, "modal verb" occurs zero times before this book).
- The future perfect continuous is still outstanding at the end of this book; it is the last tense form and opens Term 3. There is no mock examination in this book either — L49 is guidelines, tips and imitations, not a timed full paper.
- The two poems in L94 carry no author's name in the lesson, and no Sierra Leonean poet appears in this book; the named poets remain Owen (and, in earlier books, Wordsworth and Dickinson).

## Citation check

16 anchors verified against the actual PDF text, 16/16 passed: cover (PDF p. 1); Foreword signature (PDF p. 2); "worth 40 marks" (PDF p. 10); "Anthem for Doomed Youth" (PDF p. 15); Modal Verbs (PDF p. 28); Triphthongs (PDF p. 37); Narrative Essay (PDF p. 58); "There are 6 coordinating conjunctions" (PDF p. 62); Grammatical Name and Function (PDF p. 70); "microbiome" (PDF p. 82); "Atlantic Creoles" (PDF p. 84); Contractions (PDF p. 85); "Use present perfect continuous tense to show" (PDF p. 98); "Fire in Central London" (PDF p. 109); Formal Letter (PDF p. 124); Answer Key (PDF p. 126). Printed folios matched the PDF-page − 9 offset on every content-page anchor.

# Sierra Leone — English Language SSS3 Pupil Handbook, Term 3 — FULLY READ + CITATION CHECK PASSED 2026-11-03

## The book itself

- Title as printed: *Pupils' Handbook for Senior Secondary English Language — SSS III, Term III*, Free Quality School Education series, "STRICTLY NOT FOR SALE". This is the last book of the senior-secondary English run: the final term before the WASSCE.
- Issuing authority as printed: Ministry of Basic and Senior Secondary Education, Sierra Leone; Foreword signed by Mr. Alpha Osman Timbo; back cover "GOVERNMENT OF SIERRA LEONE — FUNDED BY — IN PARTNERSHIP WITH", funder/partner as logos only.
- Year: none printed. PDF metadata (not print): author Alison Morris Oswald, Word 2013, created 1 August 2018, modified January 2019.
- PDF pages: 154. Printed pagination: same front matter as the other five books, lessons on printed pp. 1–118, Answer Key on printed pp. 119–142, then two pages carrying only the numerals "1" and "2" in the text layer, then the back cover.
- Offset: printed page = PDF page − 9. Lessons 97–140 (44 lessons), practice codes PHL3-L097 to PHL3-L140. Structure of the term: Lessons 97–120 teach the remaining new material (the last tense form, two new vocabulary fields, euphemism and paradox, concord again, expository essay again, reasoning, speech reading); Lessons 121–140 are an explicit whole-course review block — pure vowels, vocabulary by field, creative writing, rhyme and intonation, stress, consonants, essay types, figurative language (twice), listening, articles, letters, parts of speech, tenses (twice) and punctuation.

## Map of the term — lessons in order

Lessons 97–140 (printed pp. 1–118).

- **L97–L98 Vocabulary Development: Motor Vehicles and Travelling** (pp. 1, 3): lorries, engine, ignition switch, in gear, accelerator, clutch, radiator and more; reading, then writing with the field's vocabulary. A WASSCE-tested field.
- **L99–L100 Future Perfect Continuous Tense** (pp. 5, 7): the twelfth and final tense form — projecting to a future point and looking back ("will have been + -ing"), usually with a time expression, also used to explain a future result; second lesson covers questions ("By 2040, how long will he have been living in London?") and the negative "will not have been".
- **L101 Vocabulary Development: Euphemism** (p. 9): euphemism as indirect saying — to seem kinder ("plain" for ugly), to confuse ("enhanced interrogation techniques" for torture), to avoid embarrassment ("between jobs" for unemployed) and to avoid taboo subjects.
- **L102 Elements of a Good Paragraph** (p. 11): the paragraph standard restated — typically 3 to 5 sentences, topic sentence, supporting sentences using facts, statistics, details, examples, stories, quotes or paraphrase, logical order, no irrelevant material.
- **L103 Reading Skills Development: Understanding the Writer (mood, tone, purpose)** (p. 14): tone (objective vs subjective), mood and purpose identified in a text; the practice text, "Lost in the Snow", describes riders crossing deep snow towards Carson City, trusting their guide Ollendorff's instinct over any compass (summary only).
- **L104 Vocabulary Development: Paradox** (p. 17): paradox as an apparently contradictory statement with an element of truth, used to point to a deeper truth, challenge traditional ideas or make the reader think afresh; a literary example is quoted from Henry David Thoreau (short fragment: the swiftest traveller goes afoot).
- **L105 Concord – Subject-Verb Agreement** (p. 20): concord again for the exam term — singular/plural rules, and the rule that a subject standing before an "of" phrase decides the verb.
- **L106 Concord – Subject-Verb Agreement (noun–numbers and pronoun–antecedent)** (p. 23): antecedents defined; singular antecedent with singular pronoun, and the parallel rules for pronoun agreement.
- **L107–L108 Expository Essay** (pp. 26, 28): final pass at the expository essay (how-to/process, compare and contrast and other types; impersonal, unbiased, facts and expert quotes); the L108 practice outline builds an essay on an important period in West African history.
- **L109–L110 Reading Skills Development: Reasoning** (pp. 30, 33): deductive reasoning (premise, evidence, conclusion — worked example: all humans are mortal; John is human; so John is mortal — plus common deductive errors) and inductive reasoning; second lesson adds the three reading levels — reading on the lines (literal), between the lines (inferential) and beyond the lines.
- **L111 Comprehending a Listening Passage – Debate** (p. 36): debate features and the motion (for/against) applied to a listening text. Production error in the book: this lesson's header reads "Class: SSS 2" although its code is PHL3-L111 and every surrounding lesson is SSS 3.
- **L112 Debate** (p. 39): debate speaking — fluency, tone and intonation, evidence, emotive language, rhetorical questions, answering the opposing view.
- **L113–L114 Vocabulary Development: Stock Exchange** (pp. 42, 45): the stock exchange as an organised, regulated market priced by supply and demand; markets, assets, liquid assets, stock, dividend, shareholder, bond, bond-issuer, crash, creditor, fluctuate, inflation and more; reading, then completing a text.
- **L115–L116 Reading Skills Development: Substitution of Words in a Passage** (pp. 47, 51): the WASSCE substitution task in its exam form — choose the option nearest in meaning (synonym) or most nearly opposite (antonym) without changing the sentence's meaning. The L116 practice text is an excerpt from "On a Mountain Trail" by Harry Perry Robinson (travellers ambushed at a turn in the trail — summary only).
- **L117 Vocabulary Development: Connotation and Denotation** (p. 54): denotation as literal meaning, connotation as the idea or feeling a word suggests ("house" = the physical building; "home" = family, comfort and belonging); euphemisms revisited as replacements for words with negative connotations.
- **L118 Vocabulary Development: Rhetorical Questions** (p. 56): questions expecting no answer (worked example: a wife's "Do you have any idea what time it is?" to a husband home after midnight); rhetorical questions inside question tags, and other uses.
- **L119 Speech** (p. 59): reading a speech — features (direct address, first person, refrain, rhetorical questions, references). The speech is Winston Churchill's 1941 address to pupils at his former school, given while the country was at war; the printed footnote names it "…In, Never, Never, Never" by Winston Churchill (1941), in the public domain — the famous "never give in" school speech (content summary only: patience through long, tough stretches, and Kipling's lesson about meeting Triumph and Disaster).
- **L120 Speech** (p. 62): writing a speech from an outline — vocatives, first person, rhetorical questions (worked stance example: running for president to stamp out poverty).
- **L121 Pure Vowel Review** (p. 65): monophthongs again — same sound from beginning to end; spelling does not match sound ("wood", "should", "pudding" share one sound); long vs short pure vowels.
- **L122 General Vocabulary Review (family and home, agriculture, fishing)** (p. 67): cousin, engaged, extended family, generation, in-law, livelihood, nuclear family and the other two fields' word lists; identifying field vocabulary and completing paragraphs.
- **L123 Creative Writing Review** (p. 70): the creative-writing elements again (plot, theme, characters, setting, conflict, point of view) plus tone; write a text.
- **L124 Rhyme and Intonation Review** (p. 72): rhyme by sound not spelling (cough/dough do not rhyme; peer/mere/clear do), rhyme patterns, and rising vs falling intonation — framed explicitly as WASSCE-tested.
- **L125 General Vocabulary Review (animal husbandry, sports, health)** (p. 75): extensive vs intensive livestock farming, herbivore, herdsman and the sports and health lists.
- **L126 Reading Skills Development: WH questions, Inferential and Literal Questions** (p. 78): the six WH questions (who, what, when, where, why, how) used to check, summarise and comprehend; literal vs inferential questions.
- **L127 Sentence Stress, Emphatic Stress and Word Stress Review** (p. 81): all three stress types together — emphatic stress by speaking a word more slowly or loudly; sentence stress resting on content words over structure words.
- **L128 Consonant and Consonant Cluster Review** (p. 85): the 24 consonant sounds with example words in initial, medial and end positions, plus clusters.
- **L129 Types of Essays Review – Persuasive, Expository, Narrative, Descriptive** (p. 88): the four essay types side by side with their features, paragraph standards, and drafting a five-paragraph essay.
- **L130 Figurative Language Review (simile, metaphor, personification, hyperbole)** (p. 90): figurative language defined as words used beyond their literal meaning; the four devices reviewed with context-clue and own-words explanation tasks.
- **L131 Listening Comprehension Review** (p. 93): the review listening text is the European folktale "The Fox and the Horse" by the Brothers Grimm — a farmer turns out his old, faithful horse, promising to keep it only if it brings him a lion (plot summary only).
- **L132 Article Review** (p. 95): newspaper-article features again (attention, audience, relevance, formal register, always a title) and writing a news article.
- **L133 Figurative Language Review (idioms, phrasal verbs, collocations, irony)** (p. 97): the second figurative review — collocations as natural-sounding combinations with no rule behind them (natural vs unnatural pairings), plus idioms, phrasal verbs and irony.
- **L134 Types of Letters Review – Informal and Formal** (p. 101): the two letter types side by side, with the informal-letter outline in full (address, date, greeting, opening, body, closing, "Yours sincerely", name) and formal-letter uses (legal, medical, business and banking matters, accepting or resigning from a job, authority).
- **L135 General Vocabulary Review (environment, science and technology, building and construction)** (p. 104): the three SSS2 fields recycled — biodegradable, carbon emissions, climate change and the rest.
- **L136 Parts of Speech Review** (p. 107): the 8 parts of speech in table form with definitions and examples (noun exemplar: Aminata, town, bicycle).
- **L137 Tenses Review** (p. 109): past and present tenses — all 4 forms within each time (simple, continuous, perfect, perfect continuous); questions and positive/negative forms.
- **L138 Tenses Review** (p. 112): future tenses in the same 4 forms (simple with will/shall or "going to"; future continuous "will be + -ing"; future perfect; future perfect continuous).
- **L139 General Vocabulary Review (traditional religion, ceremonies, culture and institutions)** (p. 115): amulet, animism, creator, deity, divination and the other two fields recycled from SSS2.
- **L140 Punctuation Review** (p. 117): the final lesson of the series — terminal stops (full stop, question mark, exclamation mark) and within-sentence punctuation (comma, semi-colon, colon, dash). The SSS3 year, and the six-book SSS2–SSS3 run, end here; the Answer Key follows.

## Grammar and vocabulary spine

- New grammar in this final term is deliberately thin: the future perfect continuous (completing the 12 tenses), and concord revisited in depth. Everything else in Lessons 121–140 is systematic review.
- New vocabulary and language points: motor vehicles and travelling; stock exchange; euphemism; paradox; connotation and denotation; rhetorical questions; reasoning (deductive/inductive) and the three levels of reading.
- Review architecture (L121–L140): the review lessons deliberately re-cover the whole SSS2–SSS3 field list — family and home, agriculture, fishing, animal husbandry, sports, health (L122, L125), environment, science and technology, building and construction (L135), traditional religion, ceremonies, culture and institutions (L139) — plus every essay type, letter type, figurative device, stress type, sound class and tense.
- Series-final grammar spine, as these six books present it: 12 tenses; 5 conditionals; active/passive voice; direct/indirect speech; question tags; modal verbs; concord; case; phrases and clauses (noun/adjectival/adverbial); participles, gerunds, infinitives, finite/non-finite and linking verbs; determiners/articles; the 8 parts of speech; the full punctuation set.

## Honest negatives — including the series-final picture

- The book contains a production error: Lesson 111's header gives "Class: SSS 2" inside an SSS3 book (verified on PDF p. 45). It also re-uses SSS2-era debate material almost verbatim for L111–L112.
- There is still no mock examination, no timed past paper and no marking scheme anywhere in the six books: the run's exam preparation is the two WASSCE Guidelines lessons (SSS3 Terms 1–2), the front-matter Key Takeaways in every book, WAEC-adapted passages (footnoted 2002, 2008, 2009, 2011, 2012, 2013 and 2014 across the series) and WASSCE-style tasks — never a complete rehearsal paper.
- What the whole Sierra Leone English run (SSS2–SSS3, as far as these six books show it) never teaches, verified by full-text search of all six books: no Sierra Leonean language content at all — Krio and Temne never occur, and "Mende" occurs only as a character's surname; no named Sierra Leonean author in any book (the named writers are Plato/Newsela, Shakespeare, Wordsworth, Dickinson, O. Henry, Glaspell, Owen, Churchill, Thoreau, the Brothers Grimm, Harry Perry Robinson and Kipling by quotation); no African set-text or novel study — literature is excerpts and short pieces only, with no bibliography, referencing or research-paper writing; no oxymoron and no onomatopoeia (the figurative set stops at simile, metaphor, personification, irony, hyperbole, alliteration, assonance, euphemism and paradox); no diary writing and no eulogy; no listening audio — every "listening" lesson is a printed script.
- Coverage is also uneven by design: some WASSCE staples arrive very late — modal verbs only in the second term of the final year, the future perfect continuous only in the final term — so the series gives them one teaching pass plus review, where tenses, letters and essays get three or more passes.
- The two pages after this book's Answer Key (PDF pp. 152–153) carry only the numerals "1" and "2" in the text layer and no images; their printed content, if any, could not be determined from the file. The same trailing-page pattern appears in SSS2 Term 2 and, with folios "143"/"1", in SSS3 Terms 1–2.
- No book in the run prints a publication year, an ISBN or publisher imprint beyond the MBSSE/Free Quality School Education branding; the only dates inside the books are the dates inside letters, essays and example sentences.

## Series verdicts (Sierra Leone, full set)

- **Primary (18 lesson-plan books)** runs one machine for
  six years: 35-minute lessons, five parts each. Primary 1
  teaches the whole alphabet letter by letter with a story
  or poem per letter; Primary 2 turns letters into sounds
  and digraphs and starts naming grammar (plural -s,
  pronouns, prepositions, present/past/continuous,
  countable/uncountable); Primaries 3–4 work word families,
  folktales and comprehension; Primaries 5–6 reach opinion
  writing, formal letters and dictionary work (taught only
  in P6 T1). Across all six primary years: the present
  perfect appears exactly once, and the passive and
  conditionals never appear at all.
- **JSS (9 pupil handbooks)** is a literature-and-usage
  course: 50 lessons a term, set texts carried as abridged
  extracts and plot summaries (Verne, Shakespeare, African
  novels), BECE in view. One gap is filed plainly: JSS2's
  own BECE-tested list names conditionals and the passive,
  but no JSS book teaches them.
- **SSS (9 pupil handbooks)** is WASSCE practice by design:
  coded activities with answer keys, an examiner-report
  "Key Takeaways" frontispiece, and the full exam structure
  taught at SSS3 — but no mock exam or past paper in any
  book, and modal verbs are not taught until SSS3 Term 2.
- **No publication year is printed in any of the 36
  books**; the 2016 (primary) and 2018/2019 (handbook)
  dates exist only as PDF file metadata and are filed as
  such, never as editions.
